Chapter 1
Introduction
​ 1.1​ Overview
One of the main communication mechanisms between a computer and a person is given by sending and receiving textual messages: the user write an order using the keyboard, and the computer executes it, returning as result an informative message about the actions carried out.
Although the evolution of computers is currently directed
towards the use of innovative and ergonomic user interfaces (such as the mouse, the touchscreens, graphic tablets, etc.), we can say that almost all actions that the user performs on these interfaces are translated sooner or later to sequences of commands that are executed as if they had been entered by keyboard. By
On the other hand, and from the point of view of the IT professional, the work that this performed on the computer is plagued with situations in which there is direct textual communication with the machine: use of an interpreter of commands ( shell ), batch job file construction, programming by various languages, etc. Even word processors like WordPerfect and MS Word stores the documents written by the user using an encoding
structured textual that, every time the document is opened, is recognized, traversed and presented on screen.
For all this, no computer scientist worth his salt can avoid the undoubted
need to know the ins and outs of the tool you use during your work daily and on which the human-machine interaction rests: the translator .
There are a lot of situations where it can be very helpful to know
how the different parts of a compiler work, especially the parts that are in charge of chopping up the source texts and converting them into syntactically valid phrases. For example, a situation of apparent complexity may arise if we have an MS Word document that comes from a merge with a database and is wants, from it, to obtain the original DB. How to solve the problem? Well based on the fact that the document structure is made up of blocks that are repeated; the solution could be:
Convert the document to pure text format.
Process this text with a translator to eliminate superfluous characters
and result in another text in which each field in the database table is in quotes.
The above text is imported with any DBMS.
Other applications of the translator construction can be the creation of
preprocessors for languages  that do not have it (for example, to easily work with SQL in C, you can make a preprocessor to introduce embedded SQL), or even converting ASCII 10 (LF) character to HTML "<br>" to pass text to the web.
In this chapter, we introduce the construction of a compiler and describe
its components, the environment in which they work and some software that facilitate its construction.
​ 1.2​ Translator concept.
A translator is defined as a program that translates or converts from
a text or program written in a source language to a text or program equivalent written in a target language producing, if possible, error messages . Translators encompass both compilers (in which the target language usually be machine code) as well as interpreters (in which the target language is made up of the atomic actions that the interpreter can execute). Fig . 1 .one shows the basic schema that makes up a compiler / interpreter.
Figure 1.1. Preliminary sketch of a translator
It is important to highlight the speed with which it can be built today
a compiler. In the 1950s, translators were viewed as programs remarkably difficult to write. The first Fortran compiler ( Formula Translator ), for example, required the equivalent of 18 years of individual work (it really didn't take that long since the work was developed in team). Until the theory of automata and formal languages  was applied to the
creation of translators, their development has been plagued with problems and errors. Without However, today a basic compiler can be the final project of any university student of Computer Science.
1.2.1 Types of translators
Since the origins of computing, there has been a gulf between the form
how people express their needs and the way a computer is capable to interpret instructions. The translators have tried to bridge this chasm to make work easier for humans, which has led to the application of automata theory to different fields and specific areas of computing, giving rise to the different types of translators that we will see next.
1.2.1.1 Language translators
They translate from one given language to another, such as from English to Spanish.
This type of translator has a multitude of problems, namely:
Need for artificial intelligence and problem with set phrases. He
The problem with artificial intelligence is that it has a lot of artificial and little intelligence, so today it is almost impossible to translate phrases with a deep meaning. As an anecdote, during the cold war, in a attempt to perform automatic translations from Russian to English and vice versa, A prototype was tested by introducing the text in English: “The spirit is strong but the flesh is weak ”whose translation into Russian was switched back to English to see if it matched the original. What was the surprise of the developers when what was obtained was: "The wine is good but the meat It is rotten ”(in English spirit means both spirit and alcohol). Others examples that are difficult to translate are made up of phrases such as: “Piel de chicken "," in case the flies "," molar mallet ", etc.
Difficult to formalize in the specification of the meaning of words.
Change in the meaning of words according to the context. Ex: "to say that, he took a cookie ”.
In general, the most satisfactory results in language translationnatural have occurred on restricted subsets of language. And even more, on subsets in which there is very little room for ambiguity in the interpretation of texts: legal speeches, technical documentation, etc.
1.2.1.2 Compilers
It is the translator whose input is a sentence in formal language
and as output it has an executable file, that is, it performs a translation of a code high-level machine code (compiler is also understood to be that program which provides an object file instead of the final executable).
1.2.1.3 Interpreters
It's like a compiler, only the output is an execution. The program of
input is recognized and executed at the same time. No physical result (code machine) but logical (one execution). There are languages  that can only be interpreted, such as SNOBOL ( StriNg Oriented SimBOlyc Language ), LISP ( LISt Processing ), some versions of BASIC ( Beginner's All-purpose Symbolic Instruction Code ), etc.
Their main advantage is that they allow easy debugging. Between the
drawbacks we can cite, in the first place, the slowness of execution, since when run at the same time as translated cannot be applied a high degree of optimization; by
For example, if the program goes into a loop and the optimization is not very fine tuned, the The same instructions will be interpreted and executed over and over again, slowing down the program execution. Another drawback is that during execution, the interpreter it must reside in memory, thus consuming more resources.
In addition to the fact that the translation optimizes the program by bringing it closer to the machine,
interpreted languages  have the characteristic that they allow building programs they can modify themselves.
Some languages  try to combine the advantages of compilers and
interpreters and avoid their disadvantages; they are pseudo-interpreted languages. In these, the source program goes through a pseudocompiler that generates a pseudo-executable. To execute this pseudo-executable it is made to pass through an execution engine that interprets relatively efficiently. This has the advantage of portability, since the pseudo-executable is independent of the machine on which it will be executed, and it is enough that said machine has the appropriate execution engine to be able to interpret any pseudo-executable. The best known current example is constitutes the Java language; are also pseudointerpreted some versions of
Pascal and COBOL ( COmmon Business Oriented Language ). The figure1 . 2 sample the steps to follow in these languages  to obtain an execution.
Figure 1.2. Translation / execution scheme of an interpreted program
1.2.1.4 Preprocessors
They allow modifying the source program before the actual compilation.
They make use of macros and compilation directives. For example, in language C, the preprocessor replaces the #include Uno.c directive with the complete code that contains the file "Uno.c", so that when the compiler starts its
execution finds the code already inserted into the source program (figure 1 .3 illustrates this situation). Some other preprocessing directives allow compiling optional code chunks (C and Clipper languages): #fi, #ifdef, #define, #ifndef, etc. Preprocessors often act transparently to the programmer, they can even be considered to be a preliminary phase of the compiler.
1.2.1.5 Command interpreters
A command interpreter translates simple statements into invocations to
programs from a library. They are used especially in operating systems (the Unix shell is a command interpreter). Invoked programs can reside in the kernel (kernel) of the system or be stored in some external device as executable routines that are brought into memory on demand.
For example, if under MS-DOS the copy command is typed, the
function of copying files of the operating system, which is resident in memory.
Figure 1.3. How the #include preprocessing directive works in C language
1.2.1.6 Assemblers and Macro Assemblers
They are the pioneers of compilers, since at the dawn of computing,
programs were written directly in machine code, and the first step toward High-level languages  are assemblers. In assembly language, establishes a one-to-one relationship between each instruction and a mnemonic word, so that the user writes the programs using mnemonics, and the assembler takes care of translating it into pure machine code. In this way, assemblers usually produce executable code directly rather than producing object files.
An assembler is a simple compiler, in which the source language has
a structure so simple that it allows the translation of each source sentence to a single instruction in machine code. To the language that this compiler supports as well it is called assembly language. Ultimately, there is a one-to-one correspondence between the assembly instructions and the machine instructions. Ex:
​ Assembler instruction:​ LD HL, # 0100
​ Generated machine code:​ 65h.00h.01h
On the other hand, there are advanced assemblers that allow defining
macro instructions that can be translated into various machine instructions. To these programs are called macroassemblers, and they represent the next step towards high-level languages. From a formal point of view, a macro-assembler can be understood as an assembler with a previous preprocessor.
1.2.1.7 Source-source converters
They allow translating from a high-level language to another high-level language,
thus achieving greater portability in high-level programs.
For example, if a computer has only one Pascal compiler, and
we want to execute a program written for another machine in COBOL, since a
COBOL to Pascal converter will fix the problem. Notwithstanding the program
Result source may require manual touch-ups for several reasons:
In situations where the target language lacks important characteristicsthat the source language does have. For example a Java to C converter, it would need modifications as C has no garbage collector.
In situations where the translation is not intelligent and the target programsthey are highly inefficient.
1.2.1.8 Cross Compiler
It is a compiler that generates code to be executed on another machine. I know
used in the development phase of new computers. In this way it is possible, e.g., building the operating system of a new computer using a language high level, and even before such a new computer even has a compiler.
Note also that, to facilitate software development of a new
computer, one of the first programs to be developed for it is, precisely, a compiler of some high-level language.
1.2.2 Basic concepts related to translation
We will now study various terminology related to the
compilation and compiler build process.
1.2.2.1 Compilation, link and upload.
These are the three basic phases that must be followed so that a computer
perform the interpretation of a written text by using a language of
high level. Although this book will focus exclusively on the first phase, we will see at this point some questions regarding the entire process.
As a general rule, the compiler does not directly produce a file
executable, but the generated code is structured in modules that are stored in an object file. The object files have information related to both the code machine as a symbol table that stores the structure of the variables and types used by the source program. Fig . 1 . 4 shows the actual result that produces a compiler.
Figure 1.4. Input and output of a real compiler
But why isn't an executable file generated directly? Simply, to allow separate compilation, so that various programmers can simultaneously develop the parts of a program more large and, more importantly, can compile them independently and perform a parallel debugging. Once each of these parts has generated its corresponding object file, these must be merged to generate a single executable.
As mentioned, an object file has a module structure
also called records. These registers have different lengths depending of its type and role. Certain types of these registers store machine code, others have information about global variables, and others include information about external objects (eg variables that are supposed to be declared in other files. The C language explicitly allows this situation through the modifier "extern").
During the linking phase, the linker resolves the referrals (this is what the use of external objects is called), which may be
declared in other object files, or in libraries (files with the extension "lib" or "dll"), includes in a single block the different registers that store machine code, structures the memory block destined to store the variables at
execution and generates the final executable incorporating some additional routines from libraries, such as those that implement mathematical functions or basic i / o. The figureone. 5 illustrates this operating mechanism.
Figure 1.5. How a linker works
In this way, the block of machine code contained in the file
executable is a relocatable code, that is, a code that in turn can be
run in different memory locations, depending on the location of the memory in the time of execution. According to the model for structuring the memory of the microprocessor, this code is structured in different ways. The most usual is that the executable file is divided into segments: code, data, data stack, etc.
When the linker builds the executable file, it assumes that each
segment is to be placed at memory address 0. How the program is going to be divided into segments, the addresses to which the instructions refer within each segment (flow control change instructions, access to data, etc.), are not treated as absolute, but are relative addresses from the base address in which each segment is placed at the time of execution. The loader loads the .exe file, places its different segments in memory (where the operating system tells you that there is free memory for it) and allocates the base registers to their correct positions, so that the relative directions work correctly. The figure1 . 6 illustrates the work that a program loader does.
Figure 1.6. Work done by the loader. The charger is usually part of the system operative
Every time a machine instruction refers to an address of
memory (starting from address 0), the microprocessor automatically takes over adding to that address the absolute starting address of its segment. For example to access the variable x stored at address 1Fh, found in the data segment located at address 8A34h, the machine instruction will do
reference to 1Fh, but the microprocessor will translate it by 8A34h + 1Fh giving rise to an access to address 8A53h: absolute dir of the segment in memory + relative dir of x in the segment = absolute dir of x in memory.
1.2.2.2 Compilation passes
It is the number of times a compiler must read the source program to
generate the code. There are some situations where, to compile, you don't it is enough to read the source file only once. For example, in situations where that there is indirect recursion (a function A calls another B and B calls A). When the body of A is read, it is not known whether B is later declared or has been forgotten to programmer; or if it is, if the actual parameters match in number and
type with the formal ones or not. Moreover, although everything was correct, it has not yet been generated the code for B, then it is not possible to generate the machine code
corresponding to the invocation of B since its starting address is not known in the code segment. For all this, in a subsequent pass it is necessary to control the errors and fill in the missing data.
Different compilers and languages  solve this problem in ways
different. One solution is to do two or more compilation passes, but doing so time consuming as I / O operations are currently one of the fundamental points in the inefficiency of the programs (including compilers). Another solution is to do a single compilation pass and modify the language by forcing recursive function declarations indirect before definition. In this way, languages  like Pascal or Modula-2 use the FORWARD reserved word for this: FORWARD precedes the declaration from B, then A is defined and B is defined last. Languages  like C also allow you not to have to declare a function if it lacks parameters and returns a whole.
Other languages  implicitly FORWARD, and if you have not already
found the one referred to, they continue, waiting for the linker resolve the problem, or issue the error message.
Currently, when a language needs to make multiple passes of
compilation, usually places an abstract representation of the source file in memory, so that the compilation passes are performed on said representation in instead of over the input file, which solves the problem of inefficiency
due to i / o operations.
1.2.2.3 Incremental compilation
When a source program is developed, it is recompiled several times until
get a definitive bug-free version. Well, in a compilation incremental only the modifications made since the last compilation. Ideally, only those parts containing the
errors or that, in general, have been modified, and that the generated code is reinserted carefully in the object file generated in the last compilation. However, this
it is very difficult to obtain and does not usually save compilation time more than in cases very specific.
Incremental compilation can be done with varying degrees of
tuning. For example, if you forget a ';' in a statement, a file could be generated partial transitory object. If the error is corrected and the ';' missing and recompiled, an incremental compiler can work at multiple levels
At the character level: the ';' is recompiled. and the sentence missing.
At the sentence level: if the ';' line 100 was missing only compiled line 100 and the object file is updated.
At the block level: if the ';' was missing in a procedure or block only compile that block and update the object file.
At source file level: if the complete application consists of 15 filessource, and only 1 is modified (which was missing the ';'), only the one is compiled when that the ';' has been added, its object file is completely generated and then they are all linked together to get the executable.
Ideally, it should be done efficiently at the sentencing level, but normally
is to find it at the file level. Most current compilers perform a incremental compilation at this level.
When a compiler does not support incremental compilation, they usually supply
an external tool (like RMAKE in the case of Clipper, MAKE in some versions of C) in which the programmer indicates the dependencies between files, so if one is recompiled, all those that depend on it are recompiled. These Tools are usually designed for a more general purpose and also allow conditional links.
1.2.2.4 Autocompiler
It is a compiler written in the same language that compiles (or similar).
Typically, when spread across many different machines the use of one compiler, and it is desired to improve, the new compiler is written using the language of the old, so that it can be compiled by all those machines different, and result in a more powerful compiler of that same language. 1.2.2.5 Metacompiler
This is one of the most important concepts that we are going to work with.
A metacompiler is a compiler of compilers. It is a program that accepts as input the description of a language and produces the compiler of said language language. Today there are no complete metacompilers, but there are partial ones in the that a grammar of a language is accepted as input and an automaton is generated that recognizes any sentence of the language. We can add code to this automaton to complete the rest of the compiler. Examples of metacompilers are: Lex, YACC, FLex, Bison, JavaCC, JLex, Cup, PCCTS, MEDISE, etc.
Metacompilers are usually divided into those that can work with
context-free grammars and those that work with regular grammars. The first they are dedicated to recognizing the syntax of the language and the seconds chop the files source and divide it into words.
PCLex is a metacompiler that generates the part of the compiler intended for
recognize reserved words. PCYACC is another metacompiler that generates the part of the compiler that reports whether a language statement is valid or not. JavaCC is a metacompiler that combines the recognition of reserved words and the acceptance or rejection of sentences of a language. PCLex and PCYACC generate code
C and admit descriptions of a language using formal grammars, while
JavaCC produces Java code and supports language descriptions expressed in
BNF ( Backus-Naur Form ) notation . The differences between them will be studied in
later topics.
1.2.2.6 Decompiler
A decompiler performs a reverse translation work, that is, it passes from a
machine code (exit program) to the equivalent written in the language that generated it (source program). Each decompiler works with a specific high-level language.
Decompilation is usually an almost impossible task, because the code
generated machine is almost always optimized at a later stage, so that the same machine code could be generated from several
source codes. For this reason, there are only decompilers for those languages  in which there is a bijective relationship between the target code and the source code, as it happens with disassemblers, in which each machine instruction corresponds to a and only one assembler instruction.
Decompilers are used especially when the machine code has
been generated with debugging options, and contains additional help information to bug discovery (breakpoints, trace tracking, display of variables, etc.).
They are also used when the compiler does not generate pure machine code,
but pseudo-code to be executed through a pseudo-interpreter. In these cases
There is usually a bijective relationship between the instructions in the pseudocode and the syntactic constructions of the source language, which allows to reconstruct a program high-level from that of a pseudocode block.
​ 1.3​ Structure of a translator
A translator divides his work into two stages: one that analyzes the input and generates
intermediate structures and another that synthesizes the output from said structures. By
Therefore, the scheme of a translator becomes that of the figure 1 .1 , to be the one in the figure1 .7.
Basically the objectives of the analysis stage are: a) to control the
correction of the source program, and b) generate the necessary structures to start the synthesis stage.
To do this, the analysis stage consists of the following phases:
O Lexicographic analysis. Divide the source program into the basic components of the language to compile. Each basic component is a subsequence of
Figure 1.7. Staged scheme of a translator
characters from the source program, and belongs to a grammatical category:
numbers, user identifiers (variables, constants, types, names of procedures, ...), reserved words, punctuation marks, etc.
O Syntactic analysis. Check that the structure of the basic components is correct according to the grammar rules of the language being compiled.
O Semantic analysis. Check that the source program respects the guidelines of the language that is compiled (everything related to the meaning): check of types, ranges of values, existence of variables, etc.
Any of these three phases can issue error messages derived from
mistakes made by the programmer in the writing of the source texts. While more errors control a compiler, fewer problems will give a program in time of execution. For example, the C language does not control the bounds of an array, which causes program behaviors to occur at runtime difficult to explain.
The synthesis stage builds the desired object program (equivalent
semantically to the source) from the structures generated by the analysis stage.
For this, it consists of three fundamental phases:
O Generation of intermediate code. Generates a code independent of the machine very similar to assembler. Machine code is not generated directly because that way it is easier to make pseudocompilers and also facilitates code optimization regardless of microprocessor.
O Generation of machine code. Create a block of machine code executable, as well as the necessary blocks intended to contain the data.
O Optimization phase. Optimization can be done on code intermediate (regardless of the specific characteristics of the microprocessor), on the machine code, or on both. And it can be a isolated from the previous two, or be integrated with them.
1.3.1 Systematic construction of compilers
Frequently, the earlier phases are grouped into an initial stage ( front-
end ) and a final stage ( back-end ). The initial stage comprises those phases, or parts of phases, which depend exclusively on the source language and are independent of the machine for which the code is to be generated. In the initial stage, the lexical and syntactic analysis, semantic analysis and code generation intermediate. The initial stage can also do some code optimization and It also includes the handling of errors corresponding to each of these phases.
The final stage includes those compiler stages that depend on the
target machine and that, in general, do not depend on the source language but only on the intermediate language. At this stage, there are aspects of the generation phase of
Figure 1.8. Building three C compilers reusing a front-end
code, in addition to its optimization, along with the necessary error handling and the access to intermediate structures as needed.
It has become common practice to take the initial stage of a
compiler and redo its associated final stage to produce a compiler for the same source language on a different machine. It is also tempting to create compilers for several different languages  and generate the same intermediate language to, finally, use a common final stage for all of them, and thus obtain several compilers for one machine. To illustrate this practice and its inverse, Figure 1 .8
Figure 1.9. Creation of three compilers (Pascal, C and COBOL) for the same Intel machine
It shows a situation in which you want to create a C compiler for three different machines: Dec-Alpha (Unix), Motorola (Mac OS) and Intel (MS-DOS). Each block of dotted lines groups a front-end with a back-end giving rise to a full compiler.
Conversely, you could build three Pascal compilers, C and
COBOL for the same machine, eg Intel, as illustrated in the figure 1 . 9.
Finally, the creation of Pascal, C and COBOL compilers for the
Dec-Alpha, Motorola and Intel machines, would go through the combination of the methods above, as illustrated in the figure 1 .10 .
Figure 1.10. Combining each front-end with a back-end results in a compiler
different: three from Pascal, three from C, and three from COBOL. The effort has been reduced considerably.
1.3.2 The symbol table
An essential function of a compiler is to record the identifiers of
user (names of variables, functions, types, etc.) used in the program source and gather information on the various attributes of each identifier. These attributes can provide information about the memory allocated to a identifier, the memory address where it will be stored at runtime, its type, its scope (the part of the program where it is visible), etc.
Well, the symbol table is a data structure that has
information about user-defined identifiers, whether constant, variables, types or others. Since it can contain information of various kinds, it must made so that its structure is not uniform, that is, it is not kept the same information about a program variable than about a user-defined type. It does data dictionary functions and its structure can be a hash table, a binary search tree, etc., as long as the access operations are as
Figure 1.11. Definitive stage diagram of a translator
quite efficient.
Both the analysis and synthesis stages access this structure, therefore
which is closely coupled to the rest of the compiler phases. Therefore, it is convenient to provide the symbol table of an interface generic enough to allow the change of internal storage structures without these phases having to be retouched. This is so because it is usual to make a first prototype of a compiler with an easy to build (and therefore inefficient) symbol table, and when the compiler has already been finished, then it proceeds to replace the table of symbols for another more efficient depending on the needs that have arisen
throughout the previous stage of development. Following this criterion, the general scheme definition of a translator is detailed in the figure 1 .11 . The figure1 .12 illustrates the schematic by phases, where each stage has been replaced by the phases that compose it and has been made explicit mention of the preprocessor ..
​ 1.4​ Build example
We are going to study below the different tasks that each
phase of a compiler until the code associated with a C statement is generated.
The sentence with which it is going to work is the following:
#define PERCENTAGE 8
commission = fixed + value * PERCENTAGE;
In order not to complicate the example too much, we will assume that the variables
referenced have previously been declared of type int , and initialized to the values desired.
We will start with the preprocessing until finally reaching the phase of
generation of machine code in any microprocessor.
Figure 1.12. Complete schematic of a phased compiler with preprocessor.
1.4.1 Preprocessing
As we have already seen, the source code of an application can be divided into
modules stored in different files. The task of assembling the source program to it is often entrusted to a different program, called a preprocessor. The preprocessor you can also expand abbreviations, called macros, to language propositions source. In our example, the constant PERCENTAGE is replaced by its value, giving rise to the text:
commission = fixed + value * 8;
which happens to be the source that will enter the compiler.
1.4.2 Analysis stage
At this stage it is checked that the source text is correct in all respects,
and the structures necessary for code generation are generated.
1.4.2.1 Lexicographic analysis phase
In this phase, the character string that constitutes the source program is read
from left to right and is grouped into lexical components, which are sequences of characters that have an atomic meaning; in addition, the lexical analyzer works with the symbol table by entering the variable names in it.
In our example the characters of the assignment statement commission = fixed + value * 8;
they would be grouped into the following lexical components:
1.- The commission identifier .
2.- The assignment symbol '='.
3.- The fixed identifier.
4.- The plus sign '+'.
5.- The identifier value.
6.- The multiplication sign '*'.
7.- The number 8.
8.- The end of sentence symbol ';'.
The figure one. 13 illustrates how each lexical component translates to its category
grammar, and some information is associated with it, which can be a pointer to the table of symbols where the identifier is described, or even a direct value, as in the case of literal 8. Thus, each component becomes a pair (category, attribute), and the symbol table is updated. This sequence of pairs is passed on to the next phase analysis.
Note how the blank spaces that separate the characters from these Lexical components are normally removed during the lexical analysis, as long as when the definition of the language to compile so advises, as in C. Lo
The same is true for unnecessary tabs and carriage returns. The Comments, whether nested or not, are also removed.
Figure 1.13. Transformation performed by the lexicographic analyzer
1.4.2.2 Syntactic analysis phase
It works with a free context grammar and generates the syntactic tree that
acknowledges your input sentence. In our case, the grammatical categories of Lexical analysis are the terminals of grammar. For the example at hand we can start from the grammar:
S ÷ <ID> <ASIG> expr <TERM> expr ÷ <ID>
| <ID> <+> expr
| <ID> <*> expr
| <NUM>
so parsing attempts to generate a syntax tree that matches the input sentence. For our example, such a syntactic tree exists and is that of figure 1. 14 . The tree can be represented as it appears in this figure, or invested.
Figure 1.14. Syntactic tree of the input statement.
Although only the categories have been represented grammatical, remember that each one carries or can have an attribute associated with it.
​ 1.4.2.2.1​ Syntax-driven compilation
An ideal situation has been represented in which the lexicographic phase acts by
separated and, only after it has finished, does the syntactic supply its result of
departure. Although we will continue in our example with this clear distinction between phases, It is important to note that the parser is in control at all times, and the lexicon by chunks, at the request of the syntactic. In other words, the syntactic goes building your tree little by little (from left to right), and every time you need a new lexical component to continue said construction, is requested to the lexicographic; it reads new characters from the input file to form a
new component and, once obtained, it is supplied to the syntactician, who continues building the tree until you need another component again, at which point the process restarts. This mechanism ends when the tree has been obtained and
there are no more components in the input file, or when it is impossible to build the tree.
This is just the beginning of what is called a driven compilation.
syntax (see figure 1 .15 ): it is the compilation mechanism in which the control is it carries the parser, and all other phases are subject to it.
Figure 1.15. Syntax-driven analysis
1.4.2.3 Semantic analysis phase
This phase reviews the syntactic tree together with the attributes and the
symbols to try to find semantic errors. For all this, the
operators and operands of expressions and propositions. Finally gather the necessary information about the data types for the subsequent phase of generating code.
The most important component of semantic analysis is verification of
types. Here the compiler checks if the operands of each operator are compatible according to the source language specification. If we assume that our language only works with real numbers, the output of this phase would be your same tree, except that the <NUM> attribute, which was the input integer 8, would now become the real 8.0. In addition, it has been necessary to control that the variables involved in the sentence, namely, commission, fixed and value are compatible with the numeric type of the constant 8.0.
1.4.3 Synthesis stage
In the previous stage, it has been checked that the input program is correct.
Therefore, the compiler is now in a position to generate the machine code semantically equivalent to the source program. For this, we start from the structures generated in said previous stage: syntactic tree and symbol table.
1.4.3.1 Intermediate code generation phase
After the analysis stage, an intermediate representation is usually generated
explicit source program. Said intermediate representation can be considered as a program for an abstract machine.
Any intermediate representation must have two important properties;
it should be easy to generate and easy to translate into target machine code. Thus a intermediate representation can take various forms. In the present example, will work with an intermediate form called "three-way code", which is very similar to an assembly language for a microprocessor lacking registers and it is only capable of working with memory addresses and literals. In code three addresses each instruction has a maximum of three operands. Following the lead proposed, the following three-way code would be generated:
t1 = 8.0 t2 = value * t1 t3 = fixed + t2 commission = t3
From this example several properties of the intermediate code can be highlighted
selected:
Each three-way instruction has at most one operator, in addition toThe assignment.
The compiler must generate a temporary name to save the valuesintermediates calculated by each instruction: t1, t2 and t3.
Some instructions have fewer than three operands, such as the first and thelast example instructions.
1.4.3.2 Code optimization phase
This phase tries to improve the intermediate code, so that in the next
phase results in faster machine code to execute. Some optimizations are trivial. In our example there is a better way to calculate the commission, and goes through making trivial substitutions in the second and fourth statements, obtaining:
t2 = value * 8.0 commission = fixed + t2
The compiler can deduce that all occurrences of the variable t1
can be replaced by the constant 8.0, since t1 is assigned a value that no longer changes, so the first statement can be removed. Something similar happens with the variable t3, which is used only once, to transmit its value to commission in a direct assignment, then it is safe to substitute commission for t3, as a result of which another line of the intermediate code is removed.
1.4.3.3 Machine code generation phase
The final phase of a compiler is the generation of object code, which is usually
It generally consists of relocatable machine code or assembly code. Each of the variables used by the program are translated to a memory address (this also it has been possible to do in the intermediate code generation phase). Then each of the intermediate instructions is translated into a sequence of machine instructions which runs the same task. A decisive aspect is the assignment of variables to
records.
Following the same example, and using registers R1 and R2 of a
hypothetical microprocessor, the translation of the optimized code could be:
MOVE [1Ah], R1
MULT # 8.0, R1
MOVE [15h], R2
ADD R1, R2
MOVE R2, [10h]
The first and second operands of each instruction specify a source and
a destination, respectively. This code translates the content of the address [1Ah] to register R1, then multiplies it by the real constant 8.0. The third instruction passes the content of address [15h] to register R2. The fourth instruction adds the previously calculated value in register R1. Finally the value of register R2 is go to address [10h]. As the reader can guess, the commission variable is stores at address [10h], fixed at [15h] and value at [1Ah].
Episode 2
Lexicographic analysis
​ 2.1​ Overview
This chapter studies the first phase of a compiler, that is, its analysis
lexicographic, also called lexical analysis for short. The techniques used to build lexical analyzers can also be applied to other areas such as, for example, query languages  and information retrieval systems.
In each application, the underlying problem is the specification and design of programs that execute the actions triggered by words that follow certain patterns within the chains to recognize. As pattern-driven programming is widely extended and is of undoubted utility, there are numerous metalanguages  that
They allow you to establish pairs of the pattern-action form, so that the action is executed every time the system finds a series of characters whose structure matches with the boss. Specifically, we are going to study Lex in order to specify the lexical analyzers. In this language, patterns are specified by means of regular expressions, and Lex's metacompiler generates a recognizer of the regular expressions using a finite automaton (obviously deterministic) efficient.
On the other hand, a software tool that automates the construction of
Lexical analyzers allow people with different backgrounds to use the pattern concordance in their own application areas, since, at the time of True, it is not necessary to have deep computer skills to apply said tools.
​ 2.2​ Lexical Analyzer Concept
It is responsible for searching the lexical components or words that make up the
source program, according to rules or patterns.
The input of the lexical analyzer can be defined as a sequence of
characters, which can be encoded according to any standard: ASCII ( American
Standard Code for Information Interchange ), EBCDIC ( Extended Binary Coded Decimal Interchange Code ), Unicode, etc. The lexical analyzer splits this sequence into words with their own meaning and then converts them to a sequence of terminals from the parser point of view. This sequence is the point starting point for the parser to build the syntax tree that it recognizes the input sentence / s, as can be seen in figure 2 .1 .
2. 3
Lexicographic analysis
Figure 2.1. Inputs and outputs of the first two phases of the analysis stage. The phrase "Sequence of Terminals" refers to the grammar of the syntactic; but it is also possible to consider that said sequence is non-terminal if we use the lexicographic point of view.
The lexical analyzer recognizes words based on a regular grammar
so that the alphabet G of said grammar are the different characters of the game characters of the computer on which you are working (which form the set of symbols terminals), while their non-terminals are the lexical categories in which they are integrated the different sequences of characters. Each nonterminal or lexical category of the regular grammar of lexical analysis is regarded as a terminal grammar free context that the parser works with, so that the output of
high level (non-terminals) of the lexical phase assumes low level input (terminals) of the syntactic phase. In the case of Lex, for example, regular grammar is expressed using regular expressions.
2.2.1 Functions of the lexical analyzer
The lexical analyzer is the first phase of a compiler. Its main function
consists of reading the input characters and producing a sequence of
Lexical components used by the parser to do the parsing. Is
Figure 2.2. The lexical analysis phase is under the control of the analysis syntactic. Usually implemented as a function of this
interaction is usually applied by converting the lexical analyzer into a subroutine or parser coroutine. Received the order "Give me the next component lexicon ”from the parser, the lexicon reads the input characters until it can identify the next lexical component, which returns the syntactic according to the agreed format (see figure 2 . two).
In addition to this main function, the lexical analyzer also performs other
of great importance, namely:
Delete comments from the program.
Eliminate blank spaces, tabs, carriage return, etc, and in general,everything that lacks meaning according to the syntax of the language.
Recognize user identifiers, numbers, reserved words from thelanguage, etc., and treat them correctly with respect to the symbol table (only in cases where this parser needs to deal with such a structure).
Keep track of the line number you are reading from, in case it occursany error, give information about where it occurred.
Report lexical errors. For example, if the '@' character does not belong to the language, an error should be thrown.
It can also perform preprocessor functions.
2.2.2 Need for the lexical analyzer
A good professional must be able to question and consider all the
design decisions that are made, and an important issue is why the
Lexical analysis of the syntactic if, after all, the control is going to be carried out by the second. In other words, why isn't all source program processing just delegated to
syntactic analysis, which is perfectly possible (although not plausible as we shall see below), since the syntactic works with free context grammars and these they encompass the regulars. Below we will study some reasons for this separation.
2.2.2.1 Simplification of the design
A simple design is perhaps the most important advantage. Separate analysis The lexicon of parsing often allows you to simplify one, the other, or both phases. Usually adding a lexical analyzer allows to significantly simplify the parser. Furthermore, the simplification obtained is made especially patent when it is necessary to make modifications or extensions to the language initially devised; In other words, it makes compiler maintenance easier as the language evolves.
Fig. 2. 3 illustrates a situation in which, using the patterns
corresponding, the lexical analyzer recognizes integers and operators arithmetic. When building a first version of the parser,
Lexicographic analysis
we can assume that two expressions can be connected with any of these operators, so you can choose to group them all under the lexical category
OPARIT ( Arithmetic Operators). At a later stage, it may be necessary break this category down into as many others as semantically different operators beech: OPARIT disappears and MORE , MINUS , MULT and DIV appear . A
Such modification is trivial if both analyzers have been properly separated, since it consists of replacing the grouped pattern ('-' | '+' | '*' | '/') by the patterns disaggregated '-', '+', '*' and '/'.
Figure 2.3. Steps in the progressive construction of a compiler
If the syntactic had the grammar from step 1, the lexicographic would be:
+ the pattern (0 | 1 | 2 | ... | 9) returns the NUM category the pattern ('+' | '-' | '*' | '/') returns the OPARIT category
On the other hand, if the syntactic adopts step 2, the lexicographic would be:
+ the pattern (0 | 1 | 2 | ... | 9) returns the NUM category the pattern '+' returns the MAS category pattern '-' returns MINUS category pattern '*' returns category MULT pattern '/' returns category DIV
You can also think about eliminating the lexicographic, incorporating its
grammar in that of the syntactic, so that it would see its number of rules with the following:
​ NUM​ 6​ 0
​ |​ one
​ |​ two
​ |​ 3
...
​ |​ NUM NUM
However, automata designed to recognize lexical components and the tree syntactic are radically different, both in their conception and in their implementation which, again, leads us to establish a division between these analysis.
By way of conclusion, it can be said that it is highly recommended to work with
two grammars, one in charge of lexical analysis and the other in charge of analysis syntactic. Where is the limit between what is recognized by one grammar and another?
Is it considered a basic component? How many grammatical categories are established? If you create many grammatical categories, you will be complicating the grammar of the syntactic, as is the case for example in step 2. In general, a couple of rules must be followed basic to keep complexity at acceptable levels. The first is that
The information associated with each lexical category must be necessary and sufficient, which it will become clearer in later chapters when the concept of attribute is known . The second is that, as a general rule, the grammars that arise (regular and free context) should not be forced, in the sense that the different concepts that make up the programming language to be compiled must be part of one or of another naturally; e.g., the recognition to be made character by character (without that these have a semantic meaning on their own) should be part of the analysis lexicon.
2.2.2.2 Efficiency
The division between lexical and syntactic analysis also improves the efficiency of the
compiler. An independent lexical analyzer allows to build a processor specialized and potentially more efficient for the functions explained in the section 2.2.1 . Much of the compilation time is spent reading the program source and divide it into lexical components. With specialized techniques for handling buffers for reading input characters and processing patterns can be significantly improve the performance of a compiler.
2.2.2.3 Portability
Compiler portability is improved, since the peculiarities of the
starting alphabet, base character set, and other anomalies specific to input devices can be limited to the lexical analyzer. The representation of Special or non-standard symbols, such as '8' in Pascal, can be isolated in the lexical analyzer.
On the other hand, some languages, such as APL (A Program Language)
benefit greatly from the treatment of the characters that make up the program of input by an isolated analyzer. The Dictionary of Computing Oxford University Press defines this language as follows:
« ... Its main characteristic is that it provides a very large set
important operators to deal with multidimensional orders together with the
Lexicographic analysis
user ability to define their own operators. The built-in operators are represented mainly by single characters that use a set of special characters. Thus, APL programs are very concise and often impenetrable . '
2.2.2.4 Complex patterns
Another reason the two analyzes are separated is so that the analyzer
lexicon focuses on the recognition of complex building blocks. For example In Fortran, there is the following pair of very syntactically similar propositions, but of very different meaning:
DO5I = 2.5
(Assignment of value 2.5 to variable DO5I)
DO 5 I = 2, 5
(Loop that repeats for I = 2, 3, 4 and 5)
In this language the blank spaces are not significant outside the
comments and a certain type of strings (to save storage space, in an era of Informatics when this was a rare commodity), so that Suppose all removable blanks are suppressed before starting
lexical analysis. In such a case, the above propositions would appear before the lexical analyzer like:
DO5I = 2.5
DO5I = 2.5
The lexical analyzer does not know if DO is a reserved word or is the prefix of the
name of a variable until the comma is read. It was necessary to examine the chain input far beyond the word itself to be recognized by doing what is called lookahead (or pre-search). The complexity of this processing makes It is recommended to isolate it in a separate phase of the parsing.
In any case, in languages  like Fortran the language was first designed
and then the compiler, which led to problems like the one just raised. Today's languages  are designed with the tools available in mind for the construction of your compiler and this type of situation is avoided.

2.3.1 Approaches to build an analyzer lexicographical
There are three basic mechanisms for building a lexicographic analyzer:
Ad hoc. It consists of the coding of a recognizer program that does not follow the formalisms of the theory of automata. This type of constructions it is very error prone and difficult to maintain.
Through the manual implementation of finite automata. This mechanism consists of building the necessary patterns for each lexical category, build your individual finite automats, merge them by optionality, and, finally, implement the resulting automata. Although the construction of analyzers using this method is systematic and not prone to errors, any update of the recognizing patterns implies the modification of the code that implements them, so maintenance is very expensive.
Through a metacompiler. In this case, a special program is used which has as input pairs of the form (regular expression, action). He 30
43
metacompiler generates all finite automata, converts them to automata deterministic finite, and implements it in C. The program C thus generated is compiles and generates an executable that is the lexical analyzer of our language. Of course, there are metacompilers that generate Java code, Pascal, etc. instead of C.
Since today there are numerous tools to build analyzers
lexicons from special purpose notations based on regular expressions, We will rely on them to continue our study since, in addition, the The resulting analyzers are usually quite efficient in both time and memory. We will start with a generator of lexical analyzers written in C, and we will continue with another that generates Java code.
2.4​ The Lexicographic Analyzer Generator: PCLex
This section describes the currently most widespread tool, called Lex, for the specification of lexical analyzers in general, although in our case we will focus on a compiler's lexical analyzer. The equivalent tool for DOS environment it is called PCLex, and the specification of its input, Lex language. The study of an existing tool will show how, using expressions regular, you can combine pattern specification with actions, for e.g.
make inserts in a symbol table.
2.4.1 Overview
As we already know, the recognition rules are of the form:
p one
{action } one
p two
{action } two
...
...
p n
{action } n
where p is a regular expression and action is a program fragment that describesi              i what should be the action of the lexical analyzer when it encounters a lexeme that fits by p. In Lex, actions are written in C, although there are manyi metacompilers similar to PCLex that allow coding in other languages, such as for example Jflex that uses the Java language.
A lexical analyzer created by PCLex is designed to behave in
sync with a parser. To do this, among the generated code there is a function called yylex () which, when invoked (usually by the syntactic), starts to read the input, character by character, until it finds the largest prefix in the input that matches one of the regular expressions p; said prefix constitutes a              i lexeme and, once read, it is said to "have been consumed" in the sense that the point The reader has advanced to the first character that follows it. Then yylex () execute the action. Usually this action will return control to the parseri              i syntactic informing you of the token found. However, if the stock does not have a
31
44
Lexicographic analysis
return , the lexical analyzer will find a new lexeme, and so on successively until an action returns control to the parser, or until
reach the end of the string, represented by the EOF ( End Of File ) character. The repeated search of lexemes until an explicit return statement is found allows the lexical analyzer to process blanks and comments appropriately.
Before executing the action associated with a pattern, yylex () stores the lexeme
read in variable yytext . Any additional information that you want to communicate to the calling function (usually the parser), in addition to the token you must be stored in the global variable yylval , whose type will be seen later.
The programs you get with PCLex are relatively large, (although
very fast as well), although this is not usually a problem in the face of huge amounts of memory that are handled today. The great advantage of PCLex is that allows you to do complex analyzers quite quickly.
2.4.2 Creating a lexical analyzer
As already mentioned, PCLex has its own language, which we will call Lex and that allows you to specify the abstract structure of a lexical analyzer, both in as regards regex as the action to take when finding a lexeme that fits in each of them.
The steps to create a lexical analyzer with this tool are (figure 2 . 4 ):

In the rules area you define the patterns of the lexemes you want

Lexicographic analysis
2.4.3.1 Lex premises for recognizing lexemes
The yylex () generated by PCLex follows two fundamental guidelines for
recognize lexemes in case of ambiguity. These guidelines are, in order of priority:
Always enter through the pattern that recognizes the longest possible lexeme.
In case of conflict, use the pattern that appears in the first position.
As a consequence of the second premise: the patterns that recognize
reserved words are always placed before the user identifier pattern.
E.g. a lexical analyzer for Pascal (where TYPE and VAR are words reserved), it might look like:
%%
"TYPE"
"VAR"
[AZ] [A-Z0-9] *
...
When yylex () meets the string "VAR" a conflict occurs,
(since this lexeme can enter both through the second and third pattern). Then take the pattern that appears before, which in the example would be "VAR", recognizing the lexeme as a reserved word. Change the order of both patterns has dire consequences:
%%
"TYPE"
[AZ] [A-Z0-9] *
"VAR"
...
since this time the lexeme "VAR" would enter through the user identifier pattern, and it would never be recognized as a reserved word: the “VAR” pattern is superfluous.
2.4.3.2 Lex special characters
Lex relies on the ASCII character set to represent expressions
regular, so the characters that we will see next have a meaning special for that purpose:
"": Used to enclose any string of literals. As a general rule it is not literals need to be enclosed in quotes unless they include symbols special, that is, the “WHILE” pattern and the WHILE pattern are equivalent; but to represent eg the beginning of the comment in Modula-2 it is necessary Quote the characters that make up the pattern: "(*", since it it contains, in turn, special symbols.
\: makes the next character literal. Eg: \ ”recognizes quotation marks. I also know used to express those characters that have no direct representation per screen: \ n for carriage return, \ t for tab, etc.
3. 4
47
\ octal # : represents the character whose ASCII value is octal # . Eg: \ 012 recognize the decimal character 10 that corresponds to LF ( Line Feed ).
[]: allow you to specify lists of characters, that is, one of the characters that encloses, eg: [abc] recognizes either the 'a', or the 'b', or the 'c', ([abc] / (a  | b | c)). Inside the brackets the following characters also have a meaning special:
-: indicates range. Ex .: [A-Z0-9] recognizes any character from 'A' to the 'Z' or '0' to '9'.
^: indicates completion when it appears at the beginning, just after “[”.
Eg: [^ abc] recognizes any character except 'a', 'b' or 'c'. Ex .: [^ AZ] recognizes any character except those from 'A' to
'Z'.
one
?: what precedes it is optional. Ex .: a? / (a | g). Ex .: [AZ]? recognize any letter from 'A' to 'Z' or g. Eg: a? B / ab | gb.
.: represents any character (but only one) except carriage return
(\ n). It is very interesting because it allows us to collect any other character that is not recognized by the above patterns.
|: indicates optionality ( OR ). Eg: a | b recognizes 'a' or 'b'. Ex .:. | \ N recognizes any character. The pattern (. | \ N) * is curious since this is where the entire program, and since yylex () has the premise of recognizing the most long, as it will probably ignore any other patterns. It also turns out it is likely that during recognition an error will occur due to storage space overflow of variable yytext which, remember, it stores the current lexeme.
*: indicates repetition 0 or more times of what precedes it.
+: indicates repetition 1 or more times of what precedes it.
(): allow grouping (same as in arithmetic expressions).
{}: indicate repeating range. Eg: a {1,5} / aa? A? A? A? Keys come to be something like a * restricted. It also allows us to assign it a name to a regular expression to reuse it in multiple patterns; this will be seen later.
2.4.3.3 Context-sensitive characters
Lex provides certain capabilities to recognize patterns that do not fit
to a regular expression, but rather to a context-free grammar. This is especially useful in certain circumstances, such as for recognizing
End-of-line comments, supported by some programming languages  (the
oneThe expression g represents the empty string.
35
48
Lexicographic analysis
which usually start with // and extend to the end of the current line.
$: the preceding pattern is only recognized if it is at the end of the line. Ex .: (a | b | cd) $. That the lexeme is at the end of the line means that it is followed by a carriage return, or by EOF. The character that identifies the end of the line is not part of the lexeme.
^: outside the brackets indicates that the pattern that follows is recognized only if is at the beginning of the line. That the lexeme is at the beginning of the line means that it is preceded by a carriage return, or that it is find at the beginning of the file. Carriage return does not become part of the lexeme. Note how Lex's two premises make the patterns of context sensitivity should be put first if there are ambiguities:
Program 1
Program 2
^ "Home"
"House"
"House"
^ "Home"
In both programs the lexeme "home" when it is at
beginning of line can be entered by both patterns then, by existing ambiguity, Lex selects first. Therefore, note how in the first program is entered by the appropriate pattern while the second is entered by the general pattern, so the pattern ^ "home" will never be used.
/: Recognizes the pattern that precedes it if and only if it is a prefix of a sequence simple as the one that happens. Ex .: ab / c; in this case if at the entrance you have "Abcd", the lexeme "ab" would be recognized because it is succeeded by 'c'. If the entry outside "abdc" the pattern would not apply. On the other hand, a pattern like ab / c + is wrong since the c + pattern is not considered simple.
2.4.3.4 Lexical status
The lexical states come to be like exclusive logical variables (one and
only one can be active at a time) which serve to indicate that a pattern can only be applied if the associated lexical state is activated. In the examples that we have seen so far we have worked with the default lexical state that, also by default, it is active when starting the yylex () job . For being a lexical state by default we have not had to make any explicit reference to it.
The various lexical states (also called start conditions ) are declared
in the Lex definitions area of  the form:
​ % START id, id, ...one​ two
An action associated with a pattern can activate a lexical state by executing the
macro BEGIN , which is supplied by the program generated by PCLex. So:
​ BEGIN id;​ i
it would activate the start id condition , which should have been previously declared. i              Activation of a lexical state automatically causes the deactivation of all the others. By

On the other hand, the default lexical state can be activated with:
BEGIN 0;
To indicate that a pattern is only a candidate to be applied in case it is
​ find the start id condition active , the string "<id>" is prepended to it. i              If a pattern              i
does not have an explicit start condition associated with it , it is assumed that it has the lexical state associated with it default.
The following example shows how to display all the names of the

% START PROC

"PROCEDURE" {BEGIN PROC;}
<PROC> [a-zA-Z] [a-zA-Z0-9] * { printf ("% s \ n", yytext); BEGIN 0; } 2.4.3.5 Definitions area and functions area
The definitions area of  a Lex program has three utilities
fundamental:
Define the lexical states.
Assign a name to the most frequent patterns.
Put C code that will be global to the whole program.
We have already seen how to define lexical states, so we will go on to study
the other two possibilities.
In the definition area we can create auxiliary regular expressions of
frequently used and assign a name to them. Later, these patterns can be referenced in the rules area simply by specifying their names in braces: {}. For example, the following two programs are equivalent, but the first is more clear and its more concise rules:

Let's remember that in Lex, everything must start in the first column. If something
does not start in that column, PCLex passes it directly to the generated C program, without processing it. In this way it is possible to create definitions of variables, etc. Without However, this is not recommended as Lex has a specific block for this in which you can even put processor directives, which must start obligatorily in the first column.
Thus, the definitions area also allows you to place pure C code that is
will translate as is to the beginning of the C program generated by PCLex. For this I 37

The reader is probably wondering what the main () function contains
of the C program that PCLex generates. The truth is that PCLex does not generate any main () , rather, it must be specified by the programmer. As a general rule, when intends to run a lexical analyzer in isolation (without an associated syntactic),
Normal is that the actions associated with each rule do not contain any returns and that include a main () that only invokes yylex ():
void main () {
yylex ();
};
To specify the main () and how many additional functions are estimated
appropriate, the functions area is available. This area is completely copied by PCLex in the generated C file. That is why, for practical purposes, writing code between "% {" and "%}" in the definition area is equivalent to writing it in the functions.

2.4.3.6 Functions and variables supplied by PCLex
As we already know, the basic core of the C program generated by PCLex 38
51
is the yylex () function , which is responsible for looking for a lexeme and executing its action associated. This process is carried out iteratively until one of the actions find a return or the input is terminated. In addition, PCLex provides other functions
macros and supporting variables; below is the list of the most interesting In order of importance:
yylex () : implements the lexicographic analyzer.
yytext : contains the current lexeme yyleng : number of characters of the current lexeme.
yylval : is a global variable that allows communication with the syntactic. It is not actually defined by PCLex but by the PCYacc tool that will be studied in later chapters.
yyin : is of type * FILE, and points to the input file that is read. Initially points to stdin , so the input file matches the input standard.
yyout : of type * FILE, it points to the output file (initially stdout ).
yyerror () : it is a function that is responsible for emitting and handling errors (it removes error messages on screen). It is actually defined by PCYacc as you will see later.
yylineno : integer variable that, curiously, must be created, initialized and updated by the programmer. Serves to keep track of the number line being processed. It is normally initialized to 1 and increments each time a carriage return is encountered. yywrap () : the yylex () algorithm calls this macro automatically every time that meets an EOF. The usefulness of this macro is that it can be redefined (for this you must first delete it with the directive
#undef ). This macro should return false (which in C is represented by the integer
0) in case the input string has not actually ended, which it can happen in two types of situations: a) a file is being processed binary that can contain the EOF character in the middle as one more, in which case the true end of the file must be discovered by contrasting the file length with the length of the consumed string; and b) the string to be recognized is partitioned into several files, in which case when you reach the end of one of them, you have to load the next one in yyin and continue processing. yywrap () should return true (integer value 1) in case the found EOF actually identifies the end of the string to process.
yyless (int n) : leaves the first n characters of the current lexeme in yytext . The rest returns them to the input, so we can say that they are de-consumed. yyleng is also conveniently modified. For example, the pattern abc * / \ n
39
52
Lexicographic analysis
It is equivalent to:
abc * \ n {yyless (yyleng-1); }
input () : consumes the next character from the input and appends it to the current lexeme.
For example, the program:
%%
​ abc​ {printf ("% s", yytext);
input (); printf ("% s", yytext);} before the input "abcde" it would enter through this pattern: the lexeme before the input () (in the first printf ) is "abc", and after the input (in the second printf ) it is "Abcd".
output (char c) : outputs the character c on standard output. PCLex for DOS no supports this function, but Lex for Unix does.
unput (char c) : unconsumes character c and places it at the beginning of the input.
For example, suppose the program:
%%
abc​ {printf ("% s", yytext); unput ('t'); } such​ {printf ("% s", yytext); }
receives the entry "abcal". The first three characters of the lexeme match with the first pattern. Then the string "al" remains in the input, but as an unput ('t') has been made , what is actually at the input is "such", that matches the second pattern.
ECHO : macro that copies the input to the output (it is the action that is applied by default to lexemes that do not fit by any explicit pattern).
yymore () : allows you to go on to recognize the next lexeme, but without deleting the current content of yytext , so the new lexeme read is concatenated to the it already exists. PCLex for DOS does not support this function, but Lex for Unix.
The main utility of yymore () is that it makes it easier to recognize
the literals in quotes. Suppose you want to build a pattern to recognize quoted literals. A first idea could be the Pattern:
​ \ ”[^ \”] * \ ”​ / * Lexemas that start with quotes,
anything, and end in quotes. * /
that it would recognize strings like "Hello", "bye", and even "Ho; *". Without However a string like "Hello, \" comrade \ "" which in turn contains quotes inside will give problems because the first quote for "comrade" is considered as a closing quotation mark. The solution to this problem happens by the following Lex code block:
​ \ ”[^ \”] * / \ ”​ {if (yytext [yyleng-1] == '\\')
yymore ();
40
53
else
input();} This pattern recognizes quoted strings (except the last quotes), so that strings that have quotes inside are recognized in parts.
Continuing with the example from before, "Hello, \" comrade \ "" would be recognized how:
This pattern would fail if the characters before the closing quotation marks were precisely '\\'. To solve it, you would have to make use of unput and a global variable that would act as a flag . Any sentence behind de yymore () will never run as it acts as a kind of goto .
REJECT : reject the current lexeme, return it to the input, and look for another pattern.
REJECT tells yylex () that the lexeme found does not match actually to the current regex, and find the following expression regulate as appropriate. The REJECT macro should be the last thing a action execute since if there is something behind it, it will not be executed. E.g. To search how many times the words "keyboard" and "side" appear, you can construct the following Lex program:
% {
int t = 0, l = 0;
%} %% keyboard​ {t ++; REJECT;} side​ {l ++;}
When entering "keyboard" this program behaves as follows:
1.- Enter through the pattern that reads the longest lexeme that would be "Keyboard" and executes the associated action: the variable t is increased and the current lexeme is rejected, so the entire text is returned to the entrance.
2.- It displays “tec” on the screen, which is the default action when they find characters that don't match any pattern.
3.- The lexeme "side" 'coincides with the second pattern and executes the associated action: variable l is increased .
Finally, it is clear that the programmer should not declare any
function or variable whose name matches the ones we just studied. In fact PCLex also generates a series of auxiliary variables whose name consists of a single character, so it is not good to declare variables or functions with names of a single letter or starting with yy .

​ 2.5​ The JFlex Lexicographic Analyzer Generator
JFlex is a generator of lexicographic analyzers developed by Gerwin Klein as an extension to the JLex tool developed at the University of Princeton. JFlex is developed in Java and generates Java code.
Programs written for JFlex have a format similar to those written
in PCLex; in fact all permissible regular patterns in Lex are also supported by JFlex, so in this section we will focus only on the differences and extensions, both of patterns and of the skeleton that the input file to JFlex.
Installing and running JFlex is trivial. Once the
file jflex-1.3.5.zip , we will have the file JFlex.jar that is only necessary in meta-compilation time, the generated parser being totally independent. The Main class of the JFlex package is the one in charge of metacompiling our input .jflex program; thus, a typical invocation is of the form:
java JFlex.Main file.jflex
which will generate a Yylex.java file that implements the lexicographic analyzer. The figure 2 . 5 illustrates the process to follow, and how the default name of the generated class it's Yylex .
Figure 2.5. Obtaining a portable Java program from a JFlex specification

2.5.2 Options and declarations area
This area allows you to indicate to JFlex a series of options to adapt the file . java resulting from the meta-compilation and that will be the one that implements our lexicographic analyzer in Java. It also allows associating a user identifier to the most used patterns in the area of  rules.
2.5.2.1 Options
The most interesting options can be classified into class options, from
the analysis function, end of file, character set and counters. All
they start with the % character and cannot be preceded by anything on the line in that appear.
​ 2.5.2.1.1​ Class options
The most useful class options are:
% class classname . By default, the class that JFlex generates and implements the lexicographic analyzer is called Yylex and is therefore written in a
Yylex.java file . Using this option you can change the name of said class.
% interface1 implements, Interface2, etc . Generate a class (by default Yylex ) which implements the indicated interfaces. The programmer must enter the methods necessary to make this implementation effective.
% extends classname . Generates a class that inherits from the indicated class.
% public . Generate a public class. The class generated by default does not have modify visibility scope.
Final% . Generate a final class, from which no one can inherit.
% abstract . Generates an abstract class from which objects cannot be created.
% {javablock%} . The programmer can also include his own declarations and methods inside the Yylex class by writing these between the % { and %} symbols .
% init {Javacode% init} . It is possible to include code in the generated constructor automatically by JFlex for the Yylex class by using this option.
​ 2.5.2.1.2​ Analysis function options
These options allow modifying the method or function in charge of performing
the lexicographic analysis itself. The most useful options are:
44
57
% function parserFunctionName . By default, the function that starts the parsing is called yylex () and returns a value of type Yytoken . This option allows you to rename the yylex () function .
% int . Makes the yylex () function return values  of type int instead of Yytoken .
% intwrap . Makes the yylex () function return values  of type Integer in Yytoken place .
% type typename . Makes the yylex () function return values  of type specified (whether primitive or not) instead of Yytoken .
In any case, JFlex does not create the Yytoken class , it must be
supplied externally, or specified in the code area.
​ 2.5.2.1.3​ End of file options
By default, when yylex () meets the end of file character, it returns a
null value . In case % int was specified in the previous options,
will return the YYEOF value defined as public static final in the Yylex class . For To change this behavior, the most useful end-of-file options are:
% eofclose . Causes yylex () to close the read channel as soon as the EOF.
% eofval {JavaCode% eofval} . It is possible to execute a block of code when yylex () finds the EOF. This option will be used for this.
​ 2.5.2.1.4​ Character set options
These options allow you to specify the character set in which it will be
encoded the input to the lexicographic parser generated by JFlex. The posibilities They are:
% 7bit . It is the default option, and it assumes that each character of the input is made up of a single byte whose most significant bit is 0, which gives 128 characters.
% 8bit . It assumes that each character is made up of a complete byte, which gives 256 characters.
% unicode . It assumes that the input will consist of Unicode characters. This does not it means that two bytes of the input are taken for each character but
character recovery is left to the platform on which it is run yylex ().
% ignore . Make yylex () ignore case by using the use of the toUpperCase and toLowerCase methods of the Character class .
It is clear that the first three options are mutually exclusive.

58
Lexicographic analysis
​ 2.5.2.1.5​ Counter options
These options cause the lexicographic analyzer to store in counters
the number of characters, lines, or columns where the current lexeme begins. The nomenclature is:
% char . Stores in the variable yychar the number of characters between the beginning of the input channel and the beginning of the current lexeme.
% line . Stores in the variable yyline the number of the line where the current lexeme.
% column . stores in the variable yycolumn the column number in which the current lexeme begins.
All counters start at 0.
2.5.2.2 Declarations.
In addition to options, the programmer can indicate declarations of two types
in the area at hand, namely, statements of lexical statements and statements of rules.
​ 2.5.2.2.1​ Statements of lexical statements.
Lexical states are declared by the option:
% state state1, state2, etc.
and they can be used in the rules area in the same way as in Lex. Also, if there is many patterns in the rule area that share the same lexical state, it is possible indicate this only once and then group all these rules in braces, such as can be seen in the preliminary example of point 2.5.1 . The initial state is given by the constant YYINITIAL and, unlike Lex, can be used as any another user-defined lexical state.
A lexical action can change its lexical state by invoking the function
yybegin (LexicalState) generated by JFlex.
​ 2.5.2.2.2​ Rule statements.
In case a pattern is used repeatedly or when its complexity
is high, it is possible to assign a name to it and use it later in any other rule enclosing it in brackets, analogous to how it was studied in Lex. The syntax is: name = pattern
2.5.3 Rules area.
The rules area has the same structure as in Lex, with the only difference
that it is possible to group the rules to apply in the same lexical state. How example, the rules:

59
​ <YYINITIAL> "="​ {System.out.println ("Found ="); }
