they can also be written as:
<YYINITIAL> {
"="
{System.out.println ("Found ="); }
"! ="
{System.out.println ("Found! ="); }
}
The important differences in this area with respect to Lex are concentrated in
that JFlex includes some additional features for specifying patterns, as they are:
\ u #hexadecimal - Represents the character whose Unicode value is #hexadecimal .
Eg: \ u042D represents the character "Э".
!: fits any lexeme except those that enter through the pattern that happens. Ex .:! (Ab) matches anything except the lexeme "ab".
~: fits a lexeme that begins with anything and ends with the first appearance of a text that fits the pattern that follows. Eg the way easier to recognize a Java-style comment (not nested) comes given by the pattern: "/ *" ~ "* /"
On the other hand, JFlex includes some predefined character classes. These
classes are enclosed between the symbols [: and:] and each one matches the set of characters that satisfy a certain predicate. For example, the pattern [: digit:] matches those characters that satisfy the isDigit () logical function of the class Character . The list of patterns is:
Pattern
Associated predicate

isLetter ()
[: digit:]
isDigit ()
[: uppercase:]
isUpperCase ()

isLowerCase ()
Finally, two other important differences from Lex are:
Every rule must have an associated lexical action, even if it isempty.
If a lexeme does not fit by any pattern, a lexical error occurs.
The pattern \ n encompasses only the ASCII code 10 character, but not the code characterASCII 13, which is represented by the \ r pattern.

Chapter 3
Syntactic analysis
​ 3.1​ Overview
Every programming language obeys rules that describe the
syntactic structure of well-formed programs it accepts. In Pascal, by
For example, a program is made up of blocks; a block, of sentences; a sentence, of expressions; an expression, of lexical components; and so on until get to the basic characters. You can describe the syntax of the constructs of programming languages  by means of free context grammars or by using BNF ( Backus-Naur Form ) notation .
Formal grammars offer significant advantages to designers of
languages  and compiler developers:
Grammars are precise, syntactic specifications of languages  of programming.
A parser can be automatically generated from a grammarsyntactic.
The automatic generation process above can lead to discoveringambiguities.
A grammar provides a structure to a programming language,being easier to generate code and detect errors.
It is easier to expand / modify language if it is described with a grammar.
Most of this topic is devoted to methods of analysis
syntactic of typical use in compilers. We'll start with the basics and proper techniques for manual application. Next we will discuss the management and syntactic error recovery. We will study the formal methods of analysis using battery-powered automata and, finally, we will delve into the operation of metacompilers that automatically generate the parser of a language.
​ 3.2​ Parser concept
It is the phase of the analyzer that is in charge of checking the sequence of tokens
A representing the input text, based on a given grammar. In case the input program is valid, it supplies the syntax tree that recognizes it based on to a computational representation. This tree is the starting point of the phase later in the analysis stage: the semantic analyzer.
49
62
Syntactic analysis
But this is the theory; in practice, the parser drives the process
compilation, so that the rest of the phases evolve as the syntactic
it recognizes the input sequence, so often the tree does not even actually generates.
In practice, the parser also:
It incorporates semantic actions in which to place the rest of the phases of thecompiler (except the lexical analyzer): from semantic analysis to code generation.
Reports the nature of the syntactic errors it finds and attemptsrecover from them to continue compilation.
Controls the flow of tokens recognized by the lexical analyzer.
In short, it performs almost all the operations of the compilation, resulting in
to a working method called syntax-driven compilation .
​ 3.3​ Syntactic error handling
If a compiler had to process only correct programs, its design and
implementation would be greatly simplified. The first versions of the programs are often incorrect, and a good compiler should help the programmer to identify and locate errors. Furthermore, consider the management of
Errors can simplify the structure of a compiler and improve its response to errors. mistakes.
Programming errors can be of the following types:
Lexical, produced by misspelling an identifier, a keyword or aoperator.
Syntactic, by an arithmetic expression or unbalanced parentheses.
Semantics, as an operator applied to an incompatible operand.
Logical, it can be an infinitely recursive call.
Correction, when the program does not do what the programmer actually doeswished.
It is clear that correction errors cannot be detected by
a compiler, since they involve the abstract concept that the programmer has about the program it builds, which is unknown, and probably
unknowable, by the compiler. On the other hand, the detection of logical errors implies a very large computational effort as the compiler must be able to find out the different flows that a running program can follow which, in
In many cases, it is not only expensive, but also impossible. For all this, Current compilers focus on recognizing the first three types of
mistakes. In this topic we will talk about syntax errors, which are the ones that can prevent the correct construction of a syntactic tree.
fifty
63
Syntax error handling is the most complicated from the point of view
of compiler creation. We are interested that when the compiler finds a error, do not cancel the compilation for good, but recover and keep looking mistakes. Recovering an error does not mean correcting it, but being able to continue building the syntactic tree despite the errors found. In view of this, the The error handler of a parser should have as objectives:
Indicate errors clearly and precisely. You must report through corresponding messages of the type of error and its location.
Recover from the error, so that you can continue to examine the entry.• Distinguish between errors and warnings. Warnings are often used to report valid sentences but which, because they are infrequent, they can be a source of logical errors.
Do not significantly slow down the compilation.
A good compiler should always be made with the
errors that may occur, thereby simplifying its structure.
Also, if the compiler itself is prepared to admit even the most bugs frequent, then you can improve the response to these errors even correcting them.
Several strategies to manage errors will be discussed below.
once detected
3.3.1 Ignore the problem
This strategy (called panic mode in English) consists of ignoring the rest
from the entrance to a safe condition. Such a condition occurs when we find a special token (for example a ';' or an 'END'). Starting from this point it continues to be analyzed normally. The tokens found from detection of the error until the error condition is discarded, as well as the sequence of tokens prior to the error that is deemed appropriate (usually until the previous safety condition).
For example, suppose the following sequence of assignment statements
in C or Java:
aux = a [i] a [i] = a [j]; a [j] = aux;
The sequence found by the parser is shown in the figure 3 . one;
clearly the semicolon from the first statement is missing and since it doesn't make sense a statement like:
aux = a [i] a [i] = a [j];
since the parser notices an error, and takes advantage of the fact that the ';' is used as statement terminator to ignore the rest of the input until the next ';'. Some additional considerations on this error management strategy may be studied in sections 4.3.4 and8.4.1 .
51
64
Syntactic analysis
Figure 3.1. Syntactic error recovery ignoring erroneous sequence
3.3.2 Phrase-level recovery
Try to correct the error once discovered. For example, in the case proposed in the
point above, you might have been smart enough to insert the token ';' . Be careful with this method, as it can lead to infinite recoveries, that is, situations in which the correction attempt is not the correct one, but introduces a new error which, in turn, attempts to correct same wrong way and so on.
3.3.3 Additional production rules
This mechanism adds to the formal grammar that describes the language rules
of production to recognize the most common mistakes. Continuing with the case of point 3.3.1 , you could have put something like:
wrong_sent​ ÷ sent_unfinished sent_finished sent_finished​ ÷ statement ';' sent_unfinished ÷ sentence
which gives us greater control, and even allows us to recover and correct the problem and issue a warning message instead of an error message.
3.3.4 Global Correction
This method tries by all means to obtain a syntactic tree for a
token sequence . If there is any error and the sequence cannot be recognized, then this method infers a syntactically correct sequence of tokens as close as possible to the original and generates the tree for that sequence. That is, the parser
it asks the whole sequence of tokens to the lexicon, and what it does is return the closest thing to the input string but without errors, as well as the tree that recognizes it.
​ 3.4​ Grammar used by a parser
We will focus our efforts on the study of syntactic analysis to
â E
÷
E + T
to
|
T
ä T
÷
T * F
to
|
F
languages  based on formal grammars, since otherwise it becomes very difficult to compiler understanding. The formalization of the language that a compiler accepts systematizes its construction and allows correcting, perhaps more easily, errors of very difficult to locate, as is the ambiguity in the recognition of certain sentences.
52
65
The grammar that the parser accepts is a context grammar
free, since it is not easy to understand more complex grammars or to construct automatically reduced automata that recognize the sentences they accept.
Remember that a grammar G is defined by a tuple of four elements (N, T, P, S), where:
N = No terminals.
T = Terminals.
P = Production Rules.
S = Initial Axiom.

3.4.2 Syntactic tree of a language sentence
Like we can analyze a sentence in Spanish and get its tree
syntactic, it is also possible to do it with a sentence of a language of programming. Basically a syntactic tree corresponds to a sentence, obeys a grammar, and constitutes a representation that is used to describe the process of derivation of said sentence. The root of the tree is the initial axiom and, according to our convenience, we will draw it on the top or bottom of the tree.
As internal nodes of the tree, the non-terminal elements of the
production rules that we apply, and each one of them will have so many children as symbols exist on the right side of the applied rule.
Let's see an example using the grammar of the box 3 . 1 ; Let's suppose
we must recognize the string (a + b) * a + b that the lexical analyzer has given us supplied as (id + id) * id + id and we will build the syntax tree, whichto              b              to              b It would be the one in figure 3 .2 .
Note that a sequence of derivations originating from the initial axiom and
destination in the string to be recognized constitutes a representation of the syntactic tree. TO each bypass step a new piece of the tree is added, so that at the beginning
The process starts with a tree that only has the root (the initial axiom), just like
54
67
Figure 3.2. Syntactic tree corresponding to the recognition of the
​ sequence (id + id) * id + id, which is marked with color.to              b              to              b
the derivations that we have seen also start from the initial axiom. In this way, in Each derivation selects a leaf node from the tree that is being formed and is their children add. At each step, the leaves of the tree that is being built constitute the sentential form through which the derivation runs. On the other hand, the sequence of derivations not only represents a syntactic tree, but also gives information in which order the production rules have been applied.
The construction of this tree entails the intelligent application of the rules
of production. If the sentence to be recognized is incorrect, that is, it does not belong to the language generated by the grammar, it will be impossible to obtain its syntactic tree. In others Sometimes a statement admits more than one syntactic tree. When this happens it is said that the grammar is ambiguous. This type of situation must be avoided, so that, either the grammar is changed, or disambiguous rules are applied (which we will see later). For example, the grammar of the box3 . 2 is ambiguous since before a multiple sum does not distinguish between right and right associativity.

​ 3.5​ Types of parsing
According to the approximation that is taken to construct the syntactic tree, They give off two types or classes of analyzers:
56
69
Descendants: they start from the initial axiom, and make derivations toleft until obtaining the sequence of leads that recognizes the judgment. They may be:
"With setback.
"With recursive functions.
"From grammars LL (1).
Ascending: They start from the input sentence, and they apply derivationsinverse (from the consequent to the antecedent), up to the axiom initial. They may be:
"With setback.
"From grammars LR (1).
We will study each of these methods in detail below.
3.5.1 Downward analysis with retracement
As it is a top-down method, we start from the initial axiom and apply
in sequence all possible rules to the leftmost nonterminal of the form sentence for which you are working.
We can say that all sentences of a language are found as
nodes of a tree representing all possible derivations from any
sentential form, and which has as root the initial axiom. This is the universal tree of a grammar, so that its branches are sequences of derivations and, therefore, represent syntactic trees. Basically, we can say that the analysis method descending with backspace intends to search the universal tree for the sentence a recognize; when it finds it, the path that separates it from the root gives us the tree syntactic. Now, it is possible that the sentence is erroneous and that no as a leaf of the tree which, together with the fact that the tree is very likely to be infinite, leads to the need to propose a statement that tells us when to cancel the search because it is considered fruitless.
To see an example of this, let's start with the grammar of the table 3 .3 what
is equivalent to the one in the picture 3 .1 , although the one proposed now is not recursive because the left. And suppose you want to recognize the sequence:

With the pruning criteria chosen, the in-depth search will not work
if the grammar is left recursive, since there will be infinite branches in which the number of terminals to the left of a sentential form does not increase and our search will go into an infinite recursion. In these cases it is necessary to include new pruning criteria.
Parsers with backtracking are rarely used in practice.
This is because backtracking is highly inefficient and there are other types of more powerful analyzes based on different mechanisms, as we will see later. Even for natural language backtracking is not efficient at all, and others are preferred methods.

3.5.2 Top-down analysis with recursive functions
60
73
A free context grammar constitutes a mechanism for expressing a
formal language. However, it is not the only one, since the BNF notation and the diagrams
syntax have the same power.
Assuming that the BNF notation also allows expressing a language
formal, a definition of the syntax diagram will be given below and it will be demonstrated that these have, at least, the same power as the aforementioned BNF notation. From this point on, we will be able to build an ad hoc parser using any programming language that allows recursion.
3.5.2.1 Syntax diagrams
A syntax diagram (also called Conway diagrams) is a graph
directed where the non-terminal elements appear as rectangles, and the terminals as circles or ellipses.
Every syntax diagram has an origin and a destination, which are not usually
represent explicitly, but the origin is assumed to be on the left on the diagram and the destination on the right.
Each arc with origin at α and destination at β represents that the symbol α can
be followed by β (α and β can be both terminal and non-terminal). This forms all possible paths from the beginning of the graph to the end, they represent valid sentential forms.
3.5.2.2 Power of syntax diagrams
We will show that syntax diagrams allow us to represent the same
grammars than the BNF notation, by induction on the basic BNF operations:
​ Operation​ BNF​ Syntax diagram
​ Juxtaposition​ AB
​ Option​ A | B
g | B
​ Repetition​ 1 or more times
{B}
0 or more times
[B]
Following the «Rosetta stone» that the previous table supposes, it is possible
convert any BNF expression to its corresponding syntax diagram, since A and B can, in turn, be expressions or complex diagrams.
On the other hand, anyone who has worked extensively on syntax diagrams

You may have noticed that it is not always easy to convert a syntax diagram any to its corresponding in BNF notation.

3.5.3 Top-down analysis of LL grammars (1)
This type of analysis is based on a recognition automaton in the form of
table, called the syntax check table. This table has as axis of
ordered to the non-terminals of the grammar, and as abscissa to the terminals (including in pseudotoken EOF). The content of each inner box (which corresponds to the column of a terminal and the row of a nonterminal) contains either a production, or it is empty, which is interpreted as a rejection of the chain to recognize.
In general, we can say that an LL (1) grammar is one in which it is
enough to examine only one symbol at the input, to know which rule to apply, starting from the initial axiom and making derivations to the left. All grammar recognizable by the method of syntax diagrams following the procedure seen in the previous point is LL (1).
The method consists of following an algorithm starting from:
The string to recognize, along with a pointer, which tells us which is the token current pre-search; denoted by a said token .
A stack of symbols (terminals and non-terminals); we will denote by X the top from this stack.
A syntax check table uniquely associated with a grammar.In this text, the way in which this table is obtained from of grammar. We will denote by M this table, which will have a row by each non-terminal of the starting grammar, and one column for each terminal including EOF: M [N × Tc {$}].
As always, the input string will end in EOF which, from now on,
we will denote by the symbol '$'. The aforementioned algorithm consists of consulting repeatedly the M table until accepting or rejecting the sentence. Starting from a pile having the $ at its base and the initial axiom S of the grammar above, each step of query consists of following one of the following points (they are exclusive):
1.- If X = a = $ then ACCEPT.
2.- If X = a… $ then
X is removed from the stack- and the pointer is advanced.
67
80
Syntactic analysis
3.- If X 0 T and X… a then REJECT.
4.- If X 0 N then we consult the entry M [X, a] of the table, and:
If M [X, a] is empty: REJECT.
If M [X, a] is not empty, X is removed from the stack and theconsequent in reverse order. Example: If M [X, a] = {X 6 UVY}, X is removed from the stack, and UVY is put in reverse order, like show the figure 3 . 19 .
Figure 3.19. Rule application in the LL method (1)
As in top-down analysis with recursive functions, a
Once a rule has been applied, it will no longer be de-applied by any kind of backtracking.
One of the main drawbacks of this type of analysis is that the number
of grammars LL (1) is relatively small; However, when a grammar is not
is LL (1), it is usually possible to translate it to obtain an equivalent that is LL (1), after a proper study. For example, the following grammar is not LL (1):
E ÷ E + T | T
T ÷ T * F | F F ÷ (E) | go

3.5.4 Overview of bottom-up analysis
Before particularizing in the different types of bottom-up analysis, it is It is convenient to study the concepts and methodology common to all of them. The objective of bottom-up analysis consists of building the syntactic tree from the bottom above, that is, from the tokens to the initial axiom, which decreases the number of misapplied rules regarding the top-down case (if we talk about the case with backspace) or broadens the number of grammars that can be analyzed (if we speak of case LL (1)).
Whether there is backspace or not, at any given time, the input string It will be divided into two parts, called α and β:
β : represents the chunk of the input string (sequence of tokens ) by
* consume: β 0 T. It will always coincide with some piece of the right part of the input string. As can be assumed, initially β coincides with the string to be fully recognized (including the end EOF).

3.5.4.1 Operations in a bottom-up analyzer
As a parser builds the tree, it faces
to a different configuration (the α-β pair configuration is called) and must take a decision on the next step or operation to perform. Basically it has
four different operations, and each type of bottom-up parser is distinguished from the others based on intelligence on when to apply each of these operations. Any bottom-up analysis mechanism consists of starting from a configuration initial and gradually applying operations, each of which allows to go from one source configuration to another destination. The process will finish when the configuration destination becomes such that α represents the complete syntactic tree and β has consumed all tokens . The available operations are as follows:
1.- ACCEPT: the string is accepted: β / EOF and α / S (initial axiom).
2.- REJECT: the input string is not valid.
3.- Reduce : consists of applying a production rule backwards to some elements located at the extreme right of α. For example, if we have the setting:
​ α / [XX ... X one​ twop + 1​ p X​ ... X m ] -β / [a ... am + 1]              n
*
​ (remember that X 0 (NcT) ya 0 T), and there is a rule of the formi              i
​ A 6k p + 1X​ ... X m
then a reduction by this rule consists of going to the configuration:
​ α / [XX ... X one​ twok​ p TO] -β / [a ... am + 1].​ n
It is feasible to reduce by epsilon rules, that is, if we start from the
setting:
​ α / [XX ... XXone​ two​ p​ p + 1 ... X] -β / [a ... a]m              m + 1              n
can be reduced by a rule of the form:
A 6k g
obtaining:
​ α / [XX ... XXone​ two​ p​ p + 1 ... X km TO] -β / [a ... am + 1]              n
4.- Move : it only consists of removing the terminal furthest to the left of β and put it to the right of α. For example, if we have the configuration: 71

​ α / [XX ... XXone​ two​ p​ p + 1 ... X] -β / [aa ... a]m              m + 1 m + 2              n
an offset would become:
​ α / [XX ... XXone​ two​ p​ p + 1 ... X a] -β / [a ... a]m m + 1              m + 2              n
In short, through reductions and shifts, we have to arrive at
accept or reject the input string. Before making the displacements we have to make all the possible reductions to α, since these are made to the part right of α, and not in the middle. When α is the initial axiom and β is the null strip (only contains EOF), the input string is accepted. When β is not the null strip or α is not the initial axiom and no rule can be applied, then the chain of entry.
3.5.5 Upward analysis with retracement

test all possible operations (reductions and shifts) using a brute force method, until reaching the syntax tree, or exhausting all options, in which case the string is rejected. The algorithm is as follows:

Syntactic analysis
3.5.6 Bottom-up analysis of LR grammars (1)
In this section an efficient syntactic analysis technique will be introduced
ascendant that can be used to process a wide class of grammars from free context. The technique is called LR (k) parsing. The abbreviation LR
This is because the input string is examined from left to right (in English, Left-to-right ), while the "R" indicates that the process provides the syntactic tree by means of the sequence of derivations to the right (in English, Rightmost derivation ) in reverse order. Finally, the "k" refers to the number of pre-search tokens used to make decisions about whether to reduce or displace. When omitted, assume k, is 1.
LR analysis is attractive for several reasons.
Can recognize the vast majority of programming languages  that can be generated using context-free grammars.
The method of operation of these analyzers has the advantage oflocate a syntactic error almost at the same time it occurs with what is acquired great compile-time efficiency versus less adequate procedures such as backtracking. Further, error messages pinpoint the source of the error.
The main drawback of the method is that it involves too much work
manually build an LR parser for a language grammar
of typical programming, being necessary to use a specialized tool to it: an automatic generator of LR parsers. Fortunately, there are several of these generators available. Later we will study the design and use of some of them: the Yacc, Cup and JavaCC metacompilers. With Yacc or Cup you can write a free context grammar with its parser automatically generated
syntactic. If the grammar contains ambiguities or other difficult-to-understand constructions parse in a left-to-right scan of the input, the metacompiler can locate problematic production rules and report them to the designer.
This technique, like that of LL (1), bases its operation on the existence
of a special table uniquely associated with a grammar. There are several techniques to build said table, and each one of them produces a “substitute” for the method principal. Most authors focus on three main methods. The first of them, called Simple LR ( SLR , in English) is the easiest to apply, but the least powerful of the three as you may not be able to produce an analysis table syntactic for some grammars that other methods do. The second method,
called Canonical LR, it is the most powerful but the most expensive. The third method, called It lr with lookahead ( Look Ahead LR in English), it is between the other two in in terms of power and cost. The LALR method works with most grammars programming languages  and, with very little effort, can be implemented in efficient way. Basically one method is more powerful than another depending on the number of grammars to which it can be applied. The broader the spectrum of The more complex supported grammars the method becomes.
74
87
Functionally speaking, an LR analyzer consists of two parts well
differentiated: a) a process program and b) an analysis table. The program of The process has, as will be seen below, a very simple operation and
it remains unchanged from analyzer to analyzer. According to the grammar to be processed the content of the analysis table, which is the one that fully identifies to the analyzer.
The figure 3 .25 shows a synoptic diagram of the general structure of a
LR analyzer. As can be seen from it, the parser processes a string of input terminated with the $ symbol representing the EOF delimiter. This chain is reads from left to right, and single symbol recognition makes it possible to take timely decisions (LR (1)).

​ 3.5.6.1.1​ Right or left recursion
One of the most basic approaches is deciding whether to use
rules recursive left or recursive right. For example, to recognize a sequence of identifiers separated by commas like:
id, id, id
it is possible to choose between two different grammars:
to)​ â list ÷ ID to​ | list ',' ID
b)​ â list ÷ ID to​ | ID ',' list
Well, when it comes to recognizing a sentence we can
observe that very different trees are obtained, as in the figure 3. 26 by recognizing “go, Yo hice". In turn, if we apply an LR recognition, the sequence of steps applied differs radically: in case b) they move sequentially, one by one, all the chain tokens and, finally, several reductions are made in a row, depending on the chain length; in case a) several tokens (the comma and an ID) are moved and reduce by Ï, and so on, the displacements and reductions.
In this way, with recursion to the right, the stack α increases in size
until the reductions begin. Therefore, before lists of identifiers large enough, an overflow could occur. Most convenient, so it is to use left recursive grammars so that the stack size remains stable. In those cases where the recursion to the right is absolutely necessary (some of these cases will be presented to us in chapters
79
92
Syntactic analysis
subsequent) should avoid supplying excessively long chains that can produce overflows.
Figure 3.26. Recognition of the same sentence by equivalent grammars: a) recursive left, and b) recursive right
​ 3.5.6.1.2​ Conflicts
A conflict occurs when the parser is unable to generate the table
syntax check for a grammar. There are basically two types of conflicts:
Shift / Reduce ( Shift / Reduce ). They are usually due to ambiguous grammars.
Reduce / Reduce ( Reduce / Reduce ). Because the grammar does not admit a LR (k) analysis.
The Shift / Shrink conflict appears when it appears in the action table In the same box both an R to reduce and a D to displace, the conflict is that
the program does not know whether to reduce or displace. For example, such a simple grammar how:
​ EXPR​ ÷ EXPR '+' EXPR
Figure 3.27. Example of ambiguity
80
93
| num
it is obviously ambiguous (it is recursive on the right and on the left simultaneously), then before an entry as simple as "num + num + num" two different syntactic trees can be produced (see figure 3 .27 ).
On the other hand, the Reduce / Reduce conflict appears when the analyzer can
apply two or more rules to certain situations. This situation usually appears when the grammar is not LR (1), that is, a pre- token is not enough search to make the decision of what action to take. For example, suppose the grammar:
Î S ÷ aaBdd Ï | aCd
Ð B ÷ a
Ñ  C ÷ yy
which only recognizes the two sequence of tokens "aaadd" and "aaad". Well, a so simple grammar is not LR (1) (see figure 3 . 28 ). Suppose that in this figure
you want to recognize the sequence "aaad"; in this case the correct thing would be to reduce by Ñ. Without However, it all depends on whether after the pre-search token (a “d”), another “d” comes or wear EOF. An LR (2) parser would be able to make the right decision, but not one LR (1).
Figure 3.28. Example of Reduce / Reduce conflict. The interrogations indicate that the analyzer does not know what comes next
Reduce / Reduce conflicts can be eliminated in most cases.
This is achieved by delaying the reduction decision at the last minute. For example, the grammar:
S ÷ aaadd | aaad
is equivalent to the previous one, but it is LR (1), since it is only decided to reduce before the EOF from the end, at which point it will be in the correct state based on the tokens that have been read in full.
3.5.6.2 Conclusions on the LR analysis (1)
In the heading 3.5.6 how LR analyzers work was studied using
the use of their corresponding analysis tables. In the final example you can be observed as the state that is always at the top of the stack contains in

Syntactic analysis
at all times the information necessary for the reduction, if this proceeds. Further, we have intentionally left out the structure of the process program since it is essentially a finite automaton with a stack.
In general, it can be stated that, given the structure of LR analyzers, with
the single inspection of k symbols in the input string to the right of the last consumed, it is possible to decide exactly what movement to perform (reduction, displacement, etc). It is for this reason that it is usually called such grammars as LR (k). As already mentioned, in practice almost all Programming languages  can be analyzed using LR grammars (1).
The LR (1) tables devised by Knuth in 1965 are too large for
grammars of traditional programming languages. In 1969 De Remer and Korenjack discovered ways to compact these boards, making them practical and manageable this type of analyzers. The algorithm applied to these tables is the same already seen. We have also seen that there are three main types of grammars LR (k) that vary depending on their power being included in each other of the following form:
SLR (k) d LALR (k) d LR (k)
but not their respective languages  that match in their sets, that is, if there is a grammar LR (1) that recognizes the language L, then there is also a grammar SLR (1) which recognizes L.
The Yacc and Cup metacompilers use LALR analysis (1). The automaton
must keep information about its configuration (α stack), and to keep
information about β is communicated with Lex, who takes care of the meta-compilation to lexical level.

​ 4.1​ Semantic analysis
In addition to controlling that a program complies with the rules of grammar
language, you have to check that what you want to do makes sense. The analysis semantic gives a coherent meaning to what we have done in the analysis syntactic. The semantic check ensures that the types that intervene in the expressions are compatible or that the actual parameters of a function are
consistent with the formal parameters: for example, it does not usually make much sense to multiply a string of characters by an integer.
We will start by looking at a simple example in which the concept is introduced
attribute by constructing the interpreter of a calculator.
4.1.1 Attributes and semantic actions
An example is developed below to have a global vision of this
theme. Suppose you want to build a small calculator that performs the + and * operations. The grammar will be as follows: E ÷ E + T | T

4.1.2 Execution of a semantic action
There are two ways to associate semantic rules with production rules:
Syntax-directed definition : consists of associating a semantic action to a production rule, but that association does not indicate when it should be execute said semantic action. It is assumed that in a first phase builds the complete syntax tree and then executes the semantic actions in a sequence that allows the calculation of all the attributes of the tree nodes.
Translation scheme : is the same as a syntax-driven definition except that additionally, information is assumed or provided about when semantic actions must be executed. What's more, it is also possible insert actions between the symbols of the consequent of a rule of
86
99
production, e.g .: A ÷ cd {A = c + d} B. In the LR case the shares are run when reductions are made. In the case with functions recursive actions are interspersed with the code intended to recognize each BNF expression.
As long as we have a translation scheme with embedded actions
it can be passed to its equivalent syntax-driven definition. For example, him scheme:
​ A ÷ cd {action} B​ one
can become
​ A ÷​ cd NB one
​ N ÷one​ g {action}​ one
which is equivalent. Basically, the transformation consists of introducing a no dummy terminal (N in the example) that replaces each intermediate semantic actionone (action), and create a new production rule of type epsilon with said non-terminalone as antecedent and with said semantic action as action.
At first glance, this transformation may appear to be troublesome;

​ N ÷ g {printf ("% d \ n", c + d); one​ }
a semantic action appears that refers to the attributes of c and d associated with a rule in which c and d do not intervene. However, since there is only one rule in which N appears in the antecedent and also only one rule in which N appearsone              one in the consequent, it is clear that whenever an analyzer tries to reduce to N or              one invoke the recursive function that implements N, the last two tokensone that have been consumed will be c and d, so the semantic action can be executed working on the attributes of these last two tokens , although apparently there seems to be something wrong when you glance lightly at the definition Syntax-driven obtained after transformation of the translation scheme.
When working with production rules (not recursive functions),
we may run into some other problem. For example, if you have:
A ÷ cd {A = c + d} B
and it becomes:

​ 4.2​ Syntax-driven translation
From this section the translation of languages  guided by
free context grammars. Information is associated with each construction of the language programming by providing attributes to grammar symbols that
represent that construction. Attribute values  are calculated using rules Semantic (actions) associated with grammatical production rules.
As we have seen, there are two notations for associating semantic rules with
production rules:
Syntax-driven definitions are high-level specifications fortranslations. The user does not need to explicitly specify the order in which the execution of the actions takes place.
The translation schemes indicate the order in which thesemantic rules.
Conceptually, both with syntax-driven definitions and with
the translation schemes, the component chain is parsed input lexicons, the parse tree is built, and then the tree to evaluate the semantic rules at its nodes. The evaluation of the rules
88
101
semantics can generate code, save information in a symbol table, emit error messages or perform other activities, such as evaluating expressions arithmetic on a calculator. The translation of the lexical component string is the result obtained when evaluating the semantic rules.
In practice, not all implementations have to follow the bottom line.
letter this very clear scheme of one phase after another. There are special cases of Syntax-driven definitions that can be implemented in a single pass evaluating semantic rules during parsing, without constructing explicitly a parse tree or graph showing the dependencies between attributes.
4.2.1 Syntax-driven definition
A syntax-driven definition is a context-free grammar in the
that each grammar symbol (terminal and nonterminal) has a set of attributes associated, divided into two subsets called synthesized attributes and attributes inherited from said grammar symbol. If each node of a tree is considered syntactic has associated a record with fields to store information, then an attribute corresponds to the name of a field.
An attribute can store anything: a string, a number, a type,
a memory location, etc. The value of an attribute at a node in a syntactic tree is defined by a semantic rule associated with the production rule used for get said node. The value of a synthesized attribute of a node is calculated from of the attribute values  of its child nodes in the syntactic tree; the value of a inherited attribute is calculated from the values  of the attributes in the nodes brothers and father. Each node in the tree represents an instance of a symbol grammatical and therefore has its own values  for each attribute.
The semantic rules establish the dependencies between the attributes;
dependencies that can be represented by a graph, once the tree is built full syntactic. An evaluation order is obtained from the dependency graph of all semantic rules. The evaluation of semantic rules defines the Attribute values  in the parse tree nodes for the string of entry. A semantic rule can also have side effects, such as
Example display a value or update a global variable. As stated
previously, in practice a translator does not need to explicitly build a tree parsing or a dependency graph; just produce the same result for each input string.
A syntactic tree showing the attribute values  at each node is
names a decorated or annotated syntactic tree . The process of calculating
Attribute values  in nodes is called annotating or decorating the syntax tree .

4.2.2.1 Synthesized attributes
Synthesized attributes are widely used in practice. A definition
syntax-driven that uses exclusively synthesized attributes is called
definition with synthesized attributes . With a definition with synthesized attributes you can decorate a syntactic tree by evaluating the semantic rules ascendingly, calculating the attributes from the leaf nodes to the root.

a calculator that reads an input line containing an arithmetic expression that

If an attribute b in a node of a syntactic tree depends on an attribute c ,
then the semantic rule for b at that node must be evaluated after the rule semantics that define c . The interdependencies between the attributes in the nodes of a Syntactic tree can be represented by a directed graph called a dependencies .
Before constructing the dependency graph for a syntactic tree, write
each semantic rule in the form b: = f (c, c, ..., c), introducing a false attributeone              two              k synthesized b for each semantic rule consisting of a procedure call. He graph has a node for each attribute of a tree node and an edge from the node c to b if attribute b depends on attribute c . Algorithmically, the graph of dependencies for a given parse tree is constructed from the Following way:
For each node n in the parse tree
For each attribute a of the grammar symbol at node n

106
Attributed grammars
construct a node in the dependency graph for a ;
For each node n in the parse tree
​ For each semantic rule b: = f (c, c, ..., c) associated with productionone              two              k
used in n
For i : = 1 to k
​ place an arc from node c to node b​ i
For example, suppose that Aa: = f (Xx, Yy) is a semantic rule for the
production A ÷ X Y. This rule defines a synthesized attribute Aa that depends on the attributes Xx and Yy If this production is used in the parse tree,
then there will be three nodes, Aa , Xx and Yy , in the dependency graph with an arc from Xx to Aa since Aa depends on Xx and another arc from Yy to Aa since Aa also depends on Yy .
If the production rule A ÷ XY had associated the semantic rule Xi: =
g (Aa, Yy), then there would be an arc from Aa to Xi and another arc from Yy to Xi , since Xi depends on both Aa and Yy

​ A topological ordering of an acyclic directed graph is every sequence m,              one
​ m, ..., m of the nodes of the graph such that the arcs go from the nodes that appeartwo              k
​ first in the order to those who appear later; that is, if m ÷ m is an arc              i              j
​ since mom, then me appears before me in the ordination.i              j              i              j
Every topological ordering of a dependency graph gives a valid order
in which the semantic rules associated with the nodes of a tree can be evaluated
​ syntactic. That is, in topological ordering, the dependent attributes c, c, ...,              one              two
c in a semantic rule b: = f (c, c, ..., c) are available at a node beforek              one              two              k be evaluated f.
The translation specified by a syntax-driven definition can be
specify as follows:
1.- The underlying grammar is used to build a syntactic tree for the entrance.
2.- The dependency graph is constructed as indicated above. 3.- From a topological ordering of the dependency graph, gets an order of evaluation for the semantic rules. The evaluation of the semantic rules in this order produces the translation of the chain of 95
108
Attributed grammars
input and / or triggers its interpretation.

in the symbol table entry for each identifier.
Finally, an attributed grammar is said to be circular if the graph of Dependencies for some syntactic tree generated by the grammar has a loop. By example, if a rule like:

it is possible to find an order of evaluation. In any other situation there is, minus, a topological ordering.

4.2.3 Translation schemes
A translation scheme is a free context grammar in which
find interspersed, on the right side of the production rule, fragments of programs that we have called semantic actions. A translation scheme is like a syntax-driven definition, with the exception that the order of evaluation of semantic rules is explicitly shown. The position in which some action is executed is given in braces and is written on the right side of the rule of production. For example:
98
111
A ÷ cd {printf (c + d)} B
When designing a translation scheme, you must respect some
limitations to ensure that the value of an attribute is available when a action refers to him. These limitations, derived from the type of parsing chosen to build the tree, they ensure that the actions do not refer to a attribute that has not yet been calculated.
The simplest example occurs when only synthesized attributes are needed.
In this case, you can build the translation scheme by creating an action that consist of an assignment for each semantic rule and placing this action at the end of the right side of the associated production rule. For example, the production rule T ÷ T * F and the semantic action T.val: = T .val * F.val result in theone              one following production and semantic action:
​ T ÷ T * F {T.val: = T .val * F.val}one​ one
If you have both inherited and synthesized attributes, you should be more
prudent, so there are some rules to be followed when using the attributes of grammar symbols:
1.- An inherited attribute for a symbol on the right side of a rule of Production must be calculated in an action before that symbol.
2.- An action must not refer to a synthesized attribute of a symbol that be to the right of the action.
3.- A synthesized attribute for the non-terminal on the left can only be calculate after all the attributes it does have been calculated reference. The action that calculates these attributes must be placed, usually at the end of the right side of the production.
Well-defined translation schemes that meet these constraints
they are said to be well defined . Example:
D ÷ T {L.her = T.type} L;
T ÷ int {T.tipo = "integer"}
T ÷ real {T.type = “real”}
L ÷ {L .her = L.her} L, id {add type (id.ptr_tbs, L.her)}one              one L ÷ id {add type (id.ptr_tbs, L.her)}
4.2.4 LALR analysis with attributes
In this section we will study how the LALR (1) analysis with attributes works. Let us remember that the LALR (1) is capable of knowing at all times which rule to apply without margin of error (before a grammar that meets the LALR (1) constraints). East
The fact is what is used to carry out actions safely. In the
In case of backtracking, it would be possible that after executing an action it is necessary to go back because we have realized that this rule was not correct. In such case, how undo an action ?. As this is impossible, in a backtracking analysis it is not
99
112
Attributed grammars
possible to execute actions at the same time as the analysis is done, but it would be necessary to first the sentence, and at a later stage execute the actions safely.
4.2.4.1 Reduce / Reduce Conflicts
There are translation schemes that cannot be recognized by parsers LALR (1) directly. In particular, semantic actions are usually executed only when making a reduction, and never when moving. Therefore, a translation scheme with interspersed actions becomes another equivalent that it only contains actions at the end of the rule, as illustrated in the section 4.1.2 . TO
Despite this, this transformation can lead to situations that cease to be LALR (1), as illustrated in figure 4 .10 .
Figure 4.10. Grammar example that is not LALR (1) after having been transformed with epsilon rules.
In this case there is a reduce / reduce conflict, because the grammar does not
is LALR. As already indicated, a reduce / reduce conflict occurs when it arrives a moment of the analysis in which it is not known which rule to apply. In the above case, if the string "abcd" is entered, when the first two tokens have been consumed (“A” and “b”) we cannot know which rule g to apply, if U or V, since the pre- token search is "c" and this happens to both U and V. To solve this problem
We would need a LALR (3) analysis, since it is not enough to see the “c”; neither it is enough to see the "d"; it is necessary to go one character further, to the "e" or the EOF to know by which rule to reduce and, therefore, which action to apply.
4.2.4.2 Move / reduce conflicts
There are more confusing cases, which occur in grammars that are ambiguous, that is,
those in which a sentence can have more than one syntactic tree. Such is the case of a grammar as simple as:
S ÷ aBadd S ÷ aCd
÷ a
÷ aa
When reading the string "aaad", when the first two "a" and the token are consumed
pre-search is the third “a”, it is not known whether to reduce the last “a” consumed to B or move to reduce next to C. It all depends on how many "d" you end with the chain which is still unknown.
100
113
Figure 4.11. Ambiguity in two nested ifs in C: case a) else associated with the external if case b) else associated with the inner if
An example of a grammar to which something similar happens is the
corresponding to the recognition of nested if statements in the C language.
grammar is:
S ÷ if COND then S
S ÷ if COND then S else S S ÷ id = EXPR
in which displace / reduce conflicts occur before an input such as:
​ if COND then ifone​ COND then S elsetwo S​ one              two
as illustrated in the figure 4 . 11 .
When a C compiler encounters a statement like this it opts for
construct the second syntactic tree following the general rule “match each else with the closest previous unpaired then ”. This rule to remove ambiguities can be incorporated directly into the grammar by adding some additional production rules:
​ S​ ÷ S Complete
​ |​ SIncomplete
​ SComplete​ ÷​ if COND then Scomplete else Scomplete
101
114
Attributed grammars
​ |​ id = EXPR
​ SIncomplete​ ÷ if COND then S
​ |​ if COND then S Complete else S Incomplete
that always associate the else to the most immediate if. So a complete SC is either a if-then-else statement that does not contain incomplete statements or any other non-conditional statement class (such as an assignment). That is to say, inside an if with else only complete ifs are allowed, which makes all else's are grouped together before we start to recognize if's without else.
​ 4.3​ The PCYacc parser generator
PCYacc is a metacompiler that accepts as input a grammar of
free context, and outputs the finite automaton that recognizes the language generated by said grammar. Although PCYacc does not accept all grammars but only LALR ( LookAhead LR ), most programming languages  are they can express using such a grammar. It also allows associating actions to each production rule and assign an attribute to each grammar symbol
(terminal or non-terminal). This makes it easy for the developer to create schematics translation into syntax-driven recognition. Since the automaton that generates PCYacc is written in C language, all semantic actions and
Necessary declarations must be written by the developer in this same language.
PCYacc starts from terminal symbols ( tokens ), which must be generated
by a lexicographic analyzer that can be implemented through PCLex, or ad hoc following some conventions imposed by PCYacc.
4.3.1 Format of a Yacc program
The language in which the input programs to PCYacc must be written
we will call Yacc. Thus, a source program in Yacc has the following syntax:
Definitions area
%%
Rules area
%%
Function area
To illustrate a source program in YACC, we will start from the following
grammar that allows you to recognize arithmetic expressions:
E ÷ E + T | T
T ÷ T * F | F
F ÷ M (E) | M id | M num M ÷ - | g
Each of these areas will be progressively described below.
4.3.1.1 Definitions area
102
115
This area is made up of two optional sections. The first of them
is delimited by the symbols % { and %} and allows encoding ordinary declarations in C. Everything that is declared here will be visible in the rules and functions areas, for which is usually used to create the symbol table, or to include it ( #include ). The
% { and %} symbols must necessarily start in column 0 (just like all the clauses that will be seen below)
The second section allows you to declare the lexical components ( tokens ) used
in grammar. For this, the clause % token is used followed by a list of
terminals (without separating them by commas); following the example of point4.3.1 should to declare:
% token ID NUM
Also, for the sake of readability, as many clauses can be used
% token as needed; By convention, tokens are written in uppercase.
Internally, PCYacc encodes each token as an integer,
starting from 257 (since 0 to 255 are used to represent the characters
ASCII, and 256 represents the error token ). Therefore the clause
% token ID NUM
It is equivalent to
​ # define ID no.​ one
​ # define NUM no.​ two
Obviously, the numbers or codes assigned by PCYacc to each token are always different.
Notwithstanding the foregoing, not all terminals have to be listed in
clauses % token . Specifically, terminals can be expressed in two ways:
If its associated lexeme consists of a single character, and the parserlexicon returns it as is (its ASCII code), then they can be indicated directly in a production rule without declaring it in a clause % token . Example: '+'.
Any other terminal must be declared in the declarations part, sothat the rules will refer to them based on their name (not code, which it's just an internal representation).
Finally, in this area the initial axiom of the
grammar of the form:
% start noTerminal
If this clause is omitted, PCYacc considers as the initial axiom the antecedent of the first grammar rule to appear in the rules area.

4.3.1.4 Function area
The third section of a specification in Yacc consists of supporting routines
written in C. Here a lexical analyzer must be provided using a function called yylex () . If necessary, other procedures can be added, such as support routines or compilation summary.
The lexical analyzer yylex () produces pairs formed by a token and its value
associated attribute. If a token is returned such as NUM, or ID, said
token must be declared in the first section of the specification in Yacc, The value of the attribute associated with a token is communicated to the parser through the variable yylval , as will be seen in point 4.3.2 . In case of integrating Yacc with Lex, in this section will have to make an #include of the file generated by PCLex.
Specifically, this section must specify at least the functions:
main () , which should start the parser by calling yyparse () .
void yyerror (char * s) that receives a string that describes the error. In this moment the variable yychar contains the terminal that has been received and that Was expected.
4.3.2 Attribute management
As already indicated, a rule can include semantic actions,
which are nothing more than a sequence of local declarations and statements in C language. But the true power of actions in Yacc is that each terminal or non-terminal an attribute can be associated with it. The attribute names are always the same and depend on the position that the symbol occupies within the ruler and not on the symbol in yes; in this way the name $$ refers to the attribute of the antecedent of the rule, while $ i refers to the attribute associated with the i-th grammar symbol of the consequent, that is:
The antecedent attribute is $$ .
er
The attribute of the 1 symbol of the consequent is $ 1 .
The attribute of the 2nd symbol of the consequent is $ 2 .
Etc.
The semantic action will be executed as long as it is reduced by the rule of
associated production, so normally it is in charge of calculating a value for
106
119
$$ based on the $ i. For example, if we assume that the attribute of all symbols of our grammar (terminals and non-terminals) is of integer type, it would have:
%%
Øe: e '+' t
{$$ = $ 1 + $ 3;}
Ù | t

Út: t '*' f

The answer is that the lexical analyzer is the one who has to take care of assign a value to the attributes of the terminals. Remember that in the section 2.4.3.6 it was studied that PCLex provided the global variable yylval that allowed associate additional information with a token . Well, by means of this variable the
The lexical analyzer can communicate attributes to the syntactic. In this way, when the The lexical analyzer receives a string like "27 + 13 + 25", it should not only generate the token sequence NUM '+' NUM '+' NUM, it should also load the value
of the attribute of each NUM, and must do so before returning the token (before doing return NUM; ). In our case we will associate to each NUM the integer value that represents its lexeme, with the objective that the semantic actions of the syntactic can operate with it. The Lex pattern and the associated lexical action are: [0-9] + {yylval = atoi (yytext); return NUM; }
The atoi () function converts from text to integer. Note that whenever
invoke atoi () , the conversion will be successful, since the lexeme stored in yytext it cannot contain more than digits thanks to the associated pattern. Furthermore, it is assumed that the variable yylval is also integer.
PCYacc needs some additional information in order to perform a
correct attribute management, that is, we have to inform you of the type of attribute that has associated each symbol of the grammar, whether terminal or non-terminal. For it The macro YYSTYPE must be defined that indicates what type all the attributes are; in other words, PCYacc assumes that all attributes are of type YYSTYPE , so that you have to give this macro a value. In this way, if we want all attributes are of type int , it would be enough to write
% {
#define YYSTYPE int
107
120
Attributed grammars
%}
as a global declarations section in the definitions area. In fact, PCYacc forces a declaration like this to be included in Yacc programs even if attributes are used for nothing: PCYacc requires associating attributes always and know your type.
However, it is clear that not all symbols in a grammar
will need attributes of the same type, being very common that numbers have associated an integer attribute, and that the identifiers have a pointer to a table of symbols that store important information about it. In this case, it can be declared
YYSTYPE as a union of C so that, although all symbols in the grammar will have as an attribute said union , each one will use only the field that specify. Although this method may be a bit wasted on memory, it allows for a Consistent management of the parsing stack, leading to greater efficiency.
Furthermore, the mechanism of using a union as type of YYSTYPE is such a practice It is common for PCYacc to have a clause that allows defining YYSTYPE and declaring the union in one step. The clause has the form:
%Union {
/ * Body of a C union * /
}
Remember that a union in C allows you to define records with a variant part. Moreover, in a union everything is part variant, that is, all the fields declared in the body of a union share the same memory space, so that the union size is the largest field it contains. Continuing with our For example, the union would have the form:
%Union {
int number; node * ptrNode;
}
With this, YYSTYPE has become a union (no longer required
include the #define YYSTYPE), so any symbol can do
reference to the number field or the ptrNode field , but not both. As a rule, each grammar symbol, regardless of the rule in which it appears uses always the same field of % union ; therefore, PCYacc supplies a mechanism to indicate which of the fields is the one to be used for each symbol. With this in addition, we save continuous references to the % union fields . The mechanism consists of including angle brackets in the% token clause of the definitions the field to be used for each terminal of the grammar; for
indicate the type of the non-terminal attributes, the % type clause will be used which, otherwise, it is equal to the % token except that it lists non-terminals instead of terminals. Following this notation, our example would be:

4.3.2.1 Intermediate actions
It is also possible to place actions in the middle of a rule, that is, between the
symbols of a consequent. However, it was previously said that PCYacc builds parsers that execute a semantic action every time it is reduced by its rule associated in the construction of a syntactic tree. So when does a intermediate action? In these cases, PCYacc performs a grammar conversion provides another equivalent in which the intermediate action appears to the right of a new rule of the form: N ÷ g, where i is an arbitrary number generated byi
PCYacc. For example,
A: B {$$ = 1; } C {x = $ 2; y = $ 3; };
becomes
Ni: / * Epsilon * / {$$ = 1; };
​ A: B Ni C​ {x = $ 2; y = $ 3;};
The execution of semantic actions is subject to the mechanism seen in the
point 4.1.2.
4.3.3 Ambiguity
PCYacc not only allows you to easily express a grammar to be recognized and
generate your parser, but also provides tools that facilitate elimination of ambiguities.
The grammar proposed in the section 4.3.1 is a device to force
product and division have higher priority than addition and subtraction, and the least unary is the highest priority operator. However, from the point of view
exclusively syntactic, the language generated by said grammar is much more easy to express using grammar
​ and​ :​ e '+' e
​ |​ e '-' e
​ |​ e '*' e
​ |​ e '/' e
​ |​ '-' e
​ |​ '(' e ')'
​ |​ ID
​ |​ NUM
;
which, as is evident, is ambiguous. For example, before the entry "27 * 13 + 25" can get the syntactic trees of figure 4 . 13 ; in case a) first it is multiplies and then adds, while in b) it first adds and then
multiply. Since the product has priority over the sum, only case a) is valid from the semantic point of view, otherwise a result is obtained
110
123
incorrect.
Figure 4.13. Syntactic trees that recognize a valid sentence according to a ambiguous grammar. Note how semantically results are produced different according to the order of evaluation. The correct option is a), since the product takes precedence over sum.
​ This situation occurs because when the parser has expr.              27
'*' expr. and encounters the '+', a shift / shrink conflict occurs: if10 displaces you get tree b), and if you reduce it you get tree a). This same problem would have occurred if you had two sums, instead of a product and a sum; analogously, a tree like a) would have been the correct one, since the sum is associative on the left. In general, if a rule is left recursive
implies that the containing operator is left associative; and the same for the right. That is why the proposed grammar is ambiguous, since each rule is simultaneously recursive to the right and to the left, which implies that the operators are left and right associative at the same time, which does not have sense. Although most operators are left associative, also
there is on the right, as is the case with the exponentiation operator. What's more, some operators are not even associative such as modulo operator (remainder) of a division.
Although we have already seen that this problem can be solved by changing the
grammar, PCYacc provides a mechanism to resolve ambiguities caused by displace / reduce conflicts without the need to introduce changes in the proposed grammar. Unless instructed otherwise, PCYacc will resolve all conflicts in the parser action table using both following premises:
A reduce / reduce conflict is resolved by choosing the conflicting rulethat was written first in the Yacc program.
A shift / shrink conflict is resolved in favor of shift.
In addition to these premises, PCYacc provides the developer with the possibility
solving grammar conflicts by yourself. The solution comes from the hand of the precedence and associativity clauses: % left , % right , % nonassoc and % prec . 111
124
Attributed grammars
The clauses % left , % right and % nonassoc allow to indicate the associativity of a operator ( left -left: shrink, right -right: move and nonassoc -not associative: error); Furthermore, the order in which the clauses are specified allows PCYacc to know which operators have higher priority over others. These clauses are placed in the definitions area, usually after the list of terminals. For example, clause % left '-'
makes the rule e: e '-' e unambiguous. Note that for PCYacc there is no concept of operator (arithmetic or not), but in these clauses any symbol where a shift / shrink conflict occurs, whether terminal or not terminal.
If multiple symbols are written to the same % left , % right, or % nonassoc
we will be indicating that everyone has the same priority. A specification of the shape:
% left '-' '+' % left '*' '/'
indicates that '+' and '-' have the same priority and are left associative and than '*' and '/' too. Since '*' and '/' come after '-' and '+', this informs PCYacc that '*' and '/' have higher priority than '+' and '-'.
There are situations in which the same terminal can represent several
operations, as in the case of the sign '-' which can be the symbol of the subtraction or minus unary. Since it only makes sense for the '-' to appear in a single clause it is impossible to indicate that, when it is the least binary, the priority is lower than that of the product, while if it is unary the priority is higher. To solve this situation, PCYacc provides the % prec modifier which, placed next to the consequent of a rule, modifies the priority and associativity of the same. % prec must be accompanied by an operator previously listed in any of the clauses
% left , % right, or % nonassoc , from which it inherits precedence and priority. In our example case could be written:
% left '+' '-'
% left '*' '/'
%%
​ and​ :​ e '+' e
​ |​ e '-' e
​ |​ e '*' e
​ |​ e '/' e
​ |​ '-' e​ % prec '*'
​ |​ '(' e ')'
​ |​ ID
​ |​ NUM
;
indicating in this way that the unary minus rule has as much priority as the of the product and that, in case of conflict, is also associative on the left. Not
112
125
However, this solution presents two problems: 1) the priority of the minus unary is not equal to the product, if not superior; and 2) the unary minus is not associative by the left, but on the right (although this would not give problems, since there is no ambiguity in the formation of the tree associated with a sentence such as “--- 5”, just as illustrates figure 4 .14 ).
Figure 4.14. Although the grammar is ambiguous, if there are several "minus unary" operators in a row, these can only be associated to the right.
To solve these problems, one can resort to a device that consists of
declare a terminal representing a dummy operator of the priority and associativity required with the sole objective of using it later in a % prec .
This new token will never be returned by the lexicographic analyzer. In addition, terminals listed in a % left , % right or % nonassoc statement is not necessary declare them also in the corresponding % token , unless they have an attribute of the % union . Thus, the final solution to the problem of priority and associativity is given by the Yacc program block:
% left '+' '-'
% left '*' '/'
% right LESS_UNARY
%%
​ and​ :​ e '+' e
​ |​ e '-' e
​ |​ e '*' e
​ |​ e '/' e
​ |​ '-' e​ % prec MINUS_UNARY
​ |​ '(' e ')'
​ |​ ID
​ |​ NUM
;
4.3.4 Error handling
Perhaps one of the biggest shortcomings of PCYacc is that of
error handling since, by default, when the parser that generates encounters a syntactic error, it simply stops execution with a sparse " syntax error " message .
113
126
Attributed grammars
This execution break can be avoided through the correct use of the token
special mistake . Basically, if a syntactic error occurs the parser generated by PCYacc discards part of the analysis stack and part of the input that remains to be read, but only until a situation is reached where the error can be recovered, which it is achieved through the reserved token error . A typical example of token usage error is as follows:
​ prog​ :​ / * Epsilon * /
​ |​ prog sent ';'
​ |​ prog error ';'
;
​ sent​ :​ ID '=' NUM
;
The last rule of this piece of grammar is to tell PCYacc that
between the non-terminal prog and the terminal ';' a syntactic error may appear. Suppose we have the sentence to recognize “a = 6; b = 7; c = 8; d = 6; " in the that a point has been "slipped" after the identifier c ; when the analyzer is the token '.' realizes there is a syntactic error and searches the grammar for all rules that contain the error symbol ; then it removes symbols from the the top of the stack, until the symbols above the stack match the located to the left of the error symbol in any of the rules in which it appears.
It then removes tokens from the input until it finds one that
Figure 4.15. Recovering from a syntactic error. "Recover" does not want say "correct" but be able to continue parsing
114
127
matches the one just to the right in the selected error rule. A
Once done, insert the error token on the stack, scroll and continue with the analysis trying to reduce by that error rule. To the terminal just to the right of error in an error rule is called a security token , since it allows the analyzer to reach a safe condition in the analysis. As a security token usually choose the termination of sentence or declaration, as in our case, in
the one that we have chosen the semicolon. The figure4 .15 graphically illustrates the process followed for error recovery.
Finally, for everything to work properly it is convenient
associate the invocation of a special PCYacc macro with the error rule named yyerrok :
prog
:
/ * Epsilon * /
|
prog sent ';'
|
;
prog error ';'
{yyerrok; }
sent
:
ID '=' NUM
;
Running this macro informs the parser that the
recovery was successful. If not invoked, the generated parser
by PCYacc enters a state called "security condition", and will remain in that been up to having shifted three tokens . The safety condition prevents parser to emit more error messages (before new syntactic errors), and has for Aim to avoid cascading errors due to improper error recovery. The invocation of yyerrok takes the parser out of the safety condition, so that This will inform us of all, absolutely all, the syntactic errors, even the due to incorrect error recovery.
Despite being able to recover syntactic errors, PCYacc does not generate
analyzers that correctly report the error, but are only limited to
report the existence of these but without specifying their nature. This lack can be correct by following the steps below:
In the Yacc functions area, place the directive: #include "errorlib.c"
and not include the yyerror (char * s) function , which is already hard-coded inside from the errorlib.c file .
Compile the YACC program as:
pcyacc -d -pyaccpar.c file.yac
-d creates the file yytab.h that has a description of the stack of the parser and the code associated with each token .
-pyaccpar.c causes a skeleton to be used to contain the finite stack automaton that implements PCYacc. Instead of taking the default skeleton, is taken to yaccpar.c which contains the 115
128
Attributed grammars
new error handling functions. yaccpar.c makes use of its once, from the errorlib.h file .
Run the programtokens.com
This program takes the file yytab.h , and generates the file yytok.h that is nothing more than the sequence of token names quoted and separated by commas. This file is used by errorlib.c to indicate what tokens were expected.
Compile file.c (for example, with Turbo C):
tc file.c
In this way, when file.exe is executed and an error occurs
syntactic, you will get a clarifying error message:
"Error in line ...: found the token ... and expected one of the tokens ..." where the ellipsis are automatically filled in by the analyzer
syntax with the line of the error, the wrong token that caused it and the list of tokens that would have been valid, respectively.

​ 4.4​ The Cup parser generator
Cup is a LALR parser developed at the Institute of
Georgia (USA) technology that generates Java code and allows you to enter
semantic actions written in that language. Use a fairly similar notation to that of PCYacc and also based on production rules.
4.4.1 Main differences
By way of introduction, the main differences with PCYacc are:
The antecedent of a rule is separated from the consequent by :: =, insteadby:.
It is not possible to use simple characters (´ + ´, ´-´, ´, ´etc.) As terminals ina production rule, so all terminals must be declared explicitly: PLUS, MINUS, COMMA, etc.
Instead of % token use terminal.
Instead of % type use non terminal.
The list of terminals must be separated with commas and end in a semicolon,like that of non-terminals. In both lists all the terminals and non-terminals, whether they have an attribute or not.
If a terminal or non-terminal has an associated attribute, it cannot beprimitive type but, necessarily, it must be an object ( Object ). By
Therefore, instead of using int you should use Integer , instead of char you should use Character , etc.
There is no % union , but the type of each terminal is directly indicated and non-terminal using the name of the corresponding class: Integer ,
Character , MyRecord , etc. The use of brackets is also not necessary angular to indicate this type.
In intermediate semantic actions, the attributes of the consequent thatpreceding the action can be used in read-only mode (as if they were from the final Java type ).
Instead of % left , % right and % nonassoc are used, respectively precedence left, precedence right, and precedence nonassoc. However, the clause % prec is used the same as in PCYacc.
Instead of % start , start with is used .
There are no sections that should be separated by the %% symbols.
You can use, as if it were a Java program, the clausesimport and package .
117
130
Attributed grammars
Before starting the execution of the generated analyzer, aJava code block using clause: init with {: Java_code_block:};
To connect the generated analyze with the lexical analyzer, usethe clause: scan with {: java_block_quereturns_an_object_of_the_class_sym:};
