4.4.2 Complete syntax.
A Cup file is made up of five main blocks that we will see at
continuation.
4.4.2.1 Package specification and imports.
Under this first block the classes that are needed are optionally indicated
to import. You can also indicate the package to which you want to make the generated classes.
4.4.2.2 User code.
Cup encompasses a separate non-public class (included within the file
parser.java ) to all user-specified semantic actions. If he developer may want to include a java block inside this class using the statement:
action code {: java_block:}
Variables, functions, etc. can be declared in this block. all of type
120
133
static as there are no accessible objects by which to reference components not static. Everything that is declared here will be accessible to semantic actions.
It is also possible to make Java declarations within the class itself
parser , by declaring:
parser code {: java_block:}
which is very useful to include the main () method that starts our application.
The following two statements serve to communicate with the
lexical analyzer. The first is:
init with {: java_block:}
and executes the indicated code just before making the request for the first token ; he The objective may be to open a file, initialize storage structures, etc.
Finally, Cup can communicate with any lexical analyzer, since
the declaration:
scan with {: java_block:}
lets you specify the code block that returns the next token on input.
4.4.2.3 Symbol lists
This section lists all the terminals and non-terminals of the
grammar. You can have multiple lists of terminals and non-terminals, as long as these are not repeated. Each terminal list is preceded by the word terminal , and each list of non-terminals is preceded by the words non terminal . The elements Both lists must be separated by commas, ending with a semicolon.
With these lists it is also possible to specify the attribute of each symbol, since
terminal or not, knowing that these must necessarily inherit from Object , that is, they cannot be primitive values. To do this, symbols whose attribute type is matching can be grouped in the same list to which said right type will be associated behind the word terminal , of the form:
terminal ClassName terminal1, terminal2, etc .;
O well
non terminal ClassName noTerminal1, noTerminal2, etc .;
4.4.2.4 Associativity and precedence.
This section has the same semantics as its counterpart in PCYacc, that is,
allows to resolve conflicts move / reduce before certain terminals. This way:
precedence left terminal1, terminal2, etc .;
chooses to reduce rather than displace when encountering a conflict in which the next token is terminal1 or terminal2, etc. On the other hand:
precedence right terminal1, terminal2, etc .;
chooses to shift in the same cases. Finally:
precedence nonassoc terminal1, terminal2, etc .;
121
134
Attributed grammars
would throw a syntactic error if encountering a shift / reduce conflict in analysis time.
As many clauses of this type as considered necessary can be indicated,
knowing that the order in which they appear causes it to be reduced first when finding the terminals of the last lists, that is, the lower a clause is found
precedence, the more priority you will have when reducing for it. In this way, it is normal to find things like:
precedence left ADD, SUBTRACT; precedence left MULTIPLY, DIVIDE;
which means that, in case of ambiguity, MULTIPLY and DIVIDE have more priority than ADD and SUBTRACT, and that all operations are associative to the left. These kinds of constructions only make sense in ambiguous grammars, such as it could be:
expr
:: =
expr MORE expr
|
expr MINUS expr
|
expr BY expr
|
expr BETWEEN expr
|
LPAREN expr RPAREN
|
NUMBER
;
Symbols that do not appear in any of these lists are assumed to have
lowest priority.
4.4.2.5 Grammar.
The last section of a Cup program is the grammar to be recognized, expressed
through production rules that must end in a semicolon, and where the symbol :: = serves as an arrow.
The grammar can start with a statement that says what the axiom is
initial. If this clause is omitted, it is assumed that the initial axiom is the antecedent of the first rule. Said declaration is made in the form:
start with noTerminal;
As in Yacc, it is possible to change the priority and precedence of a
rule that produces conflict move / reduce indicating the clause:
% prec terminal
next to her.
The production rules allow to house semantic actions inside them,
which are delimited by the symbols {: and :} . In these actions you can place any block of Java code, being of special importance the accesses to the attributes of the rule symbols. The attribute of the antecedent is given by the
Java RESULT variable , while the attributes of the consequent symbols are accessible by the names that the user has specified for each of the
122
135
they. This name is indicated to the right of the symbol in question and separated from it by two points. Its use within the semantic action must be consistent with the type assigned to the symbol when it was indicated in the symbol list. Must be paid special Be careful when using intermediate semantic actions since, in this case, the attributes of the accessible symbols of the consequent may only be used in read, which usually forces to give them an initial value from Jflex. An example of this situation can be studied in the section 8.4.6 .
On the other hand, and as with PCYacc, the special symbol error
allows error recovery using the panic mode method . In section7.4.4 proper error handling in Cup is illustrated in more detail.
4.4.3 Execution of Cup.
The Cup metacompiler itself is written in Java, so its execution
it obeys the model of any Java program. The main class is java_cup.Main , and takes its input from standard input:
java java_cup.Main <inputFile
It is also possible to specify some options to the metacompiler, from the
shape:
java java_cup.Main options <inputFile
where the most interesting options are:
-package packagename : the parser and sym classes will be located in the package indicated.
parser parsername : the parser will be called parsername and not parser .
symbols symbolName : the class that groups the symbols of the grammar it will be called symbolName and not sym .
expect number : by default, Cup aborts metacompilation when encounters a reduce / reduce conflict. If you want the behavior is identical to PCYacc, that is, choose the first rule in order of appearance in case of such conflict, it is necessary to tell Cup exactly the number of reduce / reduce conflicts expected. It is indicated using this option.
-progress : makes Cup inform as it processes the input grammar.
-dump : produces a readable dump of the grammar, the states of the automaton
generated by Cup, and from the analysis tables. if you want to get only the analysis tables (used to resolve conflicts), use can be made of from the more concise option -dump_states which skips grammar and tables analysis.

Chapter 5
JavaCC
​ 5.1​ Introduction
JavaCC ( Java Compiler Compiler - Metacompiler in Java) is the main
metacompiler in JavaCC, both for its capabilities and for its scope of diffusion. It is a tool that facilitates the construction of analyzers
lexical and syntactic by the method of recursive functions, although it allows a relaxed notation very similar to BNF. In this way, the parsers generated They use the descending technique when obtaining the syntactic tree.
5.1.1 General characteristics
JavaCC integrates the lexicographic analyzer and the
syntactic, and the code it generates is independent of any external library, which gives it an interesting property of independence from the environment. TO broadly speaking, its main characteristics are the following:
Generates top-down parsers, allowing the use of grammars fromgeneral purpose and the use of attributes both synthesized and inherited during the construction of the syntax tree.
The lexical and syntactic specifications are located in a single file. Of this way the grammar can be read and maintained more easily. Not However, when introducing semantic actions, we recommend using certain comments to improve readability.
Supports the use of lexical states and the ability to add lexical actionsincluding a block of Java code after a token identifier.
It incorporates different types of tokens: normal (TOKEN), special(SPECIAL_TOKEN), spacers (SKIP), and continuation (MORE). It allows you to work with clearer specifications, while allowing a better handling of error and warning messages by JavaCC in meta-compilation time.
Special tokens are ignored by the generated scanner, but areavailable to be processed by the developer.
The lexical specification can define tokens in such a way that they are notdifferentiate uppercase from lowercase either globally or in a concrete pattern.
It adopts its own BNF notation by using its own symbols
127
140
JavaCC
of regular expressions, such as (A) *, (A) +.
Generates an LL (1) parser by default. However, there may be portions of the grammar other than LL (1), which is solved in JavaCC through the ability to resolve displace / displace ambiguities locally at the point of conflict. In other words, allow the a.si. I know transform into LL (k) only at such points, but LL (1) is conserved at the rest of the rules improving efficiency.
Among the top-down parser generators, JavaCC isone of those with better error handling. The generated analyzers by JavaCC they are able to locate exactly the location of the errors, providing comprehensive diagnostic information.
Allows Unicode-encoded inputs, so specifications
Lexicals can also include any Unicode character. This facilitates description of language elements, such as Java identifiers that allow certain Unicode characters that are not ASCII.
Allows debugging both the generated parser and the lexicographic,using the DEBUG_PARSER, DEBUG_LOOKAHEAD, and DEBUG_TOKEN_MANAGER.
JavaCC offers many different options to customize yourbehavior and behavior of the generated parsers.
Includes the JJTree tool, a preprocessor for tree developmentwith very powerful features.
Includes the JJDoc tool that converts grammar files intodocumentation files.
It is highly efficient, which makes it suitable for professional environments and hasbecome one of the most widespread metacompilers (perhaps the one that more, above JFlex / Cup).
JavaCC is currently being maintained by the java.net code group
open, and official documentation can be found on the site
https://javacc.dev.java.net . Unfortunately, such documentation is too formal and does not incorporate any tutorial learning, although it is the best source of information once slightly familiar with the tool, what is the goal of this section.

preliminary information on the structure of a JavaCC input program and the notation used for this. The name "JavaCC" will be used to refer to both the JavaCC tool as well as the language it supports to describe grammars.

​ 5.2​ Structure of a program in JavaCC
As can be seen in the proposed example, the basic structure of a JavaCC program is:
options {

Area tokens
BNF function area
The options area allows you to specify some guidelines to help you JavaCC to generate lexical-parsers that are well more efficient, well more adapted to the specific needs of the developer. In the example we have indicated that, by default, the indicated grammar is of type LL (1), except if, at some point, indicates otherwise.
The PARSER_BEGIN and PARSER_END clauses are used to tell JavaCC the name of our main class, as well as to encompass both this and to any others who want to include support, such as, for example, a manager of symbol tables. In the example it can be seen that the main class constitutes the parser itself as the main () function creates an object of type Example while passing as a parameter in the constructor the source you want consume input: keyboard ( System.in ).
The class created by JavaCC will incorporate a function for each non-terminal of the
rules area. Each function will be responsible for consuming the part of the input that it underlies its associated nonterminal in the recognition syntax tree. By Therefore, assuming that the initial axiom is listExpr , a call of the form myParser.exprlist () will consume all the input, if it is acceptable.
The following two areas can be mixed, although the most common is usually
indicate first the tokens and finally the rules in BNF, especially for reasons clarity in the code.
In the example, tokens of two types have been indicated . The tokens grouped under
the SKIP clause are those that will be consumed without being passed to the parser syntactic; in our case they are: space, tab, carriage return (CR- Carry Return ) and line feed (LF- Line Feed ).
The tokens under the TOKEN clause constitute the normal tokens , although
the developer can also indicate this type of tokens in the BNF rules themselves, as with the patterns "(", ")", ";", etc. The declaration of each token is grouped in angle brackets and consists of the name of the token followed by the pattern associated and separated from it by two points. The lexicographic patterns are described similar to PCLex. The example illustrates the recognition of an identifier made up of letters only (either uppercase or lowercase thanks to the modifier [IGNORE_CASE] of the TOKEN clause ) and an integer.
The last area of  the example illustrates the creation of three non-terminals and their rules
Associated BNF. Since each nonterminal is going to be converted by JavaCC into a Java function, its declaration is identical to that of said function and is subject to the same restrictions than any other function in Java. The body of each of these functions will be built by JavaCC and will have as its purpose the proper consumption
131
144
JavaCC
of tokens based on the BNF expression indicated in the specification.
The BNF notation used makes use of the symbol * which represents repetition
0 or more times, + for repetition 1 or more times, | for optionality, parentheses to group, etc. The terminals can be indicated in two ways, either by placing between angle brackets any of those declared in the previous phase, or indicating their pattern directly if it consists of a constant string of characters
(Actually, JavaCC allows any regular expression, but this is not useful for
our purposes). In the example, it is worth noting the inclusion of the "@" character in the ad hoc terminals NEW and ALGO , since otherwise they would be considered identifiers by matching the token ID pattern . The "NEW" and "ALGO" patterns would have been correctly recognized if the token ID had been declared after the appearance of these in the BNF rules.
Each of these will be studied in greater detail below.
sections.
5.2.1 Options.
This section begins with the options keyword followed by a list
of one or more options in braces. The entire section can be omitted as it does not is mandatory
Options can be specified in both the grammar file and
on the command line. The same option should not be set more than once, although in the case that they are specified in the command line, they have greater priority than those specified in the file.
Option names are capitalized, ending in ";" and
They are separated from the value associated with them by the "=" sign. Below are detailed
briefly the most important options and the functions they perform:
LOOKAHEAD : indicates the amount of tokens that are taken into account by the parser before making a decision. The default value is 1 lo that will perform LL (1) analysis. The smaller the number of tokens of pre-search, the faster the analyzes will be performed. This value can be modify in certain specific rules.
CHOICE_AMBIGUITY_CHECK - Used for JavaCC to provide
more accurate error messages when you come across multiple options with a prefix of length equal to or greater than LOOKAHEAD . In these
In these cases, JavaCC usually reports that an error has been found and advises that a lookahead equal to or greater than a certain number n is tested . Yes
CHOICE_AMBIGUITY_CHECK is k , so JavaCC will try
figure out the exact lookahead we need (for
inform us of it) as long as it does not exceed the k value . The
Using this option provides more accurate information, but at the cost
132
145
longer meta-compilation time. Its default value is 2.
FORCE_LA_CHECK : one of the main problems that can be encountered JavaCC in the construction of the parser lies in the existence of common prefixes in different options of the same rule, which makes it vary the number of pre -search tokens required to be able to choose one of these options. When the LOOKAHEAD option takes the value 1, JavaCC performs error checking by prefix checks on all
options where the developer has not indicated a LOOKEAHEAD local, reporting on screen of such situations. If LOOKAHEAD is greater than 1, then JavaCC does not perform such control on any option. Yes is given to FORCE_LA_CHECK the value true (defaults to false ), the prefix check will be performed on all options including those have local LOOKEAHEAD , even though this is correct.
STATIC : if the value is true (default value), all methods and variables are create as static both in the lexical analyzer and in the syntactic what notably improves its execution. However, this makes it only possible perform an analysis each time and prevents, simultaneously, different objects of the same class can process different input sources. Yes is defined as static, the same object can process different sources sequentially, invoking ReInit () every time you want to start with a new. If it is not static, different objects can be constructed (with new ) to process each source.
DEBUG_PARSER : This option is used to obtain information from Run-time debugging of the generated parser. his
default value is false , although the trace of actions and decisions taken can be turned on or off by program by invoking
enable_tracing () and disable_tracing () respectively. In a similar way the DEBUG_LOOKAHEAD options can be used , and
DEBUG_TOKEN_MANAGER .
BUILD_PARSER : tells JavaCC whether or not to generate the parser class syntactic. Its default value is true , as is the option BUILD_TOKEN_MANAGER , which causes both to be generated analyzers.
IGNORE_CASE : if its value is true , the lexicographic analyzer ( token manager ) generated does not differentiate between uppercase letters and lowercase when it comes to recognizing lexemes. It is especially useful in the recognition of languages  such as HTML. Its default value is false .
COMMON_TOKEN_ACTION : if the value of this option is true , all Invocations to the getNextToken () method of the parser class lexicographic, cause a call to the CommonTokenAction () method after the recognition of each lexeme and after executing the action
133
146
JavaCC
associated lexical, if any. The user must define this last method in the TOKEN_MGR_DECLS section of the token area . The default value is false .
UNICODE_INPUT : if set to true , a capable lexical analyzer is generated to handle a Unicode-encoded entry. By default it is false what assumes that the input will be in ASCII.
OUTPUT_DIRECTORY : is an option whose default value is the directory current. Controls where the output files are generated.
5.2.2 Token area
This area allows the construction of a lexicographic analyzer according to the
particular needs of each project. In JavaCC's own jargon the Lexical analyzers are called token managers .
JavaCC differentiates four types of tokens or terminals, depending on what
you must do with each lexeme associated with them:
SKIP: ignore the lexeme.
MORE: searches for the next lexeme but concatenating it to the alreadyrecovered.
TOKEN: obtains a lexeme and creates an object of type Token that returns to parser (or whoever invoked it). This return occurs
automatically without the developer having to specify any statement return on no lexical action.
SPECIAL_TOKEN: same as SKIP but stores lexemes in such a waythat can be recovered if necessary. It can be useful when
construct source-source translators in such a way that they want to preserve the comments even though they do not influence the translation process.
Once a lexeme has been found, whatever its type, it is possible to run a Lexical action.
The format of this phase is as follows:
TOKEN_MGR_DECLS: {
java block
}
<LexicalState> TOKEN [IGNORE_CASE]: {
<token1: pattern1> {LexicalAction1}: newLexicalState1
​ |​ <token2: pattern2> {LexicalAction2}: newLexicalState2
​ |​ ...
}
<LexicalState> TOKEN [IGNORE_CASE]: {
<token1: pattern1> {LexicalAction1}: newLexicalState1
134
147
​ |​ <token2: pattern2> {LexicalAction2}: newLexicalState2
​ |​ ...
}
...
where:
The word TOKEN can be replaced by SKIP, MORE, or SPECIAL_TOKEN.
The [IGNORE_CASE] clause is optional but, if set, they should notforget the brackets.
The pattern can be preceded by a token name and separated from it by colon, but it is not required (for example, in separators it would not have sense).
If the pattern is a quoted constant without associated token, they can the angle brackets that surround it and, therefore, the name should be omitted.
Lexical states are any identifier, preferably inuppercase, or the word DEFAULT representing the lexical state
initial. They can be omitted. A list of lexical statements may also be indicated separated by commas.
If the lexical state is omitted before the word TOKEN, it means that theGrouping patterns can be applied in any lexical state.
If the lexical state is omitted after a pattern, it means that, after finding alexeme that fits with it, will remain in the same lexical state.
Lexical actions are made up of Java code and are optional.
Lexical actions have access to everything that has been declared withinthe optional clause TOKEN_MGR_DECLS.
Lexical actions are executed within a token manager after recover the lexeme in question.
JavaCC follows the same premises as PCLex when looking for lexemes,that is, always look for the longest possible lexeme and, if it fits For two patterns, go for the one that appears first.
If JavaCC finds a lexeme that does not match any pattern it produces aLexical error. This situation should be avoided by the developer by using recognition of erroneous lexemes and the issuance of the corresponding Error message.
5.2.2.1 Special characters for JavaCC patterns
Characters that have a special meaning when forming
regular expressions are:
"": Used to delimit any string of characters.
<>: used to reference a previously defined token . Sometimes it can be interesting to create tokens with the sole purpose of using them within others, such as:
TOKEN:
{
135
148
JavaCC
<LITERAL_COMA_FLOTANTE:
(["0" - "9"]) + "," (["0" - "9"]) * (<EXPONENT>)? (["f", "F", "d", "D"])?
| "." (["0" - "9"]) + (<EXPONENT>)? (["f", "F", "d", "D"])?
| (["0" - "9"]) + <EXPONENT> (["f", "F", "d", "D"])?
| (["0" - "9"]) + (<EXPONENT>)? ["f", "F", "d", "D"]
>
|
<EXPONENT: ["e", "E"] (["+", "-"])? (["0" - "9"]) +> }
The problem with this example is that the lexical analyzer can recognize the string "E123" as being of type EXPONENT , when actually it should be an ID. In situations like this, the EXPONENT token can be declared as (note the use of the "#" character): < # EXPONENT: ["e", "E"] (["+", "-"])? (["0" - "9"]) +> which tells JavaCC that it is not a full-fledged token , but that you should use it as a model of use in those other patterns that reference.
*: indicates repetition 0 or more times of what precedes it in parentheses. Ex .: (“Ab”) * matches g, “ab”, “abab”, etc. The pattern "ab" * is incorrect since that the symbol "*" must be preceded by a pattern in parentheses.
+: indicates repetition 1 or more times of what precedes it in parentheses.
?: indicates that what precedes it in parentheses is optional.
|: indicates optionality. Ex .: ("ab" | "cd") matches either "ab" or "cd".
[]: used to express lists of characters. Inside may appear:
Single characters enclosed in quotation marks and separated by commas.
Eg: ["a", "b", "c"] that will match the lexemes "a", "b" or "c".
Character ranges made up of two single characters betweenquotes and separated by a hyphen. Ex .: ["a" - "c"] that will match the lexemes "a", "b" or "c".
Any combination of the above. Ex .: ["a" - "z", "A" - "Z", “Ñ”, “Ñ”] that will match any Spanish letter without signs diacritics (accents or umlauts).
~ []: serves to express a list of characters complementary to the one given as seen above for []. For example, the ~ [] pattern is a way to emulate the pattern (. | \ n) of PCLex.
JavaCC always automatically creates the <EOF> token that matches the
end of file character.
On the other hand, there is no lexical statement declaration area, as in
PCLex or JFlex, but these are used directly. A simple example for recognizing non-nested Java or C style comments would be:
136
149
SKIP: {
"/ *": InsideComment
}
<InComment> SKIP: {
"* /": DEFAULT
}
<InComment> MORE: {
<~ []>
}

5.2.2.3 The Token class and the token manager
As already mentioned, each time the lexical analyzer finds a
lexeme corresponding to a normal token , returns an object of class Token . The variables and main functions of this class are:
image : String type variable that stores the recognized lexeme.
kind : for each pattern to which the developer associates a name, JavaCC includes an integer constant in the class of constants it generates. The kind variable stores one of these integers, specifically the name of the pattern by which his lexeme fitted.
beginLine , beginColumn , endLine and endColumn : variables of type integer indicating, respectively, the line and column of the beginning and end of the lexeme.
specialToken : Token type variable that refers to the previous token if and only if it was of a special kind; otherwise it will store the null value .
Finally, indicate that JavaCC provides in the parser class
Lexical ( ExampleTokenManager according to the example of point5.1.2 ) two methods that They can be interesting, if you want to build just that parser, namely:
A constructor that takes an object of type CharStream from the which will take the input to analyze.
The getNextToken () function that returns the following object of type Token found at the entrance. Developer must control token type <EOF> to not consume beyond the end of the input.
The following example illustrates a JavaCC program that is intended only
employ the lexical analyzer part. Although the BUILD_PARSER option is to false , JavaCC forces to indicate the PARSER_BEGIN and PARSER_END clauses , and a class of the same name inside, even if it's empty.

«JavaCC allows the exchange of attributes between BNF rules by means of the passing parameters and obtaining results at the time of use of a non-terminal (or what is the same, invoke the function that implements) which is equivalent, respectively, to sending attributes down and up in the syntactic tree. '

*: indicates repetition 0 or more times of what precedes it in parentheses.

?: indicates that what precedes it in parentheses is optional.

which means that they are able to choose between different flow options by consulting so just the next token in the input. However, we already know that not all grammars are LL (1), so there may be situations where it is necessary to query more than one token of the entrance in order to make a decision. For example, the following grammar: void expr (): {} {
<ID>
​ |​ <ID> "." expr ()
​ |​ <ID> "()"
}
it is not LL (1) because, before an input like "cont.aux ()" the parser is as the first token with an <ID> , but since the three possible options of expr () start with <ID> , as there is not enough information to know which of them to choose. In this case, a pre-search of two tokens would allow us to decide correctly.
To do this, the LOOKAHEAD option seen in section 5.2.1 allows you to tell JavaCC the type of grammar that is being provided, that is, the number of tokens that You must pre-search before making a decision.
Notwithstanding the foregoing, in most cases, the grammars of the languages  of 143
156
JavaCC
programming contain a majority of LL (1) expressions, and only a few rules for which may require a pre-search of 2, or 3 at the most. In these cases, you will get a more efficient parser if you leave the default pre-lookup (to 1), and tell it
to JavaCC in which specific cases you should use a superior pre-search. This is achieved with the pre-search or local lookahead , which has the form:
​ LOOKAHEAD ( n ) pattern​ one
and that it is considered in itself a pattern as well. The meaning of this clause is very
​ simple: it tells the parser that, before deciding to enter through the pattern ,              one
make sure this is correct by taking n tokens . In this way, the above grammar is you can leave as LL (1) and indicate the local pre-search of the form: void expr (): {} {
LOOKAHEAD (2) <ID>
​ |​ LOOKAHEAD (2) <ID> "." expr ()
​ |​ <ID> "()"
}
Furthermore, JavaCC itself is capable of deciding whether the pre-search specified by the
developer is adequate or not, reporting it in negative case both due to excess and default.
​ 5.3​ Attribute management.
JavaCC lacks a specific attribute management, but allows the passage of
parameters and the return of values  each time a non-terminal is invoked, as shown introduced at the point 5.2.3 . In this way, every time a non-terminal is invoked, we can assign the returned value to a global variable declared in the Java code block associated with said rule; this value acts as a synthesized attribute. Also, when declares a nonterminal, it can have formal parameters, such that it must be invoked with real parameters that will act as inherited attributes.
Unfortunately, JavaCC also does not allow associating attributes to terminals, so
that it should be the semantic actions that work directly with the lexemes. Although This can be a hassle in most cases, it has the advantage that each action
semantics you can extract the information that suits you from the lexeme, instead of always using the same attribute in all the consequents in which the same terminal appears. The next

For example, before the entry 23 + 5 + 11 is it will pass 2 times through the point Ø, since it is necessary to pass 2 times through the subexpression (“+” Term ()) * to recognize that entry.
Also, note that a semantic action placed just after a

This is so despite the pre-search that has been indicated, since it is the parser which loads said variable just after consuming each terminal, and regardless of which ones you had to pre-search to take a decision on which option to make.
When semantic actions are included inside a BNF expression, This becomes extremely rare, being very difficult to recognize grammar in a sea of code. To avoid this problem it is recommended to place a comment in front of each nonterminal the pure BNF expression that defines it.
On the other hand, when it is convenient for a terminal t to always have the same
attribute in all the consequents in which it appears, it is usually more readable to create a rule T :: = t , associate to T the attribute calculated from t and, in the rest of the rules, use T instead of t .

​ 5.4​ Error management.
Error handling can be achieved in JavaCC in two ways:
Customizing error messages.
Recovering from mistakes.
5.4.1 Old versions of JavaCC
Until version 0.6 of JavaCC to customize error messages was It is necessary to set the ERROR_REPORTING option to true . With it JavaCC will provide the parser class with the variables:
error_line , error_column : variables of type integer that represent the line and the column in which the error was detected.
error_string : String type variable that stores the lexeme that has the error occurred. If the pre-search ( lookahead ) has been set to a value greater than 1, error_string can contain a sequence of lexemes.
expected_tokens : String [] type variable that contains the tokens that they would have been valid. Again, if the pre-search has been set to a value greater than 1, each item in expected_tokens can be a sequence of tokens
146
159
instead of a loose token .
Every time the parser encounters a syntax error it will invoke
to the token_error () function , which will need to be rewritten by the developer so This will need to create a new class that inherits from the parser class.
Similarly, the lexical analyzer can make use of the
elements:
error_line , error_column : line and column of the lexical error.
error_after : character string containing the piece of lexeme to be tried to recognize.
curChar : character that produced the lexical error.
LexicalError () : function invoked by the manager token when it is found a lexical error.
5.4.2 Modern versions of JavaCC
Currently, JavaCC handles lexical and syntactic errors by launching the
TokenMgrError and ParseException exceptions , whose classes are generated by JavaCC automatically if they don't already exist in the working directory. The philosophy is that developer should prevent lexical errors from occurring by controlling properly any input lexeme including invalid ones, in which case it should issue the corresponding error message.
In this way, the developer can modify the ParseException class
to adapt it to your needs. By default, this class has the variables:
currentToken : of type Token , it stores the token object that has produced the error.
expectedTokenSequences : of type int [] [] has the same objective as the variable expected_tokens seen in the previous section.
tokenImage : of type String [] l stores the last lexemes read. If the pre-search is at 1, then it is true:
tokenImage [0] .equals (currentToken.image);
On the other hand, error recovery does not require any modification of
the ParseException class , but instead uses a clause similar to the tryJava catch to catch the exception at a convenient point. Said clause is also considered a pattern, so it can appear anywhere one is expected. The clause is of the form:
try {
Pattern catch (AnyException x) {
Java Recovery Code
147
160
JavaCC
}
}
Note that the JavaCC try-catch clause can catch any
exception, and not just the ParseException , so the developer can
create your own exceptions and raise them where and when they occur. For example, it may be useful to create the exception SymbolsTableException .
The recovery code usually works in panic mode , consuming all the
entry until you reach a security token . The following example illustrates how recover errors in statements ending in semicolons. void FinishedStatement (): {} {
try {​ (statement () <POINTANDOMA>) catch (ParseException e) {
System.out.println (e.toString ()); Token t; do {
t = getNextToken ();
} while (t.kind! = POINT ANDOMA);
}
}

​ 5.6​ The JJTree utility
JJTree is the preprocessor for JavaCC. Insert actions that build
parser trees in the JavaCC source. The output generated by JJTree runs through of JavaCC to create the parser.
5.6.1 Features
By default, JJTree generates code to build the parse node tree
for each non-terminal of our language. This operation can be changed from so that certain non-terminals do not generate nodes, or generate a node from a expansion of a production rule.
JJTree defines a Java interface type Node, which all nodes of the tree
parser must implement. The interface provides methods for operations like initialization of the parent type of a generated node, as well as addition and retrieval of children.
JJTree can operate in two different ways: simple or multi (to search
better terms). In simple mode, each node in the parser tree is of type
SimpleNode ; in multi mode, the type of the nodes in the parse tree is derived from the name of the node. If no implementations are provided for the node class, JJTree will generate implementations based on the SimpleNode class , being able to modify the implementations so that they can be useful to us.

5.6.2.1 Renaming nodes using #
# is a constructor that is used so that the user can call each node
in a different way than JavaCC calls it by default. Must operate in multi mode so that each node can be derived from the name that we put immediately after of the # constructor. It also serves to simplify the syntax and remove from the stack nodes that do not contribute information to the tree generated by our grammar.
In the options area, the multi mode must be active as follows
way:
options {

}

5.6.2.2 Tree Construction
155
168
JavaCC
Although JavaCC is a top-down grammar generator, JJTree builds
the tree of the sequence of grammar rules from below, that is, in a upward. To do this, it makes use of a stack where the nodes are stored once generated. When trying to shrink a set of nodes by assigning
a parent node for all of them, these child nodes are removed from the stack and associated with the parent, subsequently storing the parent-child set on the stack. Whenever the battery is accessible (open battery) it will be possible to carry out operations within the grammatical actions such as 'push', 'pop' and whatever else is deemed appropriate to our needs.
These methods are implemented automatically, since JJTree is the one that
is responsible for generating the status file mentioned above (for our example,
JJTeexample1State.java and JJTeexample1bisState.java ; see States in JJTree for more information). Below are some methods that have been generated automatically:

}

}
Node rootNode () {
return (Node) nodes.elementAt (0);
}
void pushNode (Node n) { nodes.push (n);
++ sp; }
Node popNode () { if (--sp <mk) { mk = ((Integer) marks.pop ()). intValue ();
} return (Node) nodes.pop ();
}
5.6.2.3 Types of Nodes
JJTree provides decorations for two basic types of nodes, in addition to
some syntactic simplifications for ease of use.
A node is built with a defined number of children. Remember that
A parent node is associated with one or more child nodes that are taken from the battery and inserted back into it, but forming part of the parent structure. A defined node can be created as follows way:
156
169
#ADefiniteNode (INTEGER EXPRESSION)
INTEGER EXPRESSION can be any type of integer expression.
The most common is that this expression is a constant (of type integer).
ADefiniteNode is the name with which we want to designate the node (non-terminal, java function) that precedes it.
A conditional node is built with all the child nodes associated with thescope of the parent as long as the parent condition evaluates to true. Yes not so, said node is not created, returning to the stack the child nodes that they had previously been associated with the evaluated parent node. This is represents as follows:
#ConditionalNode (BOOLEAN EXPRESSION)
BOOLEAN EXPRESSION can be any expression of type
boolean. As in the previous case, the name given to the node is given by ConditionalNode . There are two common types of abbreviations for said nodes:
to. #IndefiniteNode is equivalent to #IndefiniteNode (true)
b. #GTNode (> 1) is equivalent to #GTNode (JJTree.arity ()> 1) #IndefiniteNode can lead to ambiguous situations in JJTree when this expression is followed by another with parentheses. In these cases, You must replace the reduced expression with its unreduced version:
(… ..) #N (a ()) is an ambiguous expression. Instead, you should use
(… ..) #N (true) (a ())
Note that the expressions passed as parameters to the nodes do not they must have side effects. JJTree does not specify how many times it will be evaluated the expression.
By default, JJTree treats each nonterminal as an undefined node and derives
its name from its production. User can change this derived name using the following expression, as explained above, in the area of  the example:
void ExpSuma () #expS: {}
{
ExpMult () (("+" | "-") ExpMult ()) *
}
When the parser recognizes the non-terminal ExpSuma () , it starts as a node
undefined with name expS . It is drawn on the stack, so that any tree parse of nodes created and stored on the stack by non-terminals in expanding ExpSuma () will be removed from it and will become part of the node structure expS as its children. The children generated will have a name on the stack ExpMult .
If you want to suppress the creation of a node by a production, you can use
the following syntax:
void ExpSuma () #void: {}
{
157
170
JavaCC
ExpMult () (("+" | "-") ExpMult ()) *
}
Now any node in the parser tree stored on the stack that has been
generated by the non-terminal ExpSuma () , it will remain on the stack to be pulled from the itself and become a child of an ascending production in the tree, but it will not to be part of ExpSuma () , since this node is not on the stack. Their children
(productions) will become the children of the node immediately above ExpSuma () . This operation can be done by default for undecorated nodes (without attribute specification) using the NODE_DEFAULT_VOID option .
This is achieved by adding, to the options area of example1bis.jjt , the
following code:
options {
MULTI = true;
NODE_DEFAULT_VOID = true; }
The effect that this option has on the code is the "saving" of indicating each
non-terminal like #void : void ExpSum (): {}
{
ExpMult () (("+" | "-") ExpMult ()) *
}
One way of code optimization using the # constructor is to
the next:
void ExpSuma () #void: {}
{
(ExpMult () (("+" | "-") ExpMult ()) *) # Sum (> 1)
}
The ExpSuma () node will have one or more nodes named Sum as children .
The #Sum constructor is of type conditional, and indicates that it is of arity greater than 1.
The conditional type is used since it is not known how many non-terminals of this type are They have to generate to recognize the sentence that happened to the grammar. Exercises operator in post-fixed form, and its scope corresponds to the expansion unit that it immediately precedes you.
5.6.3 Scopes of Nodes and User Actions
Each node is associated with a scope. In order to access the node, the actions
that the user defines for said access must have the same scope as the node, and in addition, the special jjThis method must be used to refer to it. East identifier is implicitly declared as follows:
SimpleNode Start (): {}
{
Expression () ";"
{return jjtThis; }
158
171
}
In this way it is easier to access the fields and methods of the node to which
you want to access. This identifier always returns the scope to which the node.
A scope is the unit that expands and immediately precedes it
to the node with specification of attributes ( decorated node ); for example, it may be a expression in parentheses. When the production signature is decorated (may have to the default node implicitly), the scope is the right part of the production, including the declaration block.
You can also use the jjThis method, passing it as a parameter the part
left of a reference expansion. An example based on the grammar that I am using is as follows: void ExpUnaria (): {} {
"(" Expression () ")" | Identifier () | <ENTER>
}
void Identifier () #Identifier: { Token token;}
{ token = <IDENTIFIER> {jjtThis.setName (token.image); }
}
Here, jjtThis "works" in the scope of the node where it was invoked. Is
that is, the modifications made will only have an effect on this node. This operation can be done by using the NODE_SCOPE_HOOKS option , which, for
By default, it is set to false (see the Node Scope Hooks section for more information). When removing the nodes stored in the stack on the screen we observe that the output corresponding to the input (a + b) * (c + d) is:
Start

In order to update the value of the name field of each node, we must
implement the setName ( ) method ourselves (or whatever else we want
make use of, like setFirstToken () , setLastToken () , getName () ...). The form and the place correct implementation are explained in the Node Scope Hooks area of this document. The final action that a user takes in the scope of a node is different of everyone else's. When the code is executed, the children of the node are removed from the stack and added to the node structure, which is stored by itself in the
159
172
JavaCC
battery. The children of the parent can be accessed using the jjGetChild () method . These Access methods to the attributes of each node have been implemented in a automatic, as explained above.
Other user actions can only access children on the stack, due to
because they are not yet part of the father. The methods that imply accesses to the node do not work (such as jjGetChild () ).
A conditional node that has a node descriptor and evaluates to false,
it will not be added to the stack, nor will any children be added to its structure. The end user, within the scope of a conditional node, you can specify whether the node was created or not by calling the nodeCreated () method (implemented in
SimpleNode.java ). Returns true if the node condition is satisfied, if the node has been created and whether it has been stored on the stack. Returns false otherwise.
5.6.4 Handling Exceptions
An exception thrown by an expansion in the scope of a node that is not
captured in said scope, it is captured by JJTree. When this occurs, any node that has been stored on the stack is removed along with its scope correspondent. Then the exception is collected again.
This type of exception handling is intended to make possible a
implementation of error recovery and continue with the generation of the parser a start from a known state.
It is important to note that, currently, JJTree does not detect when a
exception is thrown from a user action within the scope of a node, so an exception can probably be handled incorrectly.
5.6.5 Node Scope Hooks
This option alters the behavior of JJTree, so that,
automatically, all recognized tokens are captured. If the option
NODE_SCOPE_HOOK is evaluated to true, JJTree generates calls to two methods user-defined at the input and output of each node scope. These methods
They are:
void jjtreeOpenNodeScope (Node node) void jjtreeCloseNodeScope (Node node)
These methods have to be defined so that they have access to the first and
last token of the generated node. In this way, it can be iterated from the first token found until the last, without fear that the scope of the iteration will change, due because those methods remain in scope of the current node.
The following fields must be added: Token first , last ;
They must be implemented in the file generated by javacc automatically
called SimpleNode.jav to, since from this class, javac feeds on all
160
173
methods used in the source with a .jjt extension .
The SimpleNode.java file from example3.jjt is presented below as support for the explanation given above:
public class SimpleNode implements Node {
protected Node parent; protected Node [] children; protected int id; protected String name; protected Token token, first, last; protected example3 parser;

}

Node c [] = new Node [i + 1];
System.arraycopy (children, 0, c, 0, children.length); children = c;
}
children [i] = n;
}
public Node jjtGetChild (int i) {
return children [i];
}
public int jjtGetNumChildren () { return (children == null)? 0: children.length;
}

5.6.6 The life cycle of a node
A node goes through a certain sequence of steps in its generation. The
sequence seen from the node itself is as follows:
1. The node's constructor is called with a single parameter of type integer. This parameter tells us what type of node it is, this being especially useful in simple mode. JJTree automatically generates a file called parserTreeConstants.java and declares constants in Java for the node identifier. Also, declare an array of Strings called jjtNodeName [], which assigns integer identifiers to the names of nodes. For our example2, the file is the following:
public interface example2TreeConstants { public int JJTHOME = 0; public int JJTVOID = 1;
public int JJTSUMA = 2; public int JJTMULT = 3; public int JJTIDENTIFIER = 4; public int JJTENTERO = 5; public String [] jjtNodeName = {
"Start",
"void",
"Sum",
"Mult",
"Identifier",
"Whole",
};
}
A call is made to the jjtOpen () method.
If the NODE_SCOPE_HOOK option is active, the method is called user-defined parser openNodeScope () and passed the node as a parameter. For example, you could store the first token in the node:
static void jjtreeOpenNodeScope (Node node) {
((SimpleNode) node) .setFirstToken (getToken (1));
}
This method has to be implemented in the main class, that is, in the example2.jjt
If a handlerless exception is thrown while the node is runninggenerating, said node is discarded. JJTree will never do again reference to this node. Said node will not be closed, and in the method of user closeNodeHook () will not use that node as a parameter in a call.
Otherwise, if the node is conditional, and its condition evaluates to false,
163
176
JavaCC
the node is discarded. This node will not be closed, unless the method user-defined closeNodeHook () can be used in a call with this node as a parameter.
Otherwise, the number of children of the node specified in the expressioninteger of the defined node, or all nodes that were stored in the stack in a conditional node scope, they are added to the node (said number is the arity of the node; if it is conditional, #Id (> 1) is defined ) . He order in which they are added is not specified.
The jjtClose () method is called .
The node is stored on the stack.
If the NODE_SCOPE_HOOK option is active, the parser method user-defined closeNodeScope () is called and uses the node as a parameter.
If the node is not the root node, it is added as a child structure to anothernode and its jjtParent () method is called .
5.6.7 Visitor Support
JJTree provides some basic support for the visitor layout pattern. If the VISITOR option is true, JJTree will add a jjtAccept () method in all classes of node that it generates, in addition to the creation of a user interface that can be implemented and passed to nodes.
The name of such an interface is constructed by adding Visitor to the name
of the parser. The interface is generated every time JJTree runs, so represents exactly the set of nodes used by the parser. If the class implemented has not been updated for the new nodes generated, it will produce compile-time errors.
5.6.7.1 Execution Parameters
JJTree provides options for the command line and for options JavaCC statements:
​ one.​ BUILD_NODE_FILES ( true by default): generates implementations of
sample for SimpleNode and for any others​ nodes used in grammar.
MULTI ( false by default): generates the parse tree in multi mode. Each node (non-terminal) created and not declared as void , generates a file named terminalNoName.java , in which each node extends from SimpleNode . These files are generated automatically.
NODE_DEFAULT_VOID ( false by default): instead of creating each undecorated output as an undefined node, creates it void in its
164
177
place. Any decorated node will not be affected by this option.
NODE_FACTORY ( false by default): use a generation method to build nodes with the following structure:
public static Node jjtCreate (int id)
NODE_PACKAGE ( "" by default): the package to generate the node in a class. By default, it contains the parser package.
NODE_PREFIX ( "AST" by default): the prefix used to build the node class names from the node ids in
multi mode. It is the prefix that is automatically set by default when the files containing the implementations of the nodes that extend from SimpleNode .
NODE_SCOPE_HOOK ( false by default): inserts calls to user-defined parser methods on each input and output of each node scope. See Node Scope Hooks
NODE_USERS_PARSER ( false by default): JJTree will use a shape
alternative of the routines of construction of nodes where the parser of the object. For example:
public static Node MyNode.jjtCreate (MyParser p, int id); MyNode (MyParser p, int id);
STATIC ( true by default): generates code for a static parser. Must be used consistently with equivalent options provided by JavaCC. The value of this option is shown in the
JavaCC source.
VISITOR ( false by default): insert a jjtAccept () method in classes of the node, and generates a visitor implementation with an input for each type of node used in the grammar.
VISITOR_EXCEPTION ( "" by default): if this option is active, it is used in the structures of the jjtAccept () and visit () methods . A lot
Be careful because this option will be removed in later versions of JJTree.
5.6.8 States in JTree
JJTree keeps its state in a field of the parser class called jjtree. Methods on this member can be used to manipulate the node's stack. Some of the auto-generated methods for this class are discussed above.
It also indicates in which file the generated code is stored.
5.6.8.1 Node objects
All AST ( Abstract Syntax Tree ) nodes must implement this interface.
Provides a basic engine for building parent-child relationships between nodes.
165
178
JavaCC
This interface is common for all the .jjt files that are launched using the
jjtree executable . The content is:
public interface Node {
/ * This method is called after the node has become the node current. Indicates that child nodes can be added * / public void jjtOpen ();
/ * This method is called after all child nodes have been added * / public void jjtClose ();
/ * The following 2 methods are used to assign / return the parent of a node*/ public void jjtSetParent (Node n); public Node jjtGetParent ();
/ * This method adds its argument to the list of child nodes * / public void jjtAddChild (Node n, int i);
/ * This method returns a child node. They are numbered from 0, from left to right*/ public Node jjtGetChild (int i);
/ * Returns the number of children that the node has * / int jjtGetNumChildren ();
}
The SimpleNode class implements the Node interface , and if it doesn't exist, it is generated
automatically by JJTree. You can use this class as a template, as a superclass for node implementations, or can be modified at your convenience.
SimpleNode provides, in addition, a simple "collector" mechanism.
recursive of the node and its children. This method is the one that appears in the examples above described as dump () .
The String type parameter of the dump () method is used as a prefix for
indicate the hierarchical structure of the tree.
If the VISITOR option is active, another method is generated that can be
utility:
public void childrenAccept (MyParserVisitor visitor) ;
This method loops through the child nodes in order, inviting them to accept visitor. East
This method is very useful when you have implemented a structure in preorder or postorder.

not found "when compiling the file .java generated automatically by javacc.

5.6.9 JJDoc Reference Manual
167
180
JavaCC
JJDoc takes the parser specification from JavaCC and produces the
corresponding documentation for BNF grammar. It can work in three ways different, depending on the command entered in the options area:
TEXT : By default, this option is false. Initializing this option to true, JJDoc generates a simple text description of the grammar in BNF. Some formatting is done via tab characters, but the goal is to keep it as simple as possible.
ONE_TABLE : By default, true. The default value of ONE_TABLE is used to generate a single HTML table for BNF notation. Update value a false supposes the generation of a table for each grammar production.
OUTPUT_FILE : The default operation is to put the output of JJDoc in a file together with the extension .html or .txt appended as a suffix to the input file name. You can supply a filename different with this option.
When the TEXT option is activated or the option is set to false ONE_TABLE , the comments in the JavaCC source that immediately precede a production are ignored by the documentation generated.
For ONE_TABLE , we obtain a file example2.html whose content is:

Chapter 6
Symbol table
​ 6.1​ Overview
Also called "table of names" or "table of identifiers", it is
simply from a high-performance data structure that stores all the necessary information about user identifiers. It has two functions main:
Carry out semantic checks.
Generate code.
Also, this structure remains in memory only in dump time.
compilation, not execution, except in those cases where it is compiled with debugging options. Interpreters also usually maintain the symbol table in memory during execution, since it occurs simultaneously with the translation.
The symbol table stores the information that is currently
you need about the program variables; information such as: name, type, memory location address, size, etc. Adequate and effective management of The symbol table is very important, since its manipulation consumes a large part compile time. Hence its efficiency is critical.
The symbol table is also used to store information regarding the
user-created data types, enumerated types, and generally any identifier created by the user. In these cases, the developer can choose to mix the different kinds of identifiers in a single table, or have several tables, where each of them will house a different class of identifiers:
variable table, data type table, user functions table, etc. In what We are still going to focus mainly on user variables.
​ 6.2​ Information about user identifiers
The information that the developer decides to store in this table will depend
of the specific characteristics of the translator you are developing. Between this information may include:
• Name of the element. The name or identifier can be stored by limiting or not the length of it. If stored with limit using a size fixed maximum for each name, creation speed can be increased and manipulation, but at the cost of limiting the length of names to a few 171
184
Symbol table
cases and wasting space in most cases. The alternative method consists of
enable the memory that we need in each case to save the name. In C this is easy with type char *; if we do the compiler in Modula-2, for
For example, the ADDRESS type should be used. In the case of Java, this does not it involves some difficulty thanks to the String type. Searches in the table symbols are usually made by this name; therefore, and to speed up the this operation, the table of symbols usually be a hash table ( hash ) where search and insert operations have a cost approximately constant (. O (1)).
Type of the element. When storing variables, it is essential know the type of data to which each of them belongs, whether it is primitive as if not, in order to be able to control that the use made of
such variables is consistent with the type with which they were declared. The chapter 7 is exclusively dedicated to this management.
Memory address where its value will be stored at runtime.This address is necessary, because instructions that refer to a variable must know where to find the value of that variable at execution in order to be able to generate machine code, whether it is global and local variables. In languages  that do not allow recursion, addresses are assigned sequentially as the addresses are made.
statements. In languages  with block structures, the address is given with relative to the beginning of the memory area assigned to that block (function or procedure) specifically.
Value of the element. When working with simple interpreters, and since compilation and execution times overlap in an interpreter, you can it will be easier to manage the variables if we store their values  in the symbol table. In a compiler, however, the symbol table does not never stores the value.
Number of dimensions. If the variable to be stored is an array, also its dimensions can be stored. Although this information may be extracted from the type structure, it can be explicitly stated for a more efficient control.
Types of formal parameters. If the identifier to store belongs to a function or procedure, it is necessary to store the types of the formal parameters to control that any invocation of this function is made with consistent real parameters. The return type is also stores as the type of the element.
Other information. In order to obtain statistical summaries and information varies, it may be interesting to store other data: line numbers in those that have used an identifier, line number in which it was declared, activation record size (see section 9.3.2), if it is a variable
172
185
global or local, in which function was it declared if local, etc.
​ 6.3​ Symbol table considerations
The symbol table can be initialized with some useful information, which can
be stored in a single structure or in several:
Constants: PI, E, NUMERO_AVOGADRO, etc.
Library functions: EXP, LOG, SQRT, etc.
Reserved words. Some lexicographic analyzers do not recognize
reserved words directly, but only recognize
user. Once one of the input is taken, they look for it in the table of reserved words in case it coincides with any; if found, they return the token to the parser associated in the table; if not, they return it as a true identifier. This facilitates work to the lexicographic, moreover, since this table is invariable, it can be stored in a perfect hash table (the one in which all searches are exactly O (1)) thus constituting a more efficient alternative to PCLex or JavaCC as we have seen it.
Figure 6.1. Accesses to the symbol table by the different phases of a compiler
In general, as the analysis stage progresses and new
identifier declarations, the lexical analyzer, or the parser depending on the strategy we follow will insert new entries in the symbol table, avoiding always the existence of repeated entries. The semantic analyzer performs the Context-sensitive checks thanks to the symbol table and, already in the stage For synthesis, the intermediate code generator uses the associated memory addresses to each identifier to generate a program equivalent to the input one. By rule
In general, the code optimizer does not need to make use of it, although nothing prevents can access it.
Although the efficiency in the accesses and manipulation of the symbol table
173
186
Symbol table
are essential to make a good compiler, it is usually convenient to perform a progressive development of it. Thus, in the early phases of the construction of the compiler, given its complexity it is better to include a symbol table as simple as possible possible (e.g. based on a simply chained list) to avoid errors
that are disseminated through the rest of the compiler phases. Once the various phases function properly, all effort can be concentrated on optimizing management from the symbol table. So that this process does not influence the phases already developed compiler, it is necessary to provide the symbol table with a clear interface defined and unchanged from the beginning: in the optimization of the symbol table, modifies its implementation, but maintains its interface. This whole process can see in figure 6 . 1 .
This figure mentions an important distinction between the construction of
compilers for languages  that force the programmer to declare all variables
(C, Modula-2, Pascal, Java, etc.), and languages  in which variables can be used directly without the need to declare them (BASIC, Logo, etc.). When a language It has a declaration area and a statement area, the appearance of an identifier has a very different semantics depending on the area you are in:
If it appears in the declaration area, then you must insert theidentifier in the symbol table, together with the information that know. If the identifier was already in the table, then it has been
A redeclaration has occurred and a semantic error message should be issued.
If it appears in the sentences area, then you have to look for the identifierin the symbol table to control that the use that is being made of it is consistent with its type. If the identifier is not found in the table, then is that it has not been previously declared, which is usually considered an error semantic to inform the programmer.
In these cases, the lexicographic analyzer, when a
identifier does not know in which area it has been found, so it is not easy to include a lexical action that discriminates between performing an insert or a search; So, it must be the parser who does these operations. Thus, the attribute of Identifier token is usually its own name.
However, there are a few programming languages  in which it is not
variables need to be declared, either because there is only a single data type (such as in our calculator example where everything is considered integer values) or because the name of the variable itself serves to discern its type (in BASIC the variables ending in "$" are of type string). In these cases, the lexicographic analyzer can insert the identifier into the symbol table the first time it appears; before inserting it performs a search operation in case
already existed. And whatever the case, you get the entry or index of the table in which it is found or has been included; This index will be the value that the lexical analyzer passes to the syntactic as an attribute of each identifier.
174
187
Finally, it is important to emphasize that the symbol table contains
useful information to be able to compile, and therefore only exists at compile time, and not of execution. However, in an interpreter since compilation and execution occurs at the same time, the symbol table remains in memory for the entire weather.
​ 6.4​ Example: a calculator with variables
At this point, we are going to increase the possibilities of the calculator
proposed in previous sections, including the possibility of manipulating variables. To simplify the design we will not include an area for variable declarations, but that these can be used directly by initializing to 0, since only we have the integer type.
To enter the variables in the calculator we need a new
syntactic construction with which to be able to give it values: it is about the assignment. In addition, we will continue with the expression evaluation and display statement to which we will prepend the reserved word PRINT . In any expression you may intervene a variable that will be evaluated to the value it has at that moment. Thus, before the entrance:
Ø a: = 7 * 3;
Ù b: = 3 * a;
Ú a: = a + b;
you want to store in the variable a the value 21, in the b the value 63 and then modify a to contain the value 84. To achieve our purposes we will use a symbol table in which we will store only the name of each variable, like this as its value since we are dealing with an interpreter. The symbol table will have
Figure 6.2. One of the simplest (and most inefficient) symbol tables that can be built
a simply chained unordered list structure (see figure 6. 2 ), since
clarity in our development takes precedence over efficiency. In developments with Java we will use the HashMap class .
Fig . 6 . 3 shows how the symbol table should be updated to
reflect the proposed assignments. Thus, in the Ø sentence, once the expression 7 * 3 to the value 21 the symbol table will be inspected for the identifier a . Since it is missing, the lexical analyzer will create it by initializing it to 0; then the rule associated with the assignment will modify the input of the a to
175
188
Symbol table
Figure 6.3. Status of the symbol table after executing some assignment statements
assign it the value 21. In the Ù statement, the evaluation of the expression requires searching a in the symbol table to collect its value -21- to multiply it by 3. The result -63- is stored in the input associated with b in the same way as was done for the variable a in the Ø statement. Finally, the Ú statement evaluates a new expression in which a and b intervene , assigning their sum -84- to the entry of a in the table of symbols.
6.4.1 Symbol table interface
As already mentioned, the interface of the symbol table should be
clear from the start so that any changes to the implementation from the symbol table has no impact on compiler stages anymore developed. The basic operations that it must have are:
create () : create an empty table.
insert (symbol) : adds the given symbol to the table.
search (name) : returns the symbol whose name matches the
parameter. If the symbol does not exist, it returns null.
print () : for information purposes, it displays the list of variables stored in the symbol table along with their associated values.
6.4.2 Solution with Lex / Yacc
We assume that there is no declaration area in our language, so
the insertion of the symbols in the table must be done from a lexical action. How attribute of the token ID will use a pointer to its entry in the symbol table.
Semantic actions will use this pointer to access the value of each
variable: if the identifier is to the left of the allocation token , then it is
it will crush the value; and if it is part of an expression, it will be evaluated to the value of the variable.
On the other hand, the attribute of the NUM token is still of type int . Given the
attributes of different types are needed (for NUM and for ID), you will have to declare a
% union in Yacc, of the form:
%Union {

} and terminals and non-terminals of the form:
% token <number> NUM
% token <ptrSymbol> ID
% type <number> expr
Finally, we can choose to allow multiple assignments or not. A
example of multiple assignment is:
a: = b: = c: = 16;
which assigns the value 16 to variables c , b and a in this order. To do this, We will consider that the assignments are given by the following rules:
assign
:
ASIG ID expr
|
ASIG ID assigned
;

can be seen in it, the assigned non-terminal must also have associated as an attribute the field number of % union , in order to propagate the value of the expression and be able to make assignments as you move up the syntax tree.

6.4.4 Solution with JavaCC
The process used to solve the calculator problem in
JavaCC is almost the same as JFlex / Cup only, remember, it is not possible to spend more Attribute of the lexical analyzer to the parser that the lexeme itself read from the input. Of this way, we have chosen to insert into the symbol table in the rule associated with the identifier, rather than in a lexical action.
The most remarkable thing about this proposal is the rule associated with the assignment
multiple:
182
Due to the top-down nature of the JavaCC-generated parser, it is difficult to propagate the value of expr as the tree is built, so it has been chosen by storing in a vector all the objects of type Symbol to which we must assign the expression, and perform this operation once it has been evaluated. An alternative would have been
assign
:: =
<ID> ": =" expr ()
|
<ID> ": =" assign ()
which would fix the problem if assign returns the value of the expression. Note how This solution is to convert the grammar to recursive to the right, although it has the problem being LL (4) because both rules start with <ID> “: =” since both expr and alloc can start with an <ID>.
