Type management
​ 7.1​ Overview
A compiler must check whether the source program follows both the
syntactic conventions such as semantics that describe the source language. Is verification ensures the detection and subsequent communication to the programmer certain bugs that make debugging easier but cannot be
managed from the exclusively syntactic point of view. Of this this kind of errors, the most common are derived from the use of data types, either provided by the language itself (primitive types), or created by the programmer based on syntactic constructions provided by the language (user types). Basically, data types are used precisely for the programmer to declare for what purpose are you going to use each variable (to store an integer, a real number, a character string, etc.), so that the compiler can automatically detect in which situations the programmer has made a mistake (for example, For example, if you tried to add a whole number and a string of characters).
Type management ensures that the type of a syntactic construction
match what is intended in your context. For example, the predefined arithmetic operator mod in Modula-2 requires integer numeric operands, so using the
Type management must ensure that these operands are of type integer. Equal In this way, type management must ensure that dereferencing is applied only to a pointer, that the indexing is done only on an array, that a function the correct number and type of arguments are applied, etc.
The information collected by the type manager is also useful when it comes to
generate code. For example, arithmetic operators typically apply both to integers as to reals, and perhaps other types, and the context of each one of them to determine the memory space required to store calculations intermediate. A symbol or function is said to be overloaded when it represents different operations in different contexts; for example, the sum (expressed by the symbol " + ") is usually overloaded for integers and for reals. The overload can be accompanied by a type conversion, where the compiler provides the operator to convert an operand to the type expected by the context. Consider for example expressions like "x + i" where x is of real type and i is of integer type. Since the representation of integers and reals is different within a computer, and different machine instructions are used for operations on integers and reals, the compiler may have to first convert one of the operators to ensure both operands are of the same type when you have
187
200
Type management
place the sum. The grammar of figure 7 .1 generates expressions consisting of integer and real constants to which the arithmetic operator of addition + applies. When two integers are added the result is integer, and if it is not real. In this way, each subexpression has associated a type and a dependent storage space of this, just as it will have a value associated with it at runtime, as shown illustrated in the tree to the right of this figure.
Figure 7.1. Example of calculation of the type of each subexpression. The sum of integers produces an integer, but if a real value is involved, then the result will be a real
A different notion of overload is that of polymorphism , which appears in
object-oriented languages. In these languages  there is a hierarchy of inheritance in which the more specialized types inherit from the more general types, in a that specialized types may incorporate features that are lacking in general, and can even change the operation of certain methods of the classes ancestors in what is called function rewriting . In these languages, a function is polymorphic when it has general type parameters, but can be executed with arguments of specialized types of the same hereditary line, that is, descendants of the general type, triggering in each case a different action by means of dynamic linking . According to dynamic bonding, in time of execution detects the type of the actual parameter and executes its own methods, in instead of the father's, if they are rewritten. It is one thing for our language to have some overloaded predefined functions, and something else allowing definition of overloaded functions. In the latter case, you have to solve in some way conflicts in the symbol table, since the same identifier for the name of function has associated different parameters and bodies.
​ 7.2​ Nominal, structural and functional compatibility
When you are going to build a new programming language, it turns out It is essential to establish a rigorous and well-documented criterion of compatibility between types, in such a way that the programmers who are going to use that language know what to expect.
We understand that two types A and B are compatible when, given two
expressions e and eto of types b​ A and B respectively, can be used interchangeably in a given context. There are many different contexts, of which the most
188
201
usual is the assignment, so that if the variable v is of type A and a              to assignment of the form v: = e , we will say that to              b              A and B are compatible for assignment. Another context is given by arithmetic operations, such as addition: if a language admits expressions of the form e + e , we will say that to              b A and B are compatible for the sum. In the latter case, types A and B could represent, for example, types int and float of C.
Basically three compatibility criteria can be established between types:
nominal, structural and functional. Nominal compatibility is the strictest, that is, the one that imposes the strongest conditions to decide when two variables can be assign to each other. Functional compatibility is the most relaxed, while structural is at an intermediate level. Of course, the developer of compilers can set other criteria that are midway between any of these compatibilities.
To explain the difference between these criteria we will use the following two
types defined in C:
typedef struct {
char name [20]; char surnames [35]; int age; } employee ; employee e1, e2;
Y
typedef struct {
char name [20]; char surnames [35]; int age; } customer ; client c1, c2;
The customer and employee types have different names, that is, they are different
from the nominal point of view. However, its content or structure is the same, therefore they are equivalent from the structural point of view. In this way, a language with nominal level compatibility prevents making assignments like e1 = c1 , but lets make e1 = e2 , since e1 and e2 belong to exactly the same type
(since there cannot be two types with the same name). A language with
Structural type compatibility allows you to do both assignments, since only requires that the structure of the l-value and the r-value be identical which is true for employee and customer types .
Nominal compatibility has strong implications when declaring
variables of anonymous types. Continuing with the example, we would have:
struct {
char name [20]; char surnames [35];
189
202
Type management
int age;
} e1, e2;
Y
struct {
char name [20]; char surnames [35]; int age;
} c1, c2; where the types of the variables e1 , e2 , c1 and c2 are unnamed. By rule In general, in these situations, a language with nominal type compatibility creates a type with an internal name for each anonymous declaration, which in the example is translates into the creation of two types, one to which e1 and e2 will belong (and which, therefore, will be nominally compatible), and another to which c1 and c2 will belong (which also will be nominally compatible).
On the other hand, functional compatibility does not even require that
expressions have the same structure. Two expressions e and e of types A and B              to              b respectively they are functionally compatible if A and B have different structures but they can be used interchangeably in some context. The most usual case consists of be able to add integer and decimal values. As a general rule, integers are usually represent two's complement, while decimal numbers are represented in floating point. Although the same number of octets are used to represent both, their structure is essentially different, but most
programming allows you to perform arithmetic operations between them. In these languages there is a functional compatibility between the integer type and the decimal type.
Functional compatibility requires the compiler to detect the situations
in which expressions of types with different structures are correctly mixed, by object to generate code that performs the type conversion of the expression minus accurate to the most accurate. For example, when adding integers with decimals, the integers must be transformed to decimals and only then perform the operation. This Automatic operation is called promotion (of the English promotion ).
In object-oriented languages  there is also support for
inheritance: if a class B inherits from another A ( B is an A ), as a general rule it can An object of class B should be used in any situation where an object of type A , since B is an A and therefore meets all the functional requirements for impersonate it.
​ 7.3​ Managing primitive types
At this point, the management of a basic calculator will be studied, when in this
the character string type is included, in addition to the integer type with which it has already been
worked in previous chapters.
7.3.1 Starting grammar

The grammar to be recognized will allow you to manage values  of type integer and of type
character string. Strings can be concatenated by overloading the
operator " + " that, remember, also allows adding integers. Through two functions that are part of the language will allow the conversion between both types. To avoid get lost in unnecessary details, some arithmetic operations have been removed and multiple assignment. Also, to illustrate that carriage return has not always to be considered as a separator, in the proposed grammar it has been used as a terminator, doing the semicolon functions of example grammars previous. The semantic role of each construction is:

Type management
It is possible to assign the value of an expression to a variable. It's not possible do multiple assignments: ASIG ID expr .
You can work with values  of two types: character strings and numbers integers.
The grammar does not have a zone where the programmer can declare the typeof each variable, but it will be defined by the one of the first assignment valid that is done. That is, if the first time the variable x appears, assigns the value 65, so the type of x is integer. The type of a variable it cannot change during the execution of a program.
It is possible to display the value of an expression: PRINT expr . Is statement allows, therefore, to display both integer values  and strings of characters, so if we consider it from a functional point of view, we can say that PRINT is a polymorphic function.
With the integers we will be able to do addition and multiplication. Instead with chains it doesn't make much sense to do any of these operations, although we will use the addition symbol to allow string concatenations. It is not possible to add or concatenate integers and chains.
It is possible to carry out type conversions using functions of theprogramming language. These are different than the pre-built functions (built-ins), which are stored in libraries but whose names are not they are actually part of the syntax of the language. These functions are:
A_String (expr) : has as parameter an expression of type integer and returns the text string that represents it as a result.
A_ENTER (expr) : its parameter is a character type expression which represents a number and returns its integer value as a result.

7.3.2.2 Definition of the symbol table
In this step, the structure of the symbol table is defined, if it is
needed for the problem, as well as its interface.
In this case, we will need the symbol table, since it is
will store the values  of the identifiers. It will also be necessary to save the type of each variable in order to control that its use is consistent with the intentions of your declaration (implicit in the first assignment). In this way, the type of a variable will be stored in the symbol table by a character with the following meaning:
'c': character string type.
'e': integer type.
'i': undefined type. This type will be used in case that in the first assignment an attempt is made to give a variable the value resulting from an erroneous expression.
It seems clear that since there are variables of different types, an input
from the symbol table must be able to store both a string and an integer, in a function of the type of the variable it represents. In C language this is solved by a union structure , while in Java you will have an Object handle that, in
depending on the type of the variable, it will point to an Integer or a String . Fig . 7 .two graphically shows the structure of the symbol table.
Figure 7.2. Situation of the symbol table after entering the sentences of the point 7.3.2.1
7.3.2.3 Attribute mapping
The next step is to study which attribute has to be associated with the
terminals and non-terminals.

​ 7.3.2.3.1​ Terminal attributes
With respect to the NUMBER and CHAIN terminals , since they represent
integer and string constants, it is wise to assign them as attributes the constants they represent. In the case of NUMERO , a
conversion of the lexeme read to the corresponding integer, since we are interested in operate arithmetically with it. As for the character string, it will be necessary to delete the opening and closing quotation marks as they are just delimiters that are not part of the literal itself.
On the other hand, and as has already been commented, since we are defining a
language without an area of  explicit declarations, it will be the lexical analyzer who inserts the identifiers in the symbol table, in order to unload the syntactic of some chores. The terminal attribute ID is therefore an entry in this table. Basically, the lexicographic analyzer, when it encounters an identifier, will
look in the table; if it is, it associates its input as an attribute to the ID found and returns to the syntactic; if it is not, it creates a new entry in the table and returns it to the syntactic. He The problem that arises here is: what is a new entry in the table of symbols? Let's analyze, for example, the first sentence of section 7.3.2.1 :
b = "Comrade Trotsky"
When the lexical analyzer finds the identifier b , it looks for it in the table of symbols (which is initially empty) and, not finding it, creates a new node in it. This node will have a name "b", but what is its type? The type of b depends on the value given in the first assignment (in this case a character string),
but the problem is that the lexical analyzer does not see beyond the lexeme in which finds at this moment, "b", so it is still unable to know the type of the expression behind the assignment terminal. The type of b is implicit in the time when its first assignment is made, but the time the parser Lexical insertion into the symbol table is before the moment it is
know the type of the assigned expression. The solution is that the parser will be the one who puts the type when the first assignment is concluded; meanwhile, the lexicographic will indicate an undefined transitory type: "i". Fig . 7 . 3 shows the contents of the symbol table at the precise moment when the lexical analyzer returns to the syntactic the entry in the symbol table corresponding to the c of the Ú sentence.
Figure 7.3. The table entry marked in red has just been inserted by the parser lexical that, since it does not know the type of the newly inserted variable c , sets it as undefined:

​ 7.3.2.3.2​ Non-terminal attributes
So far, in our calculator example, the nonterminal expr had
as an attribute the integer value it represented. However, in the case that we now occupies, the concept of expression denoted by the nonterminal expr serves to represent both integer and string expressions. This duality looks good
implemented through the concept of union in C, in a similar way to how done on the nodes of the symbol table. In this way, the attribute of expr is a record with a field to indicate its type and a union to indicate its value, either integer or string. In the case of Java, the solution is even simpler since the attribute can be a simple Object ; if said Object is instanceof Integer the type will be whole; if the Object is instanceof String the type will be character string; and if the null the type will be undefined. In any case, for the sake of readability, it is also
It is possible to associate an object with two fields, type and value, as in the solution in C. The figure 7. 5 shows an example of the associated attributes when the Ú sentence of section 7.3.2.1 . 7.3.2.4 Semantic actions
In this section, semantic actions will be studied from two points of
view:
O The type compatibility checks.
O The actions to be taken in case the restrictions of types.
As a general rule, the study of semantic actions is usually done
starting with the rules by which it is first reduced in a syntax tree
anyone, since these are usually the simplest, and this is how the problem is addressed in the same order in which semantic actions will end up being executed. The rules: expr              :              NUMBER
​ |​ CHAIN
are the simplest since the associated semantic action must only assign the type to expr and copy the token attribute as value of expr .
Rule:
​ expr​ :​ expr '*' expr
you must control that the two expressions are of type integer. If they are, the type of the expr The resulting value is also an integer and the value will be the product of the expressions of the consequent. Otherwise, an expression of undefined type will result.
Rule:
​ expr​ :​ expr '+' expr
is a bit more complicated, since the '+' operator is overloaded and with it you can both add integers and concatenate strings. Therefore, the action semantics must check that the types of the antecedent expressions are
equal. If they are integers, an integer expression is produced that is the sum of the other two. If they are character strings produces an expression that is the concatenation of the other two. In any other case an undefined expression is produced.
The rules:
​ expr​ :​ TO_ENTER '(' expr ')'
196
209
​ |​ A_CADENA '(' expr ')'
take, respectively, expressions of type string and integer and return integers and strings, also respectively. In any other case an expression is produced
indefinite. The A_String () function converts the value of its parameter into a string characters that are digits. The A_ENTER () function converts its parameter to a number, as long as the string consists of a sequence of digits; in case Otherwise an undefined expression is also generated.
Rule:
​ expr​ :​ ID
is trivial, since the parser receives the input from the lexicon table
symbols in which the variable resides. Therefore, since the attribute of an expression share part of the structure of a symbol table node, in this action semantics all you have to do is copy said piece (see figure 7 .4).
The semantics of the assignment given by the rule: assign​ :              ASIG ID expr
it's a bit more complex. Remember that the type of a variable is given by the
kind of what is assigned to you first. For example, if in the first assignment to the variable a is assigned the value 7, then its type is integer throughout implementation of the program; if it is assigned "Ana", then it will be of type string. Therefore, the assignment must:
If the type of the l-value is undefined, it is assigned the value and the type of the rvalue, whatever it is.
If the type of the l-value is defined, then:
If the types of the l-value and the r-value coincide then it is assigned to the l-value the value of the r-value.
If the types do not match, an error message is issued and the l-value it is left intact.
Implicit in this explanation is what to do when attempting to assign a
variable an undefined expression: if the variable is still undefined it is left as is; if not, the variable does not become indefinite, but remains as it is. If before a indefinite assignment it occurred to us to put the variable in an undefined state, the sequence:
a = 7
U Works
a = 7 + "hello"
X Fail (and change type from a to undefined)
a = "bad"
U Works (but shouldn't)
I would change the value and type of to , which is wrong with respect to the semantics of our language.
Once the type is assigned to a variable, it is always the same; yes during
execution is assigned a wrong expression, the type of the variable will remain the same itself and its value will not change either. This behavior could be modified adding a boolean variable to the symbol table to tell me if the value of the variable is known or not, in such a way that a wrong assignment would put this
197
210
Type management
variable to false , as illustrated in figure 7. 7 .
Figure 7.7. Inclusion of a field that indicates that, Despite knowing the type of the variable, its value is unknown because the last assignment was wrong

The solution with these two tools obeys the same mechanisms that
the previous one, with Lex / Yacc. Perhaps the most notable difference is the treatment of the type of an expression, or of a variable. In Java we take advantage of the Object class to say that the attribute of an expression will be an Object that will point to an Integer , a String or to null in case the value is undefined. The type () function declared in scope of Cup's semantic actions produces the character 'e',  'c' or 'i' depending on whatever an attribute actually points to.
On the other hand, in the function TO_ENTER () we don't need a function in
Java to tell us if the string that is passed as a parameter has a number format or not, since this can be known by catching the exception NumberFormatException at the time of conversion.

​ 7.4​ Complex type management
Once the primitive types have been studied, in this section we will study the
syntactic and semantic characteristics of a compiler that allows the programmer build complex data types, pointers, arrays , etc.
When a programming language supports complex types, it must
supply syntactic constructs both for constructing new types and for reference them. Some examples of type constructors are:
POINTER TO. To create pointers.
RECORD OF. To create records.
210
223
ARRAY OF. To create tables and arrays.
PROCEDURE RETURNS. To create functions that return a result.
These are not really types but type constructors since, although
they allow to build complex types, they do not make sense by themselves and must be applied on a base type that can be another type constructor or a type primitive. Type constructors are therefore recursive. The registry case is more special because it can be applied to Cartesian product of various types.
All this leads us to two fundamental differences regarding the management
of primitive types that was done in the previous section. First, the language to define requires a declaration area where to enumerate the variables of a
program and indicate its type. In other words, the type of a variable will no longer be given for its first assignment but it must be explicitly declared. And second
Second, since a complex type can be arbitrarily long, it cannot
be encoded with a single letter ('e' for integers, 'c' for strings, etc.) but instead some other method will have to be used.
On the other hand, when using a declared variable of any of the types
above, you need to use a type modifier to reference the components. For example:
^. Placed next to a pointer, it represents what is being pointed to.
.countryside. Placed next to a record, it represents one of its fields.
[]. Placed next to an array, it represents one of its elements.
(). Placed next to a function, it invokes it and represents the value of shoot.
As can be seen, each type constructor has its own modifier
of type and these are not interchangeable, that is, the “^” can only be applied to pointers nothing more, the "." it can only be applied to records, etc. This gives us a table that one-to-one relates each type constructor with its modifier:
​ Type constructors​ Type modifiers
​ POINTER TO​ ^
​ RECORD OF​ .countryside
​ ARRAY OF​ []
​ PROCEDURE RETURNS​ ()
It is also important to realize that a type modifier does not have
why apply exclusively to a variable, but can be applied to a
expression that has, in turn, another type modifier, eg: "ptr ^ .name" refers to to the record name field pointed to by ptr , and the modifier "." has been applied to the expression ptr ^ and not to a variable directly.
7.4.1 Objectives and proposal of an example
In the following points, the management of the following complex types will be created: 211
224
Type management
Pointers
Arrays of unknown dimensions.
Functions without parameters.
The fact of not knowing the dimensions of the arrays and of ignoring the
function parameters will allow us to focus exclusively on managing types. On the other hand, we will support the basic types:
Logical or Boolean.
Whole.
Real.
Character.
In total we have four primitive types and three type constructors. Is It is important to note that for each primitive type it is necessary to supply the programmer some mechanism with which he can define constants of each of these types: Boolean , integer, real, and character constants .
In what follows we will build the type management of a compiler, which
means that we will put the calculator example aside and focus on in recognizing valid statements and expressions. To check that our compiler is working fine, every time the programmer enters a expression should show us a message where, in natural language, the type of that expression. The following is an example input:
a: POINTER TO BOOLEAN;
a ^;
It is a boolean.
to;
It is a pointer to a boolean.
beta: POINTER TO ARRAY [] OF POINTER TO PROCEDURE (): INTEGER; beta ^;
It is an array of a pointer to a function that returns an integer.
beta [2];
I was expecting an array.
beta();
I was expecting a function.
true;
It is a boolean.

recognize the different clauses, and propose the attributes that each terminal should have and non-terminal to, based on semantic actions, culminate with our goal of control compound data types.

7.4.2.2 Attribute management
The management of the attributes of the terminal symbols is not very important
complexity. The tokens that represent constants do not need any attribute since that our goal is not to process values  or generate equivalent code. For another On the other hand, as our language defines a mechanism to make explicit the variable declarations, it is best if the lexical analyzer provides the
syntactic the names of the variables so that it inserts them or looks for them in the table symbols depending on whether they appear in a statement or not.
A more complex problem is how to identify the type of a
complex variable (since we have seen that it is not possible to do it using a single lyrics). In what follows, suppose the user defines the following variable:
alpha: POINTER TO ARRAY [23] OF POINTER TO PROCEDURE (): ARRAY
[2] OF INTEGER;
Unquestionably we will need a symbol table to store
the name of each variable and its type. Also, the guy has to be stored in some intelligent way that later helps us to detect errors and that allows us to know what Data modifier can be applied and which not. The way you decide to save the type of a variable should also be used to store the type of an expression once a sequence of type modifiers has been applied to a variable valid.
The solution is going to consist of coding each type or type constructor with a
letter, including the undefined type that we will represent with a 'u' of undefined :

Figure 7.9. Coding of a complex type and structure of the symbol table
alpha variable and the structure of this variable in the symbol table.
The stack has been built by looking at the type from right to left, which ensures
that at the top of the stack there is the main type of alpha : alpha is, first and foremost, a pointer
(represented by the 'p'). It is for this reason that at the point a
recursive grammar to the right since the reductions will be done in the order in which they are they must push items onto the stack.
Also, if an expression has a type stack associated as an attribute It is very easy to find out which modifier can be applied to it:
^, if the top is 'p'.
[NUM], if the top is 'a'.
(), if the top is 'f'.
And not only that is easy, it is also easy to calculate the type stack of a
expression after applying a type modifier to it. For example, if the variable gamma is of type POINTER TO X then gamma ^ is of type X , that is, just remove the top of the type stack associated with gamma to get the type stack of the expression gamma ^ .
7.4.2.3 Implementing a type stack
215
228
Type management
The proposed character stack can be implemented in many different ways
ways. In our case, an array will act as a stack so that position 0
will store the number of elements and these will be placed from position 1 onwards, the highest position corresponding to the top of the stack. With 20 positions it will be enough. The structure of the alpha type stack is shown in the figure7 .10 .
Figure 7.10. Storage structure of the type the alpha variable
Stacking, unstacking and top operations are very simple with this
structure. Suppose the type variable is char [20] , then:
stack (char x) . It consists of increasing type [0] and then putting x in type [type [0]]; in a single statement: type [++ type [0]] = x;
unstack () . It consists only of decreasing the length of type [0], leaving type [0] -;
char top () . Position 0 gives us the position of the top, then in a single statement: return type [type [0]];
Although this will be the method we will use, it is evident that we are
wasting memory when storing types. This should not concern us given the amounts of memory that are handled today. In case memory was a problem would have to use some kind of bit-based encoding. Since in
In total we have 8 letters to represent, 3 bits would be enough to represent each one of them, as shown in the following table:
​ Types and constructors​ Bit code
POINTER TO​ p - 100 ARRAY [] OF​ a - 101
​ PROCEDURE ():​ f - 110
​ BOOLEAN​ b - 111
​ INTEGER​ i - 001
​ REAL​ r - 010
​ CHAR​ c - 011
​ UNDEFINED​ u - 000
Also, assuming the excess storage space is filled
with 0s and since code 000 has been associated with the undefined type, it is possible not store the size of the stack but to find the top it will be enough to look for the first sequence of three bits different from 000. If this does not exist, the type is
216
229
undefined.
According to this encoding, the codes 111 , 001 , 010 , 011 and 000 can only be
be at the bottom of the pile; what's more, codes 100 , 101 and 110 can never be in the base since they require another type below on which to apply. This fact allows us to use the same codes to represent both primitive types and type constructors: if it is at the base of the stack, it refers to a type primitive and otherwise what is referenced is a constructor. Yes, besides
we represent the types by means of an integer pointer, the undefined type would be given by a pointer to null , so the encoding would be:

where lines with the same color have the same bit code associated with them. Note how again it is not possible to use code 00 to represent a constructor of type as long as you do not want to save the length of the type and unused bits are fill to 0.
There can be no confusion, since what is at the bottom of the stack can only be
be a primitive type; in figure 7 .11 shows how the alpha type would look encoded with a 16-bit integer.

7.4.2.4 Semantic actions
For semantic actions we are going to focus on the grammar given through
of production rules. In the language we are defining, there is a zone of declarations and variable usage area (although they can be intermixed) by Therefore it is the parser who inserts the identifiers in the table of symbols at the time they are declared.
As can be seen in the prog rules , there is an epsilon rule that
217
230
Type management
allows you to enter through it right at the beginning of the analysis, as the base step of the recursion of the prog rules on itself.
In the decl and type rules , we do recursive grammar to the right. Thus, the type rules allow to build the stack from the base to the top, executing just stack operations . On the other hand, decl rules allow you to assign the type to each of the declared identifiers, also from right to left, just as illustrated in figure 7 .12 . As can be seen in it, when the analyzer syntactic encounters a primitive type builds a stack with a single character (the corresponding to the type found) and assigns it as an attribute of the non-terminal type . TO as it is reduced to new type nonterminals based on the recursive rules to the
right, the attribute of the consequent type is taken as a starting point for construct the attribute of the antecedent; just add the corresponding character to it a type constructor that the case (marked in gray in Figure 7 . 12). Once constructed the full type, the parser should reduce by the rule:
​ decl:​ ID ':' type
such that an entry must be created in the symbol table for the variable
indicated as ID attribute ( c according to figure7 . 12 ); the type of this variable will come given by the type attribute . Of course it is necessary to check that it has not performed a variable redeclaration. Since it is possible to make declarations of
several variables simultaneously, the rest of variables ( b and a according to the figure7. 12 ) it recognize by reducing the rule:
​ decl:​ ID ',' decl
Figure 7.12. Construction of a stack-type and assignment to each variable in the symbol table
218
231
Obviously the process must be very similar to that of the previous rule, that is, create a input in symbol table for ID attribute variable and assign a stack to it
of types. Of course, for this it is necessary to have the type stack as an attribute of the non-terminal decl , so that stack must be propagated in both
production from the non-terminal of the consequent to the antecedent, that is, to decl . In conclusion, both type and decl have a type stack as attributes.
Let us now go on to study the rules of expressions. The goal is that a
expression also has a type stack associated with it so that, once completed parsing the entire expression, its type stack is passed as a parameter to a function that translates its content into a natural language sentence.
Expressions can be divided into three blocks: 1) primitives, formed by
a constant of any type; 2) simple, formed by a variable; and 3) complex, made up of a simple expression with type modifiers to its right.
Expressions of type 1 have a type stack associated with a single
character, which will correspond to the type of the recognized literal:
'i' for NUM.
'r' for NUMREAL.
'c' for CHARACTER.
'b' for CTELOGICA.
Expressions of type 2 have a type stack exactly the same as
of the variable they represent. If the variable has not been declared then the stack types will only have a 'u' representing the undefined type (you cannot use the letter 'i' because it would be confused with the 'i' of INTEGER ).
219
232
Type management
Expressions of type 3 start from an expression (which has a stack of types
associated) along with a type modifier. In this case, check that the type main expression (represented by the top of its type stack), it supports with the type modifier, as seen at the end of the section 7.4.2.2 . For example, if we find “expr ^”, “expr [NUM]” or “expr ()”, check that the type
Alpha's principal is 'p', 'a' or 'f' respectively. Assuming that the variable a has been declared as it appears in the figure 7. 12 , the figure shows how the stack of types during recognition of the expression "a ^ [3]".
The moment it reduces by the rule: expr​ :​ ID
the symbol table is searched for the variable that is given as an ID attribute ; a once found, the attribute of expr will be a copy of the type stack of that variable.
As the rules apply:
​ expr​ :​ expr '^'
​ |​ expr '[' NUM ']'
​ |​ expr '(' ')'
;
it is verified that the modifier of the applied rule is compatible with the type
from the top of the stack of the expression to which it is applied. If compatible, then the type of the resulting expression (the antecedent) will be the same as that of the consequent but without the top. If an error message should not be issued and the resulting expression will be undefined type.
In short, type constructors build the stack by inserting characters
in it, while type modifiers destroy the stack by removing characters by the top.

On the other hand, and as already indicated, an object of type Stack to store the type stack. Note how, as a general rule, is used
structural sharing, that is, no copies of the type stack are generated. For example, during a declaration, the type stack is built by finding a primitive type and as type constructors are found, elements are added to the stack original, without creating copies of it (pure assignment is used and not the clone () function ). The only case where you need to work with a copy of a type stack is when retrieving the type of an identifier as a base expression on which to apply post modifiers (see line 82); if a clone () was not made in this In this case, each time a type modifier is applied, the type of the identifier (and of all those that were declared next to it).

Chapter 8 Code generation
​ 8.1​ Overview
According to the architecture model of a compiler in which it is divided into
frontend and backend , the initial stage translates a source program to a representation intermediate stage from which the final stage generates the object code, either in the form of machine or assembler code. The details of the object language are confined to the stage final, if this is possible, making it easy to reuse the frontend to create other compilers of the same language but that generate code for other platforms. Of this way, although a priori it may be easier to translate a source program directly to the object language, the two main advantages of using a form
Figure 8.1. Building a compiler using stage division frontend and backend stage is done using intermediate code independent of the target machine and in which the source program is encoded. The resulting intermediate code program is the output of the frontend stage and the entrance to the backend
235
248
Code generation
intermediate independent of the target machine are:
Retargeting is facilitated, that is, you can create a compiler for adifferent machine by linking a final stage for the new machine to a stage existing initial.
A code optimizer can be applied to the intermediate representationindependent of the machine, which also allows reuse of this phase of the compiler regardless of the target machine.
Fig . 8 .1 shows how the developer should choose to create a
compiler through a versatile layout or through an easy layout. The final choice It depends on the utility that is going to be given to the short compiler source code or medium term, that is, if you are going to create several compilers or just one.
The versatile philosophy in compiler construction reaches its extreme in
the implementation of languages  that are compiled and pseudo-interpreted in execution. This means that at compile time a code is generated own machine of a virtual microprocessor (called P-code in UCSD Pascal, bytecodes in Java, etc.) which, in turn, is interpreted at runtime via what is called the execution engine . These languages  cannot be classified as interpreted since what is interpreted at run time is not exactly the source program; but neither can they be considered fully compiled since they are that is generated at compile time is not exactly machine code. Is
Design philosophy has the advantage that it facilitates the portability of programs. He The most current language that works with this philosophy is Java, which uses .class files , instead of .exe . .Class files contain bytecodes that are subjected to a Java Virtual Machine (JVM) at runtime, for you to interpret. The JVM does the virtual microprocessor and the bytecodes act as instructions machine. Of course, to be able to execute the bytecodes on different platforms it is Each of them must have an adapted JVM implementation.
This chapter shows how you can use the analysis methods
syntax-driven for translating a source program to a target program equivalent written in intermediate code. Since variable declarations do not generate code, the following sections focus on the sentences specific to a imperative programming language, especially assignments and clauses control of the execution flow. Generation of intermediate code can be interleaved in the parsing by means of the appropriate semantic actions.
It is also important to note that sometimes it can be interesting to build
a source-source translator instead of a full compiler. For example, yes we have a C compiler and we want to build a Pascal compiler,
it may be much more convenient to build a program that translates from Pascal to C, so that, once the C program equivalent to Pascal's has been obtained, it will suffice to compile this one to get the desired result. Basically, the techniques
you will see in this chapter to generate intermediate code can also be applied to the high-level code generation.
236
249
​ 8.2​ Triplet code
In order to facilitate the understanding of the code generation phase,
we will not focus on the pure machine code of any specific microprocessor, but in an intermediate code close to any of them. This approximation It will also facilitate the optimization of the generated code.
Each of the instructions that we can generate has a maximum of
four sections:
1st operand (memory address where the first operand is).
2nd operand (memory address where the second operand is).
Operator (operation code)
Result (memory address where to store the result, or to which to jumpin case it is a jump operation).
We will assume that both direct addressing and
Immediate, that is, the address of an operand can be substituted for the operand itself. These types of instructions are called 3-way codes, triplets, or codes of maximum 3 operands.
In essence, triplets are very similar to any assembly code,
there are operations to add, subtract, etc. There are also instructions for control the flow of execution, and symbolic labels may appear in the middle of the code in order to identify the destination of the jumps.
Not all instructions have to have exactly all the
mentioned sections. By way of introduction, the triplets that we can use are:
Binary assignment: x: = y op z , where op is a binary arithmetic operation or logic. Although there must be different operations depending on the type of data with which to work (it is not the same to add integers than to add real ones), to facilitate our study we will assume that we only have the type whole.
Unary assignment: x: = op y , where op is a unary operation. The Major unary operations include unary minus, logical negation, the bit shift operators and the bit shift operators types.
Simple assignment or copy: x: = y , where the value of y is assigned to x .
Unconditional jump: goto tag .
Conditional jumps: if x or relational and goto label .
As can be deduced from the proposed triplets, the memory addresses
will be managed symbolically, that is, through names instead of numbers. Of course, to generate triplets closer to a machine code Generally, each of these names must be translated into a memory address only. The memory addresses would be taken consecutively taking into account it counts the size of the type of data that each one of them is supposed to house; by 237
250
Code generation
For example, you can assume that an integer value occupies 16 bits, a real one occupies 32 bits, a character occupies 8 bits, etc.
With the above triplets we will cover all the examples proposed in the
this chapter. However, these triplets do not include all the possibilities of basic execution contemplated by a current microprocessor. For example, for calling a subroutine you have triplets to put the parameters on the stack calls, to invoke the subroutine indicating the number of parameters passed to it, to take a parameter from the stack, and to return:
param x : puts the real parameter x on the stack.
call p, n : calls the subroutine that begins at label p , and tells it to
take n parameters from the top of the stack.
pop x :​ takes a parameter from the stack and stores it in the x address .
return y : returns the value y .
Other triplets allow managing the indirect addressing and indexing:
Indexed addressing: triplets are of the form y: = x [i] and x [i]: = y , where x is the base direction, and i is the displacement.
Indirect addressing: triplets are of the form y: = & x and x: = * y , where the symbol & means "the address of ..." and the symbol * means say "the value of the address contained in the address ...".
The choice of allowable operators is an important aspect in design
intermediate code. The set of operators must be rich enough to allow to implement all source language operations. A set of small operators is easier to implement in a new target machine, but if it is Too limited can force the initial stage to generate long sequences of instructions for some source language operations. In such a case, the optimizer and the code generator will have to work harder if it is to produce good code.
Below is an example of code for triplets that stores in
the variable c the sum of a and b (note that b is destroyed as a consequence of this calculation): c = a
label etqLoop if b = 0 goto etqFin b = b-1 c = c + 1 goto etqLoop
label etqFin
​ 8.3​ A simple compiled calculator
To illustrate how the code of triplets is used in a grammar we are going to
suppose that our calculator instead of being an interpreted calculator is a
compiled calculator, that is, instead of interpreting the expressions we are going to generate equivalent intermediate code.
238

8.3.1 Construction steps
Before starting to code the lexical and parsers it is necessary
state exactly what you want to do and with what grammar. For this you must
propose a preliminary example of what the calculator should do to, at then create the grammar that recognizes the language, assign the attributes necessary and, once the purpose of the semantic actions is clear, begin the coding.
8.3.1.1 Proposal of an example
The goal is for the calculator to produce an output text before a text from
entry. For example, if the input is:
a: = 5 * b; c: = b: = d: = a * (4 + v); c: = c: = c;
the output should be: tmp1 = 5 * b a = tmp1 tmp2 = 4 + v tmp3 = a * tmp2 d = tmp3 b = d c = b c = c c = c
which is the equivalent triplet code.

On the other hand, the completion of each sentence is done with a semicolon
';', instead of carriage return, which is closer to the actual treatment of current programming languages.

8.3.1.3 Important considerations about the translator
To facilitate the reading of the resulting intermediate code, use will be made of
variable identifiers instead of directly using their address memory. In any case, do not forget that the symbol table must contain, together with the identifier of each variable, the memory address of each of them, in order to carry out the corresponding translation at the time of generating the final machine code.
The grammar to be used will be practically the same as the one used
in previous sections. Remember that in each rule in which an operator appears arithmetic, only two expressions are involved, which fits with the generation of a
240
253
binary assignment triplet, in which there are only two operands and one result. Without However, the reduction for each of these rules represents an intermediate result on the way to calculate the final result, as illustrated in Figure 8 .3 .
These intermediate results should be stored somewhere so that the
triplets can work with them. For this purpose temporary variables are used automatically generated by the compiler in the amount that is necessary. The figure 8. 2 shows the triplet code to be generated for an assignment that
has as r-value the expression of the figure 8 . 3 , as well as the temporary variables that you need to generate at every step. As is evident, the code of triplets resulting is equivalent to source code.
Figure 8.3. Intermediate results occur each time reduces by a production rule, and they intervene decisively in the calculation of the final result
Through the mechanism of temporary variables it is possible to build, step
by step, any complex arithmetic calculation, in which parentheses can intervene,
Figure 8.2. Representation of the temporary variable to be generated in each reduction, and the triplet that must occur. The ultimate goal of these triplets is to store in the variable a the value 3 + 5 + 7 + 8 , which is achieved based on the temporary variables

Code generation
other variables, etc. It is also interesting to note that the temporary variables they can be reused in different calculations. However, this is an optimization that we will not implement for the sake of concentrating exclusively on generating correct code.
For the realization of our calculator, we must observe two very aspects
important:
Properly manage attributes. In the calculator there is no part of declarations or type checking, since all variables are assumed are of type numeric and that are declared at the moment they do their First apparition. This is why a symbol table will no longer be necessary.
that, in addition, the identifiers of the variables will appear in the triplets, and not the memory addresses they represent. This also allows us bypass a memory address manager that assigns them as that new variables appear.
Carry out a printf sequence . Our purpose is to make a translation textual, in which you enter a text representing high-level assignments, and Another text comes out that represents the triplets. Therefore our actions semantics will aim to perform a sequence of printf or
System.out.println to display these output texts. For this reason, in
compile time, expressions will not be evaluated or performed calculations of any kind. Our translator is solely responsible for managing text strings; This means that, for example, the attribute associated with the token NUM does not have to be of type int, it just needs to be a char [] or a String, since it will not be manipulated arithmetically.
In building a real compiler, the output is usually not text, but
a binary file that contains the machine code grouped by blocks that will be managed by a linker to obtain the final executable file.
Likewise, semantic actions must perform all the necessary type management before proceeding to code generation, leading to the need for a table of complete symbols.
8.3.1.4 Required attributes
As already mentioned, all the management of the entry code will be
symbolic, in the sense that you are only going to work with text strings. This form, both terminal ID and terminal NUM will have a string attribute as that stores the lexeme they represent.
As for expressions, it makes sense to think that they must also have
as an attribute to a character string that somehow represents the chunk of syntactic tree of which this expression is the root. Taking up, for example, the tree of figure 8 .2 , you can think of assigning to an expression the text that represents each variable, whether temporary or not; if we associate the same type of attribute to the non-terminal 242
255
assign , then it will also be possible to generate triplet code even for the multiple assignments. Regarding expressions that represent a single value numeric, you can choose to generate a temporary variable to store them, or save the numeric lexeme as an attribute of the expression, which leads to a optimization regarding the number of generated triplets and temporary variables used. The figure8 . 4 .a) shows the strict case where it is only allowed as an attribute the name of a variable, and figure 8 . 4 .b) shows the relaxed case. In both situations the attribute type is the same. In the solution proposed below case b) is chosen, since it involves optimization without any effort on the part from the developer.
Finally, figure 8. 5 shows attribute propagation in the case of
perform a multiple assignment. As mentioned before, the generation of code is made possible by using an attribute to the non-terminal assign that Figure 8.4. Assignment of attributes to terminals and non-terminals of the calculator grammar. In both cases the type of the attributes is the

The semantic actions are extremely simple, as there is no table
of symbols, nor to have type checking. They just need to be managed properly the attributes, propagating them when necessary, generating the temporary variables and perform the necessary printf .

​ 8.4​ Code generation in control statements
After studying the general mechanism of code generation in
arithmetic expressions, the next step is to approach the study of the code of triplets equivalent to high-level statements for control of the execution flow. These types of statements include the WHILE and REPEAT loops, as well as the IF and CASE conditional statements.
As we saw in the section 8.2 , the control of the execution flow is performed
low level through the goto and call triplets . In this text we will not go into detail about subroutine management, so we will focus exclusively on the goto unconditional and conditional.
8.4.1 Starting grammar
Our purpose is to recognize programs that allow us to carry out
assignments to variables of integer type, and make flow changes based on the condition assessment. Conditions can be simple or complex; in the simple only relational operators appear: > , < , > = , etc .; in the complex ones can use the logical operators AND , OR, and NOT in order to concatenate most basic conditions.
As can be seen, loops of type WHILE and of type
REPEAT . In the former, a condition is evaluated before entering the body of the loop; if this is true, the body is executed and the process begins again; in case
Otherwise, it is continued by the next statement after the WHILE . Therefore the body executes as long as the condition accompanying the WHILE is true . On the other hand, the REPEAT loop evaluates the condition after execution of the body, so that the body is runs until the condition of the loop is true .
As conditional statements, the use of the IF clause is allowed, which allows
execute a block of code optionally, as long as the condition is true . Likewise, an IF can be accompanied by an optional ELSE part that is it would execute if the condition was false. On the other hand, it has also been included the CASE statement that evaluates an arithmetic expression and compares it with each of the elements of a default list of values; case of coinciding with any of they then execute the code block associated with that value. If it doesn't match none of them, happens to execute (optionally) the code block indicated by the
OTHERWISE clause . Finally, it has been decided not to include the FOR statement with so that the reader tries to code his own solution, since it is an exercise interesting.
To illustrate the pure mechanisms of code generation, and not to intermix
in semantic actions no action related to the management of symbol tables
250
263
nor type control, it has been decided that our little language only has the type integer and that it is not necessary to declare variables before use.
The grammar to be used, in production rules format, is:
​ prog​ :​ prog sent ';'
​ |​ prog error ';'
​ |​ / * Epsilon * /

It should be noted that the body of a WHILE , REPEAT , IF , etc. this
formed by a single sentence. If you want to include more than one, the mechanism consists in resorting to the compound sentence. A compound statement is made up of a list of statements delimited by braces, as indicated by the rule:
sent: '{' sent_list '}'
Furthermore, nothing prevents entering as the body of any sentence of
flow control to another flow control statement. What's more, the nesting of Judgments can occur at any level of depth. This fact makes pay particular attention to error control. As can be seen in the
grammar, there are two error rules, one at the program level and the other at the sent_list , that is, in the body of a compound statement.
If a syntactic error occurs inside a compound statement, and
if you only had the error rule at the program level, a recovery would be made
incorrect, as the panic mode mechanism would pull items from the stack until find prog ; then it would extract tokens until it finds the semicolon. And finally would continue the parsing process thinking that it has recovered the error conveniently and that the following statements are at the program level, which is false since it was inside a compound sentence. This problem is
illustrated in the figure 8 . 6 . To avoid this it is necessary to introduce a new error rule at the compound statement level.
Figure 8.6. Need for two error rules when using compound statements

This grammar has two differences with respect to that expressed with rules of
production. First of all the non-terminal list_sent has disappeared, since syntactically it is equivalent to prog (which we have called grammar here ). It us leads to needing a single error handling in the last rule of the nonterminal sent Finished . Obviously this could have also been done in grammar
rule-based, but it has been preferred to illustrate the management of several error rules in one same grammar.
The second difference lies in the management of conditions through a
descending grammar. Before a sequence of characters such as: "((((27 * 8) ..." is It is necessary to know the content of the ellipsis in order to decide whether the three first parentheses are associated with an expression or a condition: if we find the character ">" the parentheses are associated with a condition, but if we find "7) ”then, at least, the third parenthesis is of an expression and we would still have to follow looking to see what the other two parentheses belong to. This problem has three clearly differentiated solutions:
Establish an infinite lookahead .
Differentiate the groupings of expressions and those of conditions; by For example, expressions can be grouped in parentheses, and conditions in brackets. This has been the solution adopted in this example.
Consider that a condition is an expression of a logical or Boolean type . This is the most widely extended decision, although it has not been chosen in this case since it implies a type management on which we do not want to center.

8.4.3 Condition management
All flow changes supported by the language we just defined
they are conditional. In other words, based on the veracity or falsity of a condition, the program follows its flow of execution by one statement or another. Is by This means that the code that we decide to generate to evaluate a condition will be decisive to manage the flow in the different control statements.
In this regard, there are two fundamental possibilities. Perhaps the simplest
is to consider that the conditions are special expressions of logical type and
257
270
Code generation
whose evaluation value is 0 or 1 (false or true respectively) and is stored in a temporary variable as it happens with expressions of type integer. The statement in which this condition is framed will ask for the value of the variable time to decide where the flow continues. For example, the rule:
sent: IF cond THEN sent optional FIN IF
​ 8​ 8​ 8
​ Î​ Ï​ Ð
would establish in an action located at point Î a triplet of the type
if tmpXX = 0 goto etqFalse
where tmpXX is the variable in which the result of having evaluated the condition. If its value is different from 0, that is when the condition is true, then the jump does not occur and the execution flow continues, which corresponds to the IF body statement. The result is that this statement is only executed when the condition is true. As optional it corresponds to the body of the ELSE (possibly empty), as this is exactly where you should jump in case
the condition is false. In any case, once the body of the IF has been executed, it must jump behind the FIN IF , in order not to execute in sequence the optional part of the
ELSE . Therefore, at point Ï the triplets must be placed: goto etqFINIF
label etqFalse:
Finally, the end tag of the IF must be placed after the complete statement, in point Ð: label etqFINIF:
with what the complete code generated would be:
// Code that evaluates cond and puts its value in tmpXX if tmpXX = 0 goto etqFalse
// Code of the IF statement goto etgFINIF

To achieve this purpose, it is essential to insert actions
semantic interspersed in the consequent of a semantic action. In addition, Tags that have been identified here as etqFalso and etqFINIF must be, in actually, any labels generated ad hoc by our compiler, for example etq125 and etq563 respectively.
In any case, this condition assessment mechanism incurs
a decrease in efficiency as composite conditions must be evaluated full. To avoid this problem without thinning the code, a technique is used that allows the evaluation of conditions using short circuit. For example, in a compound condition of the form cond OR cond , only the second will be evaluatedone              two condition if the first is false since, otherwise, it can be ensured that the
​ OR result will be true regardless of the value of cond. One thing              two
similar happens with the AND . This will be the method that we will use, and that is explained
258
271
in detail below.
8.4.3.1 Condition evaluation using short circuit
The mechanism is that each cond generates a block of code that
has two jumps ( goto ): a jump to a label A if the condition is met, and a jump to another label B if the condition is not met.
Generating this code for simple conditions is trivial. From
they will study the short circuit in compound conditions.

8.4.4.1 IF-THEN-ELSE statement
The case of the IF statement is the simplest. Here it is sufficient to indicate that the
The condition's truth tag is associated with the code after the THEN , and the false label is associated with the code that may be behind the ELSE . In any
In this case, once the THEN sentences have been completed, a jump must occur at the end of the
IF , because we don't want the ELSE statements to be executed as well . So, after the THEN sentences , we create a new label to which we will produce a jump, and we place the destination of that tag at the end of the IF code . This can best appreciated with an example:

Intermediate code

S1: = 1;
ELSE
S2: = 2;
END IF
if A> 0 goto etq1 goto etq2
label etq1: S1 = 1 goto etq3
label etq2:
S2 = 2 label etq3:

On the other hand, etqFINIF cannot be declared in the semantic action in

So you need to declare it somewhere so that it is global enough

It is noteworthy that a metacompiler how JavaCC solves this problem using the code area associated with each BNF rule; here you could declare the variable etqFINIF and use it in any action semantics of that same rule. If there are nested IFs , the recursion itself in the calls to the functions that represent non-terminals would be responsible for creating new scopes with copies of that variable.
The solution adopted by JavaCC can give us some clue to solve
the problem in PCYacc: we need a variable local to the full rule, which is you can use in all your semantic actions, but make it different if there is IF nested. The solution is to use the IF token as a container for said variable, assigning it as an attribute: the IF token attribute is accessible by the semantic actions behind it, and if there are, for example, three nested IFs , each of them will have its own attribute. 8.4.4.2 WHILE statement
The case of WHILE and REPEAT is very similar. In both it is necessary
place a label at the beginning of the loop, which will be skipped to repeat each iteration.
In the case of WHILE , the condition code is generated below, since
that this must be evaluated before entering to execute the body of the loop. The label of truth is located just before the sentences that make up the body, which is what is must execute if condition is true. At the end of the sentences a jump will be made to start of the loop, where the condition will be checked again. Fake label
the condition, will be located at the end of everything related to the WHILE , or what is same, at the beginning of the code generated for the statements that follow the WHILE . Of this way, when the condition is no longer met, the execution of the sequence of statements that follow the WHILE statement .
Remember that the production rule associated with WHILE is:
sent: WHILE cond DO sent FIN WHILE
so the blocks of code that we have to build properly our flow are those that appear shaded in the figure 8 . 19 . The rest of the figure

properties very similar to those already explained in the previous point with respect to the IF statement . In other words, it will be stored as an attribute of the WHILE token itself .
In this way, the block diagram exactly follows the semantics of a
WHILE loop :
The condition is checked.
If it is true, it executes sent and returns to the previous point, to check theone condition.
If it is false, the loop is exited and the flow continues through the sentence that follows the
WHILE.

8.4.4.3 REPEAT statement
Let's remember the production rule associated with the REPEAT statement :
sent: REPEAT sent UNTIL cond
in which you can see how the condition is evaluated after having executed at least a cycle. The body will run as long as the condition is false, or what is the same, until the condition is met.
For the intermediate code generated to behave in the same way,

ideally, the condition's false label should be placed at the beginning of the body, and that the label of certain allows the sequential flow of the program to continue.
Thus, following the false label, the code of the
statements, since the condition is evaluated last. After the sentences we place the condition code. Since the false label has already been put at the beginning of the loop, it only remains to put the truth label after the condition code, which will give an ideal block diagram like the one presented in figure 8 .20 .
Figure 8.20. Ideal block diagram of a REPEAT statement
However, managing to generate code that obeys the diagram in the figure
8 . 20 It is not feasible, since the false label cannot be placed before body sentences as their value is not yet known. In other words, the label of false is generated when the condition is reduced, which happens after we need to visualize your label . From another point of view, when you get to know the value of the label false, it is too late to display its label , since it had to be done even before reducing body sentences. Note how the following
action is incorrect because an attempt is made to access an attribute that is unknown in the moment of executing said action:
sent: REPEAT​ {printf ("label% s: \ n", $ 5.etqFalso ); } sent UNTIL cond
To solve this problem we do what is called an indirection, that is,
create a new REPEAT start tag and then create a block of code in which we place in sequence:
The label of the false label.
A goto to the REPEAT start tag.
The block diagram would be as illustrated in the figure 8. 21 . This code
so inefficient it could be optimized at a later stage.

The semantic actions associated with the REPEAT production rule
would remain:

8.4.4.4 CASE statement
The CASE statement is the most complex to translate, since it allows a
indeterminate and potentially infinite number of CASE clauses within it, which which forces to drag a series of attributes as they are carried out
reductions. The semantic objective of this sentence is the same as that of the sentence
C language switch , only a break clause is not necessary to break the flow at the end of the sentence associated with each particular case.
On the other hand, and from a grammatical point of view, we cannot use a
only production rule to indicate its syntax so you have to resort to rules recursive left and declare a few new nonterminals. The part of the grammar that recognizes it is:
​ sent​ : sent_case;
​ sent_case​ : start_case FIN CASE
| start_case OTHERWISE sent FIN CASE
;
​ home_case​ : CASE expr OF
| case_start CASE expr ':' sent
;
As can be seen, two possibilities have been included for the non-terminal
sent_case , one with the OTHERWISE clause and one without it, since that clause is optional. Both rules could have been replaced by a single one in which a no new terminal that made reference to the OTHERWISE optionality , such as for example:
sent_case​ : start_case optional_case FIN CASE; optional_case: OTHERWISE sent
| / * Epsilon * /
;
But we will leave it as it has been proposed to show, together with the decision made for IF, how the same thing can be done in different ways and how it affects each decision when including semantic actions to generate code intermediate.
Furthermore, the proposed grammar allows constructions like “CASE to OF
FIN CASE ”, in which not a single CASE clause has been included . From our point of view we will consider it valid, but if we wanted to avoid it we would have to replace the first one in home_case and put it like:
case_home: CASE expr OF CASE expr ':' sent
The figure 8 .23 shows part of the syntax tree that recognizes a sentence CASE , in which you can see the recursion on the non-terminal start_case .
Once these details have been seen, we will now move on to code generation
intermediate itself. The first thing to realize is that the main rule that
272
285
Figure 8.23. Syntactic tree that recognizes a CASE statement
is going to be in charge of generating code is the one that appears the most in the syntactic tree, that is, the recursive rule of case_start :
​ home_case​ : home_case CASE expr ':' sent
in such a way that the semantic actions that we associate with this rule will be executed
repeatedly during reduction of complete CASE statement . Having this in mind let's give a preliminary idea of the intermediate code structure that we want to generate, considering that the structure that controls each CASE block must always be the same (structure, but not the variables and labels that involved in each CASE ). So that, associated with the example in figure 8. 23 se they would have blocks like the ones in the figure 8 . 24 . In this last figure they have been marked in different color code blocks depending on which production rule it is It has generated. In blue are the blocks generated by the second rule of start_case , in green the one generated by the first rule of start_case , and in brown the one generated by the sent_case rule that includes the OTHERWISE clause . Also, apart from each expr block the name of the variable (temporary or no) in which it is assumed that the total result of evaluating said expression at runtime. Finally, the striped blocks represent code
286
Code generation
Figure 8.24. Code blocks for a
CASE statement
273

intermediate that has already been generated by other production rules; therefore, everything that is not lined, we must generate it in semantic actions associated with the rules of CASE production .
As can be seen in this figure, it has been achieved that all the blocks in blue are roughly the same shape:
the calculation of a local expression that is stored in tmp              i
a comparison of tmp. with tmp . If they are not the same, we jump to the end of theexpr              i block
the intermediate code associated with a sent statement              i
a jump to the end of the CASE statement
It seems clear that the greatest complexity in the generation of this code lies
in the CASE rule (second rule of start_case ), since it is the one that should generate
all code marked in blue in the figure 8 . 24 . Let us carefully study one of the blue blocks. The triplets that we must generate in each block of blue code
are divided, in turn, into two parts: one interspersed between expr and sent , and another at the end of thei              i block. The next step is to examine this code to deduce which attributes we will need in each non-terminal. Focusing on the variables and labels that appear, we can classify them into two sections:
The tmp variable and the ietq tag are local to each block, that is, they are noti they use more than in a single block.
The variable tmp. and the expretq tag              FIN_CASE              are used repeatedly in all the blocks. It is also used in the sent_case rule in order to
view the “ label etq​ FIN_CASE​ " latest. tmp.              expr represents the expression that it will be compared and etq              FIN_CASE              represents the end of the CASE , label a the one that will be skipped after entering the code associated with the sentence of each CASE .
​ On the one hand, the variable tmp is generated by the expression local to the i              CASE and
stored in the nonterminal attribute expr , so it is accessible in any semantic action of the production rule that is placed behind said nonterminal. Regarding the variable etq, it can be seen that it is used in a i              goto in a triplet between the generation of the expr and sent code , while the i              label appearsi after sent . This means that we will need this label in two actionsi
semantics of the same rule, so we will choose to associate it as an attribute of the token CASE , similar to what has been done for IF , WHILE statements and REPEAT .
​ On the other hand, and a little more complicated, the variable tmp.              expr is generated by
expr that appears in the CASE rule , but must be used in each and every
​ CASE rules in order to compare its value with successive tmp . Thus              i
we have to find some mechanism that allows us to propagate the name of this variable through all CASE rule reductions necessary to recognize the
​ full sentence. With the label etq              FIN_CASE              something similar happens, since said
label is needed in each CASE block in order to jump to the end of the CASE
274
287
once the execution of the sentence associated with each CASE has been completed . Likewise, etq FIN_CASE              must be accessible by the rule sent_case to place the label end (in brown according to the figure 8 .24 ).
​ To fix the accessibility of tmp.              expr              and etqFIN_CASE              for the actions
semantics that need them, it is best to associate them as attributes of the non-terminal home_case . This decision is crucial, and represents the central mechanism by which we will give solution to code generation for CASE statement . In this way, and such and as can be seen in the figure 8 . 25, both variables must be propagated from a no terminal start_case to another, making use of the recursive rule:
​ home_case​ : home_case CASE expr ':' sent
Thus, the semantic actions of this rule must generate the intermediate code
based on the attributes of the case_start of the consequent and finally propagate these attributes at the start_case of the antecedent so that they can be used in the following CASE (if any). As can be seen, the person in charge of generating the etq FIN_CASE              is the first rule of start_case , even though this rule will not need such an attribute: the goal is that all other applications of the rule recursive have the same tag to jump to.
Thus, the rules of production and their semantic actions remain,
starting with those that are first reduced:
start_ case: CASE expr OF {
strcpy ($$. var, $ 2); newEtq ($$. etq);
}
| case_start CASE expr ':' {
newEtq ($ 2.etq); printf ("if% s! =% s goto% s",
$ 1.var, $ 3, $ 2.etq);
}

To begin with, the generation of the most frequent triplets (expressions
arithmetic and simple conditions) has been delegated to the functions generateTerceto and GenerateCondition from lines 178 to 193, which receive the appropriate parameters and they make the rules for lines 119 to 134 much more compact and clear.
The% union on line 13 next to the type declarations of the lines
precedents establishes the attribute framework for terminals and non-terminals of the grammar. It should be noted that the% union has several fields with the same type
(char [21]), but different name: number, variable_aux, label_aux and next_label, like two records with the same internal structure (two
21 character strings): second_block and case_block. This is because, although structurally coincide, semantically they have different objectives, so it has been opted to replicate them with distinctive names. This has no repercussion from the point of view of efficiency, since the space occupied by the% union is the same.
Finally, as an attribute of the token NUMBER , a field of
type text instead of an integer numeric value, since our purpose is to generate
triplets and do not operate arithmetically with the values  themselves. Also, the semantic action on line 119 forces all numeric literals to be managed through a variable temporary thanks to a triplet of direct assignment.
8.4.6 Solution with JFlex / Cup
The solution with JFlex and Cup is very similar to the one given above for Lex
and Yacc. The only relevant difference from JFlex is that it must be this who is in charge of assigning the necessary labels to the terminals IF , WHILE , REPEAT and CASE because attributes cannot be modified in actions Cup intermediate. To make this more evident, the JFlex code has a function static that allows generating labels (with the prefix etqL to distinguish them from generated by Cup that will have the prefix etqY ), since JFlex actions do not can access the definitions of functions declared in Cup. So the code remains:
