Chapter 9
Run-time memory management
​ 9.1​ Memory organization during execution
As already commented in the first sections, for a program to be
run on an operating system, it is necessary the existence of a loader that supplies the program with a contiguous block of memory on which to execute. Therefore, the program code resulting from the compilation must be organized in form that makes use of this block, so the compiler must incorporate the object program all the code necessary for it.
Memory management techniques during program execution
they differ from one language to another, and even from one compiler to another. In this chapter studies memory management used in imperative languages like Fortran, Pascal, C, Modula-2, etc. Memory management in another type of languages  (functional, logical, etc.) is, in general, different from the organization that here it arises.
For imperative languages, compilers generate programs that will have
at runtime an organization of memory similar (roughly) to the that appears in the figure 9 . 1 .
In this diagram the sections of:
the code
the Fixed Size Data Zone
the Stack or stack
Heap or heap
Figure 9.1. Structure of the memory while executing a compiled program
293
306
Run-time memory management
​ 9.2​ Code zone
It is the area where the instructions of the executable program are stored in
machine code, and also the code corresponding to procedures and functions you use. Its size and content are set at compile time and by it is said that its size is fixed at runtime.
In this way, the compiler, as it generates code, will
placing sequentially in this area, conveniently delimiting the start of each function, procedure and main program (if any, as it happens in languages such as Pascal or Modula-2).
The final executable program will consist of this area together with
information regarding memory requirements for runtime data, both the fixed size block and an estimate of the dynamic block size of data. This information will be used by the loader ( linker ) at the time of load the executable into main memory to proceed with its execution.
9.2.1 Overlays
Some compilers fragment the object program code using
overlays or “flaps”, when the main memory available is less than the size of the Full program. These overlays are sections of object code that are stored in independent files and that are loaded in the central memory dynamically, it is that is, during program execution. They are called "flaps" because two overlays they can occupy the same chunk of memory at different moments of time, overlapping. To make memory usage more efficient, the overlays of a program are grouped into zones and modules, each of which contains a set full functions or procedures. The figure9. 2 shows how several are located
Figure 9.2. Examples of code zone configurations using overlays
294
307
overlays in different zones of static memory.
During runtime only one of the overlays of each of the Overlay zones can be stored in main memory. The compiler reserves in the code section a contiguous memory area for each set of overlays .
The size of this area must be equal to that of the largest module loaded on it.
It is the function of the programmer to determine how many overlay zones are defined, what
functions and procedures are encapsulated in each overlay module , and how they are organize these modules to occupy each of the overlay zones . A restriction to keep in mind is that the functions of a module should not refer to functions of another module of the same overlay , since they will never be simultaneously In Memory.
Obviously, the execution time of a structured program with
overlays is higher than if it had no overlays and all the code was resident in memory, since during program execution it is necessary to load each module when a call is made to any of the functions it includes. Too
it is the programmer's task to design the overlays structure in such a way that it is minimized the number of these operations. The overlays technique is not only used when the program to compile is very large in relation to the memory availability of the system, but also when you want to obtain smaller programs that must coexist with other operating systems when memory is low.
​ 9.3​ Data zone
The data handled by a program is currently divided into three large
blocks:
One dedicated to storing global variables accessible by anyline of program code. This block is the Size Data Zone Permanent.
Another dedicated to storing the local variables to each function andprocess. These cannot be stored in the previous block for two main reasons: a) it is not necessary to store the variables of a function until the moment it is invoked and, once its
execution, either; and b) during the execution of a recursive function must store multiple instances of your local variables, specifically as many as invocations to that same function have occurred. This block is the battery.
A last block dedicated to storing managed memory blocksdirectly by the user through malloc / free , new / dispose , etc. The amount of memory required for these tasks varies from running the program, and the data located is not local to no function, but persist until released. This block is the Heap.
295
308
Run-time memory management
9.3.1 Fixed Size Data Zone
The easiest way to store the contents of a variable in memory in
runtime is in static or permanent memory throughout the entire program execution. Not all objects (variables) can be stored statically. For an object to be stored in static memory, its size (number of bytes required for storage) must be known in compile time. As a consequence of this condition, they cannot be stored in static memory:
Local variables corresponding to procedures or functionsrecursive, since at compile time the number of times is not known that these variables will be needed.
Dynamic data structures such as lists, trees, etc. since the number of elements that form it is not known until the program is run.
Therefore, the variables local to a function are data of defined size,
but for those who do not know the number of occurrences of the scope on which they depend, for what should be stored following some dynamic criteria. Nor is it possible store in this area the eminently dynamic structures, that is, those that are managed through pointers through dynamic free space creation: dynamic lists, dynamic graphs, etc. This data is of indefinite size so they cannot be saved in the fixed size data area. Although it may be thought that the code size of a recursive function also depends on the number of times the function calls itself, this is not true: the instructions to execute are always the same, and what changes is the data with which you work, that is, the local variables.
Static memory allocation techniques are straightforward, just as
illustrates figure 9 .3 . Starting from a position indicated by a reference pointer
Figure 9.3. Allocation of a memory block for a global variable X
296
309
(limit of the fixed data area) the global variable X is stored, and the pointer is advanced as many bytes as are necessary to store said variable. In other words, to each variable is assigned an address and a chunk of memory from it according to with the type of the variable; these pieces are sequentially housed from the start of fixed size data area. For example, if we find the statement by Modula-2:
a, b: INTEGER; c: ARRAY [1..20] OF REAL; c: CHAR; d: REAL;
and assuming that an INTEGER occupies 2 bytes, a REAL occupies 5 and a CHAR occupies
1, and assuming the beginning of the data area is at position 0000h (This address is relative to the DS data segment that can actually point to any memory address and that will be conveniently initialized by the charger), then we have the following address and size assignment:
Variable
Size
Dir. Start
to
2 bytes
0000h
This memory allocation is done at compile time and the objects
are valid and accessible from the beginning of the program execution until that ends. The direction associated with each global variable is constant and relative to the data segment, that is, always the same from the memory starting point in that the program has been loaded (point that can vary from one execution to another).
In languages  that allow subprograms, and provided that
all the objects of these applets can be stored statically (for
example in Fortran-IV that does not allow recursion) is stored in static memory a activation record corresponding to each of the subprograms, with a structure as in figure 9 . 4 .a). These activation records will contain the
local variables, formal parameters, and value returned by the function, and are distributed linearly in the fixed size data area as shown in Figure 9 .4 .b).
Within each activation record the local variables are organized into
sequence. There is only one activation record for each procedure and therefore no recursive calls are allowed. The process that is followed when a procedure P calls another Q is as follows:

P (the caller) evaluates the actual invocation parameters, in case try complex expressions, using a memory zone temporary for buffering. For example, if the call to Q is "Q ((3 * 5) + (2 * 2), 7)" the operations prior to the call itself in machine code they have to be carried out on some area of  temporary memory.

Run-time memory management
(At some point there must be a memory area that contains the value intermediate 15, and intermediate value 4 to add them below). In case static memory utilization, this temporary zone can be common to the entire program, since its size can be deducted at compilation.
Q initializes its variables and begins its execution.
Since the variables are permanently in memory it is easy
implement the property of whether or not they keep their content for each new call (similar behavior to the static modifier of the C language).
Figure 9.4. Structure of an activation record associated with a non-recursive function. Each function or procedure has associated an activation record of different size, in depending on how many local variables it has, the type of the return value, etc.
9.3.2 Stack ( Stack )
A language with a block structure is one that is composed of
modules or pieces of code whose execution is sequential; these modules in turn can contain in their sequence of instructions calls to other modules, which in turn they can sometimes call other submodules and so on.
The appearance of this type of language brought with it the need for techniques
more flexible memory hosting, which could adapt to the demands of memory during program execution due to recursive invocations. In these languages, each time a procedure begins execution, a activation record to contain the objects necessary for its execution, eliminating it once it is finished.
During a recursive invocation, only the
last invoked procedure, while the invoker sequence is found
"Asleep." This means that, as the invoked functions finish, they must the calling procedures are reactivated in reverse order of the sequence of calls.
298
311
For this reason, the different activation registers associated with each block must be placed in a stack that they will enter when the execution of the block begins and will exit at finish the same. The structure of the activation records varies from language to language to others, and even from some compilers to others, this being one of the problems which is sometimes difficult to link the codes generated by two compilers different In general, procedure activation records usually have some of the fields that can be seen in figure 9. 5 .
Figure 9.5. Complete structure of a record activation in languages  that support recursion
The content is stored in the area corresponding to the status of the machine
that was in the microprocessor registers before starting the
process. These values  must be replaced at the end of their execution. The code in charge of making the copy of the state of the machine is common for all procedures.
The pointer to the parent's activation record allows access to variables
declared in other procedures within which the person is involved This activation record belongs to. For example, suppose a block of program like the following:
PROCEDURE A1
VAR
A1_a, A1_b: INTEGER;
PROCEDURE B1
VAR
B1_c, B1_d: INTEGER;
BEGIN
...
END B1;
PROCEDURE B2
VAR
B2_e, B2_f: INTEGER;
299
312
Run-time memory management
PROCEDURE C2 VAR C2_g, C2_h: INTEGER; BEGIN
...
CALL B1;
...
END C2;
BEGIN
...
CALL C2;
...
END B2; BEGIN (* Main program *)
...
CALL B2;
...
END;
The pointer to the parent's activation record is used because a
procedure can make use of the local variables of the procedures in the that is declared. In this example, C2 can make use of variables:
C2_g and C2_h (which are yours).
B2_e and B2_f (which are from their parent, the one in which C2 is declared).
A1_a and A1_b (which are from their father's father).
but it cannot make use of either B1_c or B1_d, since C2 is not embedded in B1.
As in the fixed-size data zone, the trigger logs
contain space to store the actual parameters (values  associated with the variables that appear in the header) and local variables, (those defined within of the block or procedure) as well as a zone to store the value returned by the function and a zone of temporary values  for the intermediate calculation of expressions.
Any two different blocks or procedures usually have records
activation buttons of different sizes. This size is generally known in compile time as there is sufficient information about the space occupied by the objects that compose it. In certain cases this is not so as per example occurs in C when using arrays of indeterminate length. In these
In these cases, the activation record must include an overflow zone at the end whose size is not fixed at compile time but only when it actually reaches run the procedure. This complicates memory management a bit, so that some low-cost compilers remove this facility.
Although it is essential to save successive versions of the data
As recursive calls are made, the instructions that are applied to they are always the same, so these instructions only need save them once. However, it is clear that the same code will work with different data on each recursive invocation, so the compiler should generate
300
313
code prepared for such eventuality, not making absolute reference to variables (except those defined globally whose memory location does not change in the course of program execution), but relative reference depending on the start of your activation record.
The stack management procedure when one P procedure calls another
procedure Q , is developed in two phases; the first of them corresponds to the code which is included in procedure P before transferring control to Q , and the second, to the code that must be included at the beginning of Q to be executed when it receives the control. An example of this invocation can be given by a code like:
PROCEDURE P ( / * formal param. Of P * / ) {
VAR
// var. local P
PROCEDURE Q ( / * formal param. Of Q * / ) {
VAR
// var. Q premises
BEGIN
...
Q (...); // Recursive invocation from Q to Q
...
END Q;
BEGIN
...
x = Q ( / * real params to Q * / ); // Invocation from P to Q
END P;
The first of these phases follows the following steps:
1.1 The procedure that makes the call ( P ) evaluates the expressions of the invocation, using its temporary variable zone, and copies the result in the area corresponding to the actual parameters of the procedure that receives the call. Prior to that, leave room for Q Deposit the returned value.
1.2 The caller P places on the stack a pointer to the activation register of the procedure in which Q is declared , so that it can access non-local variables declared in its parent. Finally, P transfers control to Q , which places the return address on top from the stack.
1.3 The receiver of the call ( Q ) saves the state of the machine before start your performance using the corresponding area of  your activation record.
1.4 Q initializes its variables and begins its execution.
At the end Q execution registration activation dislodges proceeding
also in two phases. The first is implemented by instructions at the end of the
procedure that has just finished its execution ( Q ), and the second in the procedure that made the call ( P ), after regaining control:
2.1 The outgoing procedure ( Q ) before ending its execution places the value
301
314
Run-time memory management
back to the beginning of your activation record.
2.2 Using the information contained in its registry, Q restores the status of the machine and positions the end-of-stack pointer at the position it was originally.
2.3 The calling procedure P copies the value returned by the procedure invoked Q within its own activation record (that of Q ).

Figure 9.6. Stack of logs activation when a P function summon another Q and it calls itself same one time. Note that the father of Q is P , since Q is locally declared to P . P does not have father since it is supposed globally declared
Within a procedure or function, local variables are referenced
always as addresses relative to the beginning of your activation record, or to the beginning of local variable zone. Therefore, when it is necessary to access from a procedure to variables defined from other procedures whose scope is accessible, it will be necessary to dynamically provide the starting address of the variables of that procedure. To do this, the activation record is used pointer to the parent's activation record. This pointer will point to the beginning of the local variables of the immediately superior procedure (in which the call is found declared). The link pointer occupies a fixed position relative to the

315
start of your local variables. When procedures call themselves recursively, the scope of variables generally prevents an activation modify the local variables of another activation of the same procedure, so
In these cases, the immediate superior procedure will be, for liaison purposes, the one originated the first activation, as can be seen in the figure 9. 6 . It is important note that the concept of "parent procedure" is independent of that of "Calling procedure", although it may match this one.
9.3.3 Stack ( heap )
When the size of an object to be placed in memory can vary in time
execution, its location on the stack is not possible, much less in the data zone fixed size. Examples of this type of objects are lists and dynamic trees, variable-length character strings, etc. To handle this type of object the compiler must have a memory area of  variable size and not be seen affected by the activation or deactivation of procedures. This piece of memory it is called heap (from English heap ). In those high-level languages  that require the use of the heap , the compiler must incorporate in the generated object program, all the code corresponding to its management; this code is always the same for all built programs. The basic operations performed on the heap They are:
Host: a contiguous block of memory is requested to be able to store aitem of a certain size.
Evict: indicates that it is no longer necessary to preserve memory previouslyhoused for an object and that, therefore, it must be free to be reused if necessary by other hosting operations.
Depending on the programmer or the system itself that invokes them, these
operations can be explicit or implicit respectively. In case of accommodation explicitly, the programmer must include in the source code, one by one, the instructions that require a certain amount of memory for the location of each data or register (for example, Pascal provides the new statement , C provides malloc , etc.). The amount of memory required in each atomic operation of hosting, can be calculated by the compiler based on the corresponding type to the object to be hosted, or can be specified by the developer directly. The result of the host function is usually a pointer to a chunk
contiguous memory within the heap that can be used to store the value of the object. Imperative programming languages  often use hosting and Explicit evictions. On the contrary, declarative languages  (logical and functional) do most of these operations implicitly due to mechanisms underlying its operation.
Heap management requires adequate techniques that optimize the space that
takes care (in order to prevent memory fragmentation) or access time, both factors being opposed as usually happens almost always in
303
316
Run-time memory management
computing. In this regard, the techniques are usually divided into two large groups: those in which exactly the amount of memory requested is housed (they favor fragmentation), and those in which the block hosted is of a similar size, but generally higher than requested, allowing the heap to only store blocks of certain sizes preset at compiler build time (favors defragmentation).
Finally, one of the most interesting features of languages
very current imperatives, such as Java, is that they include an automatic garbage ( garbage collection ). The garbage collector is part of the heap manager , and has control over the pointers and memory chunks of the heap that are inaccessible through program variables, either directly or indirectly, this is, of the memory chunks hosted but not being pointed by anyone and which, therefore, constitute garbage. By means of this control they can effect automatic evacuation operations, freeing the programmer from performing explicitly this action. Among the techniques used to implement the garbage collectors we can quote reference counters, mark and sweep, generational techniques, etc.
